\iota(\tilde{\theta}(B))\in H^{2}_{an}(Y,{\mathcal{O}}^{*})
of
B
vanishes.
This is the case, if and only if
\tilde{\theta}([B])
belongs to the image of the connecting homomorphism

\delta:H^{1}_{an}(Y,{\mathcal{O}}^{*})\rightarrow H^{2}_{an}(Y,\mu_{r})
of the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mu_{r}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota}}{{\rightarrow}}{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\bullet)^{r}}}{{\rightarrow}}{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}^{*}\rightarrow 0.

This is the case, if and only if there exists a line bundle
L\in{\rm Pic}(Y)
, such that the class
c_{1}(L)-g(c_{1}(E))

belong to
rH^{2}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})
, as seen from the commutative diagram

\textstyle{[L]}
\textstyle{\theta([B])}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}(Y,{\mathcal{O}}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\bullet)^{r}}
\scriptstyle{c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H^{1}(Y,{\mathcal{O}}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\scriptstyle{c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H^{1}(Y,\mu_{r})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H^{2}(Y,{\mathcal{O}}^{*})}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cdot r}
\textstyle{H^{2}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}}/r{\mathbb{Z}})}
\textstyle{c_{1}(L)}
\textstyle{c_{1}(L)+rH^{2}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})}

and the fact that the right vertical isomorphism above maps the right hand side of (
3.6
)
to
g(c_{1}(E))+rH^{2}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})
.

The above argument goes through even if
E
is reflexive, but not locally free, since then
E
is locally free away from a closed analytic subset
Z
of co-dimension
\geq 3
. In that case Poincare and
Lefschetz dualities yield the commutative diagram whose vertical arrows are isomorphisms.

\textstyle{H_{4n-2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{P.D.}
\textstyle{H_{4n-2}(X,Z,{\mathbb{Z}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{L.D.}
\textstyle{H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}(X\setminus Z,{\mathbb{Z}}),}

where
2n
is the complex dimension of
X
. The top horizontal arrow is an isomorphism as well, since
H_{4n-k}(Z,{\mathbb{Z}})
vanishes for
k<6
. Hence, the bottom vertical arrow is an isomorphism as well. Consequently, the restriction homomorphism
H^{2}(X,\mu_{r})\rightarrow H^{2}(X\setminus Z,\mu_{r})
is an isomorphism as well and the class
\tilde{\sigma}({\mathbb{P}}(E_{\mid_{X\setminus Z}}))\in H^{2}(X\setminus Z,\mu_{r})
is the restriction of the class

\exp\left(\frac{2\pi i}{r}c_{1}(E)\right)

in
H^{2}(X,\mu_{r})
. Similarly,

\tilde{\sigma}({\mathbb{P}}(F^{\prime}_{\mid_{Y\setminus Z^{\prime}}}))
is the restriction of the parallel transport

\exp\left(\frac{2\pi i}{r}g(c_{1}(E))\right)

in
H^{2}(Y,\mu_{r})
.
where
Z^{\prime}
is the singular locus of the deformed twisted reflexive sheaf
F^{\prime}
along the twistor path
\gamma
, by

[
Ma4
, Construction 6.9]
.
∎

Definition 3.14
.

Let
\Lambda
be a lattice. A non-zero class
\lambda\in\Lambda
is
primitive
, if the rank
1
lattice
{\rm span}_{\mathbb{Z}}\{\lambda\}
is saturated in
\Lambda
.
The
divisibility

{\rm div}(\lambda)
of a primitive class
\lambda
of
\Lambda
is the greatest common divisor of
\{(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\ :\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda\}
.

Let
L
be a lattice. Denote by
\widetilde{O}(L)
the subgroup of isometries acting trivially on the discriminant group
L^{*}/L
. Let
O_{+}(L)
be the subgroup of isometries preserving the orientation of the positive cone
[
Ma3
, Sec. 4]
.
Let
SO(L)
be the subgroup of isometries with determinant
1
. Denote by

S\widetilde{O}_{+}(L)
the intersection of these three subgroups.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
X
be an irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifold of
K3^{[n]}
-type or generalized Kummer deformation type of dimension
2n
,
n\geq 2
, and let
\alpha
be a primitive class in
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
with
{\rm div}(\alpha)=1
or
2
.
Then the
{\rm Mon}^{2}(X)
-orbit of
\alpha
is determined by
(\alpha,\alpha)
and
{\rm div}(\alpha)
.

Proof.

The assumption on
{\rm div}(\alpha)
determines the image of
(\alpha,\bullet)/{\rm div}(\alpha)
in the discriminant group

H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})^{*}/H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
, since the latter is cyclic of even order, so contains a unique element of order
2
.
Eichler’s criterion then implies that the
\widetilde{O}(H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}}))
-orbit of
\alpha
is determined by
{\rm div}(\alpha)
and

(\alpha,\alpha)

[
GHS
, Lemma 3.5]
.
It remains to prove that the
{\rm Mon}^{2}(X)
-orbit of
\alpha
is equal to the

\widetilde{O}(H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
-orbit of
\alpha
.
The discriminant group
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})^{*}/H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
is cyclic of even order and
The
{\rm Mon}^{2}(X)
-orbit of
\alpha
is contained in the

\widetilde{O}(H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
-orbit of
\alpha
, since both
(\alpha,\alpha)
and
{\rm div}(\alpha)
are
{\rm Mon}^{2}(X)
-invariant.
Now
{\rm Mon}^{2}(X)
contains
S\widetilde{O}_{+}(H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})),

by
[
Ma8
, Cor. 1.8]
and
[
Ma9
, Theorem 1.4]
.
Hence, it suffices to prove that the
\widetilde{O}(H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}}))
-orbit
and
S\widetilde{O}_{+}(H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}}))
-orbit
of
\alpha
are equal. The orthogonal complement
\alpha^{\perp}
of every class
\alpha
contains a copy of a unimodular rank
2
even lattice
U
of signature
(1,1)
and
O(U)
and
S\widetilde{O}_{+}(H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}}))
generate

\widetilde{O}(H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
, where we embed
O(U)
as a subgroup of
\widetilde{O}(H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
stabilizing
\alpha
by extending each isometry via the identity on
U^{\perp}
. Hence, indeed, the
\widetilde{O}(H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}}))
-orbit
and
S\widetilde{O}_{+}(H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}}))
-orbit
of
\alpha
are equal.
∎

We provide next a sufficient condition for the existence of a parallel transport operator as in Lemma

3.13
in a special case.
Let
X
and
Y
be deformation equivalent
2n
-dimensional,
n\geq 2
, irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifolds of
K3^{[n]}
-type or of generalized Kummer type. Let
E
be the sheaf in
Lemma
3.13
and
r
its rank.
Write
c_{1}(E)=a\alpha
, such that
\alpha
is a primitive class in
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
.
Set
\rho:=r/\gcd(a,r)
.

Lemma 3.16
.

Assume that
\gcd(\rho,{\rm div}(\alpha))
is
1
or
2
.
There exists a reflexive sheaf
F
on
Y
, such that
(Y,{\mathcal{E}}nd(F))
is deformation equivalent to
(X,{\mathcal{E}}nd(E))

if and only if
there exist a primitive class
\beta\in H^{1,1}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})
and an integer
k
satisfying
\gcd(k,\rho)=1
,

(3.7)

\displaystyle\gcd(\rho,{\rm div}(\beta))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gcd(\rho,{\rm div}(\alpha)),

\displaystyle k^{2}\frac{(\beta,\beta)}{2}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\frac{(\alpha,\alpha)}{2}\ \ \left\{\begin{array}[]{ccl}\left({\rm mod}\ \rho\right)&\mbox{if}&\gcd(\rho,{\rm div}(\alpha))=1,\\
\left({\rm mod}\ 2\rho\right)&\mbox{if}&\gcd(\rho,{\rm div}(\alpha))=2.\end{array}\right.

If such
\beta
and
k
exist, then
F
may be chosen with
c_{1}(F)=ka\beta
.

Note that
{\rm div}(\alpha)
and
{\rm div}(\beta)
divide
2n-2
in the
K3^{[n]}
-type case and they divide
2n+2
in the generalized Kummer case. In the
K3^{[2]}
-type case the condition that
\gcd(\rho,{\rm div}(\alpha))
is
1
or
2
is automatically satisfied.

Proof.

Such
F
exists, if and only if
there exists a parallel-transport operator
g:H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})\rightarrow H^{2}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})
, such that

g(a\alpha)+rH^{2}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})=b\beta+rH^{2}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})
, for some integer
b
and a primitive class
\beta\in H^{1,1}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})
,
by Lemma

3.13
.
The order of the left hand side coset is
r/\gcd(a,r)
and the order of the right hand side coset is
r/\gcd(b,r)
. Hence,
two integers
a
and
b
satisfying the latter equality must satisfy
\gcd(a,r)=\gcd(b,r)
and so
\frac{a}{\gcd(a,r)}

and
\frac{b}{\gcd(a,r)}
are both invertible modulo
\rho
. Furthermore, the equation of cosets above is equivalent to

\frac{a}{\gcd(a,r)}g(\alpha)+\rho H^{2}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})=\frac{b}{\gcd(a,r)}\beta+\rho H^{2}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})

The latter equality is equivalent to

(3.11)

g(\alpha)+\rho H^{2}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})=k\beta+\rho H^{2}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}}),

for some integer
k
satisfying
\gcd(k,\rho)=1
, by the invertibility of
\frac{a}{\gcd(a,r)}
modulo
\rho
.
Equations (
3.7
) and (
3.16
) follow from Equation (
3.11
). The latter is clear if modulo
\rho
and if
\gcd(\rho,{\rm div}(\alpha))=2

and
g(\alpha)=k\beta+\rho\lambda
, then

(\alpha,\alpha)=(g(\alpha),g(\alpha))=(k\beta+\rho\lambda,k\beta+\rho\lambda)=k^{2}(\beta,\beta)+2k\rho(\beta,\lambda)+\rho^{2}(\lambda,\lambda),

and so the congruence (
3.11
) holds modulo
2\rho
, since both
\rho
and
(\beta,\lambda)
are even.
Hence, if a sheaf
F
satisfying the condition of the Lemma exists, then an integer
k
and a class
\beta\in H^{1,1}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})
satisfying the conditions of the Lemma exist as well.

Conversely, assume that an integer
k
and a class
\beta\in H^{1,1}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})
satisfying the conditions of the Lemma exist.
We prove the existence of a sheaf
F
by
proving that there exists a parallel-transport operator
g:H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})\rightarrow H^{2}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})

satisfying Equation (
3.11
).

Step 1: We prove first that there exists a class
\alpha_{1}\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}}),
such that
{\rm div}(\alpha+\rho\alpha_{1})=\gcd(\rho,{\rm div}(\alpha))
.
There exists a class
\lambda\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
, such that
\gcd((\alpha,\lambda),\rho)=\gcd(\rho,{\rm div}(\alpha))
.
Choose
x,y\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, such that
x(\alpha,\lambda)+y\rho=\gcd(\rho,{\rm div}(\alpha))
.
There exists a class
\delta\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
, such that
\delta^{\perp}
is unimodular isomorphic to the orthogonal direct sum
U^{\oplus 3}
(generalized Kummer case) or

U^{\oplus 3}\oplus E_{8}(-1)^{\oplus 2}
(
K3^{[n]}
-type case), where
U
is the even unimodular rank
2
lattice of signature
(1,1)
and
E_{8}(-1)
is the negative definite
E_{8}
lattice. Write
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}+\lambda^{\prime\prime}
with
\lambda^{\prime}\in\delta^{\perp}
and
\lambda^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\delta
. Decompose
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}+\alpha^{\prime\prime}
similarly.
We may and do choose
\lambda
so that the saturation of the lattice spanned by
\lambda^{\prime}
and
\alpha^{\prime}
is isometric to
U
.
Indeed, choose a sublattice
U_{0}
of
\delta^{\perp}
isometric to
U
containing
\alpha^{\prime}
. Then there exists an element
u
of

U_{0}
, of the same divisibility as
\lambda^{\prime}
, such that
(u,\alpha^{\prime})=(\lambda^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
and so we can replace
\lambda
by
u+\lambda^{\prime\prime}
.
Now
\{\alpha^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},\delta\}^{\perp}
contains a copy of
U
and so it contains classes
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
with
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=y
. Then
{\rm div}(\alpha+\rho\alpha_{1})=\gcd(\rho,{\rm div}(\alpha))
, since

(\alpha+\rho\alpha_{1},x\lambda+\alpha_{2})=x(\alpha,\lambda)+\rho(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=\gcd(\rho,{\rm div}(\alpha)).

Step 2: There exists a class
\beta_{1}\in H^{2}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})
, such that
{\rm div}(\beta+\rho\beta_{1})=\gcd(\rho,{\rm div}(\beta))
, by Step 1.
Set
\tilde{\alpha}=\alpha+\rho\alpha_{1}
and
\tilde{\beta}:=\beta+\rho\beta_{1}
. The
{\rm Mon}^{2}(X)
-orbit of
\tilde{\alpha}
consists of primitive classes
\lambda
with
(\lambda,\lambda)=(\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\alpha})
and
{\rm div}(\lambda)={\rm div}(\alpha)
,
by
Lemma
3.15
.
Similarly, the

{\rm Mon}^{2}(Y)
-orbit of
\tilde{\beta}
consists of primitive classes
\lambda
with
(\lambda,\lambda)=(\tilde{\beta},\tilde{\beta})
and
{\rm div}(\lambda)={\rm div}(\beta)
.
Let
f:H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})\rightarrow H^{2}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})
be a parallel-transport operator.
There exists a class
\delta\in H^{2}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})
, such that
\delta^{\perp}
is unimodular.
Set
\Lambda_{2}:=\delta^{\perp}
.
Fix
k
solving the congruence (
3.16
).
Then there exist integers
x
,
y
satisfying

\frac{(\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\alpha})-k^{2}(\tilde{\beta},\tilde{\beta})}{2\rho}=xk+y\rho,

since
\gcd(k,\rho)=1
. We claim that
x
can be chosen to be even, if
{\rm div}(\alpha)=2
. Indeed, if

\gcd(\rho,{\rm div}(\alpha))=2
, then
\rho
is even,
k
is odd, and
the left hand side of the above displayed equation is even, by assumption (
3.16
), and so
x
must be even. If
{\rm div}(\alpha)=2
and
\rho
is odd, then a solution
(x,y)
can be replaced by
(x-\rho,y+k)
, and so
x
may be chosen even.

Case
{\rm div}(\tilde{\beta})=1
:

In this case
\delta^{\perp}
contains classes in the
{\rm Mon}^{2}(Y)
-orbit of
\tilde{\beta}
. So
\tilde{\beta}^{\perp}
contains classes in the
{\rm Mon}^{2}(Y)
-orbit of
\delta
and we may choose
\delta
so that

(\tilde{\beta},\delta)=0
.
Let
T
be the rank
2
lattice with basis
\{t_{1},t_{2}\}
satisfying

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(t_{1},t_{1})&(t_{1},t_{2})\\
(t_{2},t_{1})&(t_{2},t_{2})\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(\tilde{\beta},\tilde{\beta})&x\\
x&2y\end{array}\right)

There exists a primitive embedding
\tau:T\rightarrow\Lambda_{2}
such that
\tau(t_{1})=\tilde{\beta}
, by results of Nikulin (see
[
Ma8
, Lemma 8.1]
).
Then
\gamma:=k\tilde{\beta}+\rho\tau(t_{2})
is a primitive class of
\Lambda_{2}
satisfying
(\gamma,\gamma)=(\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\alpha})
.
Furthermore,
{\rm div}(\gamma)=1
, since
\Lambda_{2}
is unimodular.
Hence, there exists an element
h
of
{\rm Mon}^{2}(Y)
, such that
h(f(\tilde{\alpha})=\gamma
and
h(f(\delta))=f(\delta)
, since
Mon^{2}(Y)
contains
SO^{+}(\Lambda_{2})
, by
[
Ma8
, Theorem 1.2]
and
[
Ma9
, Theorem 1.4]
.
So
g:=h\circ f
is a parallel-transport operator satisfying Equation
(
3.11
).

Case
{\rm div}(\tilde{\beta})=2
:

The
{\rm Mon}^{2}(Y)
-orbit of
\tilde{\beta}
contains a class of the form

2\tilde{\beta}^{\prime}+\delta
, where
\tilde{\beta}^{\prime}
is a primitive class in
\Lambda_{2}
,
by Lemma
3.15
.
Hence, we may assume that
\tilde{\beta}=2\tilde{\beta}^{\prime}+\delta
is of this form, possibly after modifying the choice of
\delta
.
Recall that
x
can be chosen even in the current case.
Let
T
be the rank
2
lattice with basis
\{t_{1},t_{2}\}
satisfying

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(t_{1},t_{1})&(t_{1},t_{2})\\
(t_{2},t_{1})&(t_{2},t_{2})\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(\tilde{\beta}^{\prime},\tilde{\beta}^{\prime})&x/2\\
x/2&2y\end{array}\right).

There exists a primitive embedding
\tau:T\rightarrow\Lambda_{2}
such that
\tau(t_{1})=\tilde{\beta}^{\prime}
, by results of Nikulin (see
[
Ma8
, Lemma 8.1]
.
Then
\gamma:=k\tilde{\beta}+\rho\tau(t_{2})=(2k\tilde{\beta}^{\prime}+\rho\tau(t_{2}))+k\delta
is a primitive class satisfying
(\gamma,\gamma)=(\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\alpha})
.
We claim that
{\rm div}(\gamma)=2
. Indeed,
2
divides
{\rm div}(\gamma)
, since
\rho
is even, as
\gcd(\rho,{\rm div}(\beta))=2
.
In addition,
{\rm div}(\alpha)
divides
2
, since
k\tilde{\beta}^{\prime}+(\rho/2)\tau(t_{2})
is a primitive class of
\Lambda_{2}
and
\Lambda_{2}
is unimodular.
Hence, there exists an element
h
of
{\rm Mon}^{2}(Y)
, such that
h(f(\tilde{\alpha}))=\gamma
,
by Lemma
3.15
. Setting

g:=h\circ f
we get a parallel-transport operator satisfying Equation
(
3.11
).
∎

Given a
K3
surface
S
, let
S^{(n)}
be its
n
-th symmetric power and let

(3.12)

\theta:H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Z}})\rightarrow H^{2}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Z}})

be the composition of the isomorphism
H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Z}})\cong H^{2}(S^{(n)},{\mathbb{Z}})
with the pull-back homomorphism
H^{2}(S^{(n)},{\mathbb{Z}})\rightarrow H^{2}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Z}})
via the Hilbert-Chow morphism.

Example 3.17
.

Let
X=S^{[2]}
,
S
a
K3
surface, and let
E
be the vector bundle
G^{[2]}
over
S^{[2]}
associated to a stable and rigid vector bundle
G
of rank
r_{0}
on
S
as in Theorem
1.4
. Let
\delta\in H^{2}(S^{[2]},{\mathbb{Z}})
be half the class of the divisor of non-reduced subschemes.
Then
c_{1}(E)=r_{0}\theta(c_{1}(G))-\frac{r_{0}(r_{0}-1)}{2}\delta
, by
[
O2
, Prop. 5.8]
.
If
v(G)=(r_{0},c_{1}(G),s),
then
(v(G),v(G))=-2
, and so

\frac{(c_{1}(G),c_{1}(G))}{2}=sr_{0}-1.

Assume that
c_{1}(G)
is a primitive class.
If
r_{0}
is odd, then
c_{1}(E)=r_{0}\alpha
, where
\alpha=\theta(c_{1}(G))-\frac{r_{0}-1}{2}\delta
is a primitive class of divisibility
1
satisfying

(3.13)

\frac{(\alpha,\alpha)}{2}=\frac{(c_{1}(G),c_{1}(G))}{2}-\left(\frac{r_{0}-1}{2}\right)^{2}=sr_{0}-1-\left(\frac{r_{0}-1}{2}\right)^{2}.

If
r_{0}
is even, then
c_{1}(E)=(r_{0}/2)\alpha
, where
\alpha=2\theta(c_{1}(G))-(r_{0}-1)\delta
is a primitive class of divisibility
2
satisfying

\frac{(\alpha,\alpha)}{2}=2(c_{1}(G),c_{1}(G))-\left(r_{0}-1\right)^{2}=4sr_{0}-4-\left(r_{0}-1\right)^{2}.

If
r_{0}=2
and
c_{1}(G)
is primitive, then

c_{1}(E)=2\theta(c_{1}(G))-\delta
is a primitive class of divisibility
2
. So
\rho={\rm rank}(E)=4
. An untwisted vector bundle
F
exists on an irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifold of
K3^{[2]}
-type
Y
, such that
(S^{[2]},{\mathbb{P}}(E))
is deformation equivalent to
(Y,{\mathbb{P}}(F))
, if and only if there exists a primitive class
\beta\in H^{1,1}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})
of divisibility
2
, such that

(\beta,\beta)/2\equiv(\alpha,\alpha)/2
(mod
8
),
by Lemma
3.16
.
The latter congruence is equivalent to (
3.16
), since if
k
is odd, then
k^{2}\equiv 1
(mod
8
).

If
r_{0}=3
and
c_{1}(G)
is primitive, then
c_{1}(E)=3\alpha
, where
\alpha=\theta(c_{1}(G))-\delta
is a primitive class of divisibility
1
satisfying
(\alpha,\alpha)/2\equiv 1
(mod
3
), by Equation (
3.13
). So
{\rm rank}(E)=9
and
\rho=3
. An untwisted vector bundle
F
exists on an irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifold of
K3^{[2]}
-type
Y
, such that
(S^{[2]},{\mathbb{P}}(E))
is deformation equivalent to
(Y,{\mathbb{P}}(F))
, if and only if there exists a primitive class
\beta\in H^{1,1}(Y,{\mathbb{Z}})
(with no constraint on its divisibility), such that
(\beta,\beta)/2\equiv 1
(mod
3
),
by Lemma
3.16
. Note:
The congruence condition
(\beta,\beta)/2\equiv 1
(mod
3
) follows also from Remark
1.5
and Equation (
9.3
), which imply that
5+2(\beta,\beta)\equiv 0
(mod
3
).

4.
The BKR equivalence

Let
S
be a smooth projective surface.
We review in this section the equivalence between the equivariant derived category of coherent sheaves on the
n
-th cartesian product
S^{n}
and the derived category of coherent sheaves on the Hilbert scheme
S^{[n]}
. This equivalence relies on results of Bridgeland-King-Ried
[
BKR
]
and Haiman
[
Hai
]
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety on which a finite group
G
acts. A
G
-linearization
of a coherent sheaf
E
on
X

is given by isomorphisms
\lambda_{g}:E\rightarrow g^{*}E
, for
g\in G
, satisfying
\lambda_{1}=id_{E}
and

\lambda_{gh}=h^{*}(\lambda_{g})\circ\lambda_{h}
. A morphism of linearized sheaves
f:(E_{1},\lambda_{1})\rightarrow(E_{2},\lambda_{2})
is an
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-module homomorphism
f:E_{1}\rightarrow E_{2}
satisfying

f=\lambda_{2,g}^{-1}\circ g^{*}(f)\circ\lambda_{1,g}
, for all
g\in G
. We get the abelian category of
G
-linearized coherent sheaves, whose bounded derived category is denoted by
D^{b}_{G}(X)

[
BKR
,
P
]
.

Let
(X,G)
and
(X^{\prime},G^{\prime})
be smooth projective varieties with group actions.
An equivariant morphism from
(X,G)
to
(X^{\prime},G^{\prime})
consists of a
morphism
f:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
and a group homomorphism
\varphi:G\rightarrow G^{\prime}
satisfying

f\circ g=\varphi(g)\circ f
, for all
g\in G
. We get the derived pullback
L(f,\varphi)^{*}:D^{b}_{G^{\prime}}(X^{\prime})\rightarrow D^{b}_{G}(X)
.
When
\varphi
is surjective we get also the derived pushforward
R(f,\varphi)^{G}_{*}:D^{b}_{G}(X)\rightarrow D^{b}_{G^{\prime}}(X^{\prime})

[
P
, Sec. 1.3]
.
When
G=G^{\prime}
and
\varphi
is the identity, or when
G^{\prime}=1
, we will denote
L(f,\varphi)^{*}
by
Lf^{*}
and

R(f,\varphi)^{G}_{*}
by
Rf^{G}_{*}
. When
G^{\prime}=1
, then
Rf^{G}_{*}(E,\lambda)
is the
G
-invariant sub-object of

Rf_{*}E
with respect to the
G
-action on
Rf_{*}E
induced by
\lambda
.

Let
G
act on two smooth projective varieties
X
and
Y
. Denote by
G_{\Delta}
the diagonal subgroup of
G\times G
.
Let
(P,\rho)
be an object of
D^{b}_{G_{\Delta}}(X\times Y)
.
Let
(\pi_{X},\pi_{1}):(X\times Y,G_{\Delta})\rightarrow(X,G)
and
(\pi_{Y},\pi_{2}):(X\times Y,G_{\Delta})\rightarrow(Y,G)
be the projections from
X\times Y
to
X
and
Y
equivariant with respect to the projections from the subgroup
G_{\Delta}
of
G\times G
to the two factors. We get the Fourier-Mukai transformation

\Phi_{(P,\rho)}:D^{b}_{G}(X)\rightarrow D^{b}_{G}(Y)
, given by

(4.1)

\Phi_{(P,\rho)}(\bullet)=R(\pi_{Y},\pi_{2})_{*}^{G}\left(L(\pi_{X},\pi_{1})^{*}(\bullet)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L}}{{\otimes}}(P,\rho)\right).

Tensorization of a linearization
\lambda
by a character
\chi
of
G
yields the linearization
(\chi\otimes\lambda)_{g}=\chi(g)\lambda_{g}
. We get an auto-equivalence
\Phi_{\chi}
of each of
D^{b}_{G}(X)
and
D^{b}_{G}(Y)

sending an object
(E,\lambda)
to
(E,\chi\otimes\lambda)
and sending a morphism
f:(E_{1},\lambda_{1})\rightarrow(E_{2},\lambda_{2})
to itself under the equality of
{\rm Hom}((E_{1},\lambda_{1}),(E_{2},\lambda_{2}))
and
{\rm Hom}((E_{1},\chi\otimes\lambda_{1}),(E_{2},\chi\otimes\lambda_{2}))
as subspaces of
{\rm Hom}(E_{1},E_{2})
.
Note that
\Phi_{\chi}
commutes with the Fourier-Mukai transformation
\Phi_{(P,\rho)}
above with kernel
(P,\rho)\in D^{b}_{G_{\Delta}}(X\times Y)
,

(4.2)

\Phi_{\chi}\circ\Phi_{(P,\rho)}\cong\Phi_{(P,\rho)}\circ\Phi_{\chi}.

Let
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
be smooth projective varieties and assume that the Fourier-Mukai transformation
\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}:D^{b}(S_{1})\rightarrow D^{b}(S_{2})
with kernel an object
P\in D^{b}(S_{1}\times S_{2})
is an equivalence.
Let
X=S_{1}^{n}
,
Y=S_{2}^{n}
, and let
G={\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
act on both by permuting the factors. We get the object

P^{\boxtimes n}:=P\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes P
in
D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n,\Delta}}(S_{1}^{n}\times S_{2}^{n})
, endowed with the linearization
\rho_{\boxtimes}
permuting the factors, and

(4.3)

\Phi_{(P^{\boxtimes n},\rho_{\boxtimes})}:D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}(S_{1}^{n})\rightarrow D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}(S_{2}^{n})

is an equivalence as well.

The definition of the functor
\Phi_{(P,\rho)}
in (
4.1
) admits an obvious generalization when
\varphi:G\rightarrow G^{\prime}
is a surjective homomorphism,
G
acts on
X
,
G^{\prime}
acts on
Y
, and the subgroup
G_{\Delta}
is replaced by the subgroup
G_{\varphi}
of
G\times G^{\prime}
, which is the graph of
\varphi
. We will use this generalization in the next paragraph with
G^{\prime}=1
.

Let a finite group
G
act on a smooth projective variety
X
. Let
G
-
\mbox{Hilb}(X)
be moduli space of
G
-clusters in
X
, i.e., of
G
-invariant
0
-dimensional subschemes
Z
, such that
H^{0}(Z,{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})
is the regular representation of
G
.
When
S
is a surface,
X
is the
n
-th cartesian power
S^{n}
of
S
, and
G={\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
acts by permuting the factors, then the Hilbert scheme

S^{[n]}
is isomorphic to the connected component of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
-
\mbox{Hilb}(S^{n})
which contains the free orbits, by
[
Hai
]
. We get a universal
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}\times 1
-invariant subscheme
\Gamma\subset S^{n}\times S^{[n]}
, so that

{\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma}
, with the natural linearization
\rho
, is an object of
D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}\times 1}(S^{n}\times S^{[n]})
.
When
S
is a smooth projective surface, the Fourier-Mukai transformation

(4.4)

BKR:=\Phi_{({\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma},\rho)}:D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}(S^{n})\rightarrow D^{b}(S^{[n]})

is an equivalence, by
[
BKR
, Cor. 1.3]
.
We are using here the convention
9
9

9

The Fourier-Mukai transformation from

D^{b}(S^{[n]})
to
D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}(S^{n})

with kernel the linearized object
({\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma},\rho)
is an equivalence as well, which is often referred to as the derived McKay correspondence.

of
[
Kr
]
for the derived McKay correspondence.
Denote by
b:\Gamma\rightarrow S^{n}
the restriction of the first projection and by

q:\Gamma\rightarrow S^{[n]}
that of the second projection.

(4.5)

S^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\longleftarrow}}\Gamma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q}}{{\longrightarrow}}S^{[n]}.

The morphism
q
is
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
-invariant and flat of degree
n!
.
Note that
q
factors through the quotient of
\Gamma
by the index
2
alternating subgroup
{\mathfrak{A}}_{n}
of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
, and

\bar{q}:\Gamma/{\mathfrak{A}}_{n}\rightarrow S^{[n]}
is a double cover of
S^{[n]}
branched over the divisor
D
of non-reduced subschemes.
Hence,

\bar{q}_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma/{\mathfrak{A}}_{n}}\cong(q_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma})^{{\mathfrak{A}}_{n}}\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[n]}}\oplus{\mathcal{L}},

where
{\mathcal{L}}^{2}\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[n]}}(-D)
. Let
\chi
be the sign character of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
. Denote by
\chi
also the linearization of
{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{n}}
acting via the character
\chi
. We get the isomorphisms

(4.6)

\displaystyle BKR({\mathcal{O}}_{S^{n}},1)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle Rq_{*}^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma})\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[n]}},

\displaystyle BKR({\mathcal{O}}_{S^{n}},\chi)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle Rq_{*}^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma},\chi)\cong{\mathcal{L}}.

Given two smooth projective surfaces
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
and an object
P\in D^{b}(S_{1}\times S_{2})
, denote by

(4.7)

\Phi_{P}^{[n]}:D^{b}(S_{1}^{[n]})\rightarrow D^{b}(S_{2}^{[n]})

the
BKR
-conjugate
\Phi_{P}^{[n]}:=BKR\circ\Phi_{(P^{\boxtimes n},\rho_{\boxtimes})}\circ BKR^{-1}
of
(
4.3
). Denote by

(4.8)

\Phi_{\chi}^{[n]}:=BKR\circ\Phi_{\chi}\circ BKR^{-1}:D^{b}(S_{i}^{[n]})\rightarrow D^{b}(S_{i}^{[n]}),

i=1,2
,
the auto-equivalence obtained by conjugating the functor

\Phi_{\chi}
of tensorization by the sign character of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
. Equation (
4.2
) yields

(4.9)

\Phi_{\chi}^{[n]}\circ\Phi_{P}^{[n]}\cong\Phi_{P}^{[n]}\circ\Phi_{\chi}^{[n]}.

Equations (
4.6
) yield

(4.10)

\Phi_{\chi}^{[n]}({\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[n]}})\cong{\mathcal{L}}.

Remark 4.1
.

(1)

Let
G
be a finite group acting on a variety
X
, let
Y
be a subscheme of
X
, let
\iota:Y\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion, and let
F
be a sheaf on
Y
. Set
E:=\oplus_{g\in G}[g^{*}(\iota_{*}F)]
. Endow
E
with the linearization
\lambda
, such that

\lambda_{f}:E\rightarrow f^{*}E
maps the direct summand
g^{*}(\iota_{*}F)
to itself via the identity map. Let
\chi:G\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
be a character. Let
\eta:E\rightarrow E
be the automorphism acting on the direct summand
g^{*}(\iota_{*}F)
by
\chi(g^{-1})
.
Then
\eta
lifts to an isomorphism
\eta:(E,\lambda)\rightarrow(E,\chi\otimes\lambda)
.

(2)

Assume, furthermore, that the subschemes
Y
and
g(Y)
are disjoint, for every
g\in G
. Set
Z:=\cup_{g\in G}\ g(Y)
and let
e:Z\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion. Let
(A,\rho)
be an object of
D^{b}_{G}(X)
. Set
F:=\iota^{*}A
.
The linearization
\rho
induces an isomorphism between
e_{*}e^{*}A
and the object
E=\oplus_{g\in G}[g^{*}(\iota_{*}F)]
above. Indeed,

e
restricts to
g^{-1}(Y)
as
g^{-1}\circ\iota\circ g
and so the direct summand of
e_{*}e^{*}A
supported on
g^{-1}(Y)
is

(g^{-1}\circ\iota\circ g)_{*}(g^{-1}\circ\iota\circ g)^{*}A=g^{*}\left[\iota_{*}(\iota^{*}(g^{-1})^{*}A)\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho_{g^{-1}}}}{{\rightarrow}}g^{*}(\iota_{*}\iota^{*}(A)),

and the right hand side is the direct summand of
E
supported on
g^{-1}(Y)
.
We claim that
e_{*}e^{*}(A,\rho)
is isomorphic to the object
(E,\lambda)
above.
In particular,
e_{*}e^{*}(A,\rho)
is isomorphic to
e_{*}e^{*}(A,\chi\otimes\rho)
.
It suffices to prove, more generally, that any two linearizations
(E,\rho)
and
(E,\lambda)
of
E
are isomorphic.
We need to construct an automorphism
\phi
of
E
satisfying

(4.11)

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{g^{*}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{*}(\phi)}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{g}}
\textstyle{g^{*}E,}

for all
g\in G
.
Let
E_{g(Y)}:=g_{*}\iota_{*}F
be the direct summand of
E
supported on
g(Y)
.
Let
\rho_{g}(f(Y))
be the component of
\rho_{g}:E\rightarrow g^{*}E
supported on
f(Y)
. We identify the factor of
{\rm Aut}(g^{*}E)
supported on
Y
with
{\rm Aut}(E_{g(Y)})
and
define
\phi(g(Y))\in{\rm Aut}(E_{g(Y)})
by

\phi(g(Y))=\rho_{g}(Y)(\lambda_{g}(Y))^{-1}.

Note that
\phi(Y)=id
, since
\rho_{1}
and
\lambda_{1}
are both the identity.
The diagram above commutes over the component
Y
of
Z
, by definition of
\phi
. Consider the diagram

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{gf}}
\textstyle{f^{*}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}(\phi)}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}(\lambda_{g})}
\textstyle{f^{*}g^{*}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(gf)^{*}(\phi)}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{gf}}
\textstyle{f^{*}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}(\rho_{g})}
\textstyle{f^{*}g^{*}E.}

The upper and lower domes commute, by definition of a linearization.
The left and outer squares commute over the component
Y
, by definition of
\phi
. Hence, the right square commute as well over
Y
. Hence, the square (
4.11
) commutes also over
f(Y)
, for all
f\in G
.

5.
The derived monodromy group

Taelman introduced a natural action of the group
{\rm Aut}(D^{b}(X))
of auto-equivalences of
D^{b}(X)
on the rational LLV lattice
\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})

[
Ta
, Theorem C]
.
We review Taelman’s results, as they play a central role in Section
6
.

Let
IHSM
be the following groupoid. Objects of
IHSM
are pairs
(X,\epsilon)
, where
X
is a projective irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifold satisfying the condition that if
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(X)
is divisible by
4
then the dimension of
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
is odd. All known examples satisfy this latter condition.
If the complex dimension of
X
is divisible by
4
, then
X
is enriched with the data
\epsilon
of an orientation of the vector space
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
(which determines an orientation of
\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
). If the complex dimension of
X
is not divisible by
4
, then the data
\epsilon
is empty.
The morphisms in

{\rm Hom}_{IHSM}((X,\epsilon),(Y,\epsilon^{\prime}))

are vector spaces isomorphisms in
{\rm Hom}(H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}),H^{*}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}}))
, which are
compositions of parallel transport operators and isomorphisms induced by
derived equivalences (independent of the data
\epsilon
).
Explicitly, a morphism
f\in{\rm Hom}_{IHSM}(X,Y)
is the composition
f_{n}\circ\cdots\circ f_{2}\circ f_{1}
, where
X_{i}
is an irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifold deformation equivalent to
X
,
X_{0}=X
,
X_{n}=Y
, and

f_{i}:H^{*}(X_{i-1},{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow H^{*}(X_{i},{\mathbb{Q}})
is either a parallel transport operator
10
10

10

A parallel transport operator
f:H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow H^{*}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})

is the parallel-transport in the local system
R\pi_{*}{\mathbb{Q}}
associated to a path from a point
b_{0}
to a point
b_{1}
in the analytic base
B
of a smooth and proper family
\pi:{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow B
of irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifolds, not necessarily projective, with fiber
X
over
b_{0}
and
Y
over
b_{1}
.

or the isomorphism induced by the correspondence
\pi_{i-1}^{*}\sqrt{td_{X_{i-1}}}ch({\mathcal{P}})\pi_{i}^{*}\sqrt{td_{X_{i}}}
, where
X_{i-1}
and
X_{i}
are projective,
{\mathcal{P}}\in D^{b}(X_{i-1}\times X_{i})
is the kernel of the Fourier-Mukai functor
\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}:D^{b}(X_{i-1})\rightarrow D^{b}(X_{i})
, and
\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}
is an exact equivalence of triangulated categories.

Definition 5.1
.

The
derived monodromy group

DMon(X)
is the subgroup
{\rm Hom}_{IHSM}((X,\epsilon),(X,\epsilon))
of
GL(H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))

(which is independent of
\epsilon
).
A
derived parallel transport operator
is a linear transformation
\phi:H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow H^{*}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})
, which belongs to
{\rm Hom}_{IHSM}((X,\epsilon),(Y,\epsilon^{\prime}))
.

Let
Lat
be the category of rational vector spaces with a non-degenerate inner product. Morphisms in
Lat
are isometries.
Taelman defines the functor

(5.1)

\tilde{H}:IHSM\rightarrow Lat

sending an object
X
to its rational LLV lattice
\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
given in (
2.1
).
The functor
\tilde{H}
sends a morphism in

f\in{\rm Hom}_{IHSM}((X,\epsilon),(Y,\epsilon^{\prime}))
to an isometry
\tilde{H}(f):\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow\tilde{H}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})
.
If
f
is induced by a parallel transport operator and
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(X)=2n
for odd
n
, then
\tilde{H}(f)
is induced by the restriction of
f
to the second cohomology extended via the identity to the direct summand
U_{\mathbb{Q}}
.
If
f
is induced by a parallel transport operator, the dimension of
X
is divisible by
4
,
and
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
is odd dimensional, then
\tilde{H}(f)
is defined by the above description only up to sign, and the sign is chosen so that
\tilde{H}(f)
is orientation preserving.
If
f
is induced by a derived equivalence
\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}
as above, then

\tilde{H}(f)
is the isometry defined
in
[
Ta
, Theorems 4.7 and 4.9]
, which we will recall in Theorem
5.3
below. We get the homomorphism

(5.2)

\tilde{H}:DMon(X)\rightarrow O(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})).

If
\lambda\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
is
c_{1}(L)
, for some line bundle
L\in{\rm Pic}(X)
, then
tensorization by
L
corresponds to the element of
DMon(X)
acting on
H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
by multiplication by
ch(L)=\exp(\lambda)
and the latter is mapped via
\tilde{H}
to

exp(e_{\lambda}):=id+e_{\lambda}+e^{2}_{\lambda}/2
in
SO(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))
, where
e_{\lambda}
is given in Equation (
2.2
), by
[
Ta
, Theorem 3.1 and Prop. 3.2]
.

Let
K3^{[n]}
be the full subgroupid of
IHSM
, whose objects are projective irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifolds deformation equivalent to the Hilbert scheme
S^{[n]}
of length
n
subschemes of a
K3
surface
S
. We get the functor

(5.3)

{}^{[n]}:K3^{[1]}\rightarrow K3^{[n]}

sending a
K3
surface
S
to its Hilbert scheme
S^{[n]}
, sending a parallel transport operator associated to the path in the base of a family of
K3
surfaces to the parallel transport operator associated to the same path for the relative family of Hilbert schemes over the same base, and sending the morphism associated to an equivalence
\Phi:D^{b}(S_{1})\rightarrow D^{n}(S_{2})
to the morphism associated to
\Phi^{[n]}:D^{b}(S_{1}^{[n]})\rightarrow D^{b}(S_{2}^{[n]})
obtained by conjugating

\Phi\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\Phi:D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}(S_{1}^{n})\rightarrow D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}(S_{2}^{n})
with the BKR equivalence

D^{b}(S_{i}^{[n]})\rightarrow D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}(S_{i}^{n})
,
i=1,2
, given in (
4.4
).

Let
SH^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
be the subalgebra of
H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
generated by
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
.
Then
SH^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
is an irreducible
{\mathfrak{g}}
-sub-module of
H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
, which is isomorphic to the irreducible submodule of the symmetric power

{\rm Sym}^{n}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))
containing
\alpha^{n}
, by
[
Ta
, Prop. 3.5 and 3.7]
. The embedding is given by

(5.4)

\displaystyle\Psi:SH^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle{\rm Sym}^{n}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})),

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{n}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle e_{\lambda_{1}}\cdots e_{\lambda_{n}}(\alpha^{n}/n!),

where
\lambda_{i}\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
,
1\leq i\leq n
, and

e_{\lambda}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n}):=\sum_{i}x_{1}\cdots e_{\lambda}(x_{i})\cdots x_{n}
. Note that
\Psi(1)=\alpha^{n}/n!
and

(5.5)

\Psi([pt])=\beta^{n}/c_{X},

where
c_{X}
is the Fujiki constant,
by
[
Ta
, Lemma 3.6]
.
The image of
\Psi
is equal to the kernel of the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-module homomorphism

(5.6)

\Delta:{\rm Sym}^{n}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))\rightarrow{\rm Sym}^{n-2}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})),

\Delta(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})=\sum_{i<j}(x_{i},x_{j})x_{1}\cdots\hat{x}_{i}\cdots\hat{x}_{j}\cdots x_{n}
, by
[
Ta
, Lemma 3.7]
.
Hence, the image of
\Psi
is equal to the subspace spanned by
n
-th powers of isotropic elements of
\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
. The latter subspace is an irreducible
{\mathfrak{g}}
-submodule that appears with multiplicity one in
{\rm Sym}^{n}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))
.
We get a natural
{\mathfrak{g}}
-equivariant projection

(5.7)

{\rm Sym}^{n}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))\rightarrow Im(\Psi).

The submodule
SH^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
appears with multiplicity one in
H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
, by
[
GKLR
, Prop. 2.32]
, so we get a
{\mathfrak{g}}
-equivariant projection

(5.8)

H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow SH^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}).

Example 5.2
.

Suppose that
\dim(X)=4
, so that
n=2
.
Let
\tilde{q}\in{\rm Sym}^{2}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))
be the unique
{\mathfrak{g}}
-invariant class, such that

\Delta(\tilde{q})=1
. If
\{x_{i}\}
is an orthogonal basis of
\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
,
then
\tilde{q}=\frac{1}{\dim\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})}\sum_{i}\frac{1}{(x_{i},x_{i})}x_{i}^{2}
.
The projection from
{\rm Sym}^{2}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))
to
the image of
\Psi:SH^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow{\rm Sym}^{2}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))
maps
the square
\gamma^{2}
of a class

\gamma\in\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
to
\gamma^{2}-(\gamma,\gamma)\tilde{q}
.

Theorem A in
[
Ta
]
states that the morphism
\phi
in
{\rm Hom}_{IHSM}((X,\epsilon_{1}),(Y,\epsilon_{2}))
induced by an equivalence of derived categories
\Phi:D^{b}(X)\rightarrow D^{b}(Y)
conjugates the LLV algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{X}
to

{\mathfrak{g}}_{Y}
. The latter statement is clear for parallel-transport operators, and so holds for every morphism
\phi

in
{\rm Hom}_{IHSM}((X,\epsilon_{1}),(Y,\epsilon_{2}))
.
It follows that
\phi
maps
SH^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
to
SH^{*}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})
, as each is an irreducible subrepresentation of the LLV algebra, which appears with multiplicity
1
. Denote by

SH(\phi):SH^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow SH^{*}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})

the restriction of
\phi
to
SH^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
.

Theorem 5.3
.

(
[
Ta
, Theorems 4.7 and 4.9]
)
Assume that
X
and
Y
are of dimension
2n
and
\phi
is a morphism in
{\rm Hom}_{IHSM}((X,\epsilon_{1}),(Y,\epsilon_{2}))
.
There exists a unique isometry
\widetilde{H}(\phi):\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow\widetilde{H}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})
making the following diagram commutative

\textstyle{SH^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon(\widetilde{H}(\phi))SH(\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\textstyle{SH^{*}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\textstyle{{\rm Sym}^{n}\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Sym}^{n}(\widetilde{H}(\phi))}
\textstyle{{\rm Sym}^{n}\widetilde{H}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}}),}

where

\epsilon(\widetilde{H}(\phi))=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccl}1&\mbox{if}&n\ \mbox{is odd},\\
1&\mbox{if}&n\ \mbox{is even and}\ \widetilde{H}(\phi)\ \mbox{is orientation preserving,}\\
-1&\mbox{if}&n\ \mbox{is even and}\ \widetilde{H}(\phi)\ \mbox{is orientation reversing.}\end{array}\right.

Remark 5.4
.

Assume
n
is even. In that case
{\rm Sym}^{n}(\widetilde{H}(\phi))={\rm Sym}^{n}(-\widetilde{H}(\phi))
.
Let
IHSM_{n}
be the full subcategory of
IHSM
, whose objects
(X,\epsilon_{X})
consist of
X
of dimension
2n
.
There is a functor
\chi
from the groupoid
IHSM_{n}
to the group
\mu_{2}:=\{\pm 1\}
, which is defined as follows. The image of
\Psi_{X}:SH^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow{\rm Sym}^{n}\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
is an irreducible
{\mathfrak{g}}_{X}:={\mathfrak{s}o}(\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))
-representation spanned by the subvariety
Isot_{X}
of
n
-th powers of non-zero isotropic classes in

\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
. The analogous subvariety
Isot_{X,{\mathbb{R}}}
of
{\rm Sym}^{n}\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{R}})
is connected (in the classical topology) being the image of the complement of the origin in the quadric cone in
\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{R}})
.
Indeed, the signature of
\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{R}})
is
(4,b_{2}(X)-2)
and so the complement of the origin in the quadric cone in
\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{R}})
is homotopic to the product of a
3
-sphere and a
b_{2}(X)-3
sphere. The image

-Isot_{X,{\mathbb{R}}}
of
Isot_{X,{\mathbb{R}}}
under multiplication by
-1
is disjoint from
Isot_{X,{\mathbb{R}}}
, since
n
is even.
If
\phi
belongs to

{\rm Hom}_{IHSM}((X,\epsilon_{X}),(Y,\epsilon_{Y}))
, then
\Psi_{Y}\circ SH(\phi)\circ\Psi_{X}^{-1}
maps

Isot_{X,{\mathbb{R}}}
isomorphically to one of
Isot_{Y,{\mathbb{R}}}
or
-Isot_{Y,{\mathbb{R}}}
, and we set
\chi(\phi)=1
, if
the image is
Isot_{Y,{\mathbb{R}}}
and
-1
if it is
-Isot_{Y,{\mathbb{R}}}
. Note that the functor
\chi
is independent of the data of orientations.
If
\phi
arrises from a parallel transport operator, then
\chi(\phi)=1
. If
\phi
corresponds to an equivalence of derived categories
\Phi:D^{b}(X)\rightarrow D^{b}(Y)
mapping the sky-scraper sheaf of some point to an object
F
of positive rank, then

\chi(\phi)=1
, since then
(\Psi_{Y}\circ SH(\phi)\circ\Psi_{X}^{-1})(\beta^{n}/c_{X})=\Psi(v(F))
and both
c_{X}
and the coefficient
{\rm rank}(F)/n!
of
\alpha^{n}

in
\Psi(v(F))
are positive.
Taelman first proves that there exists an isometry

\widetilde{H}(\phi)
, unique up to sign, such that the diagram

\textstyle{SH^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi(\phi)SH(\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{X}}
\textstyle{SH^{*}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{Y}}
\textstyle{{\rm Sym}^{n}\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Sym}^{n}(\widetilde{H}(\phi))}
\textstyle{{\rm Sym}^{n}\widetilde{H}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})}

commutes. The orientations of the objects in
IHSM_{n}
are introduced in order to determine the sign of
\widetilde{H}(\phi)
so that

\chi(\phi)=\epsilon(\widetilde{H}(\phi))
.

Remark 5.5
.

We sketch the more functorial description of
\Psi
as a natural transformation. We have the functor
SH:IHSM\rightarrow Lat
sending an irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifold
X
to the subring
SH^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
of
H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
, forgetting the ring structure but endowing it with the restriction of the Mukai pairing of
H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})

[
Ta
, Sec. 3.2]
. We have the endofunctor

Isot_{n}:Lat\rightarrow Lat
sending a rational vector space
V
with a bilinear pairing to the subspace
V(n)
of
{\rm Sym}^{n}(V)
spanned by
n
-th powers of isotropic vectors.
We have the full functor
F_{n}:IHSM_{n}\rightarrow IHSM
from the category
IHSM_{n}
of irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifolds of dimension
2n
into that without dimension restriction.
Then the above construction produces the natural transformation

\Psi:SH\circ F_{n}\rightarrow Isot_{n}\circ\tilde{H}\circ F_{n}
.

6.
The LLV line of an object of
D^{b}(X)
which deforms in co-dimension
1
in
HH^{2}(X)

Let
X
be a projective irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifold.
In Section
6.1
we review results of Taelman and Verbitsky
stating that two Lie subalgebras of
{\rm End}(H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{C}}))
coincide, one is
the LLV Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{X,{\mathbb{C}}}
of
X
, defined in terms of the cohomology ring of
X
, and the other is an analogous Lie algebra defined in terms of the Hochschield cohomology of
X
.
The vector space
HT^{2}(X)
naturally embeds in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{X,{\mathbb{C}}}
.

In Section
6.2

we show that if a hyperplane
\Sigma
in
HT^{2}(X)
annihilates a non-zero rational class in the subspace of

{\rm Sym}^{n}\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
,
n=\frac{1}{2}\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(X)
, spanned by powers of isotropic classes, then
\Sigma
and its complex conjugate generate
the Lie subalgebra of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{X,{\mathbb{C}}}
annihilating some non-zero rational class in
\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})

(Lemma
6.9
).

Let
F
be an object in
D^{b}(X)
.
In Section
6.3
we show that the kernel in
HT^{2}(X)
of the obstruction map
{\rm obs}^{HT}_{F}
, given in (
3.1
),
is mapped via the Duflo automorphism of
HT^{2}(X)
into the stabilizer of the Mukai vector
v(F)
of
F
. We use a result of Huang relating
{\rm obs}^{HT}_{F}
and
{\rm obs}_{F}
, given in (
1.1
)

[
Hua
]
.

In section
6.4
we associate to every object
F
in
D^{b}(X)
with a rank
1
obstruction map

HT^{2}(X)\rightarrow{\rm Hom}(F,F[2])

a line
\ell(F)
in

\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
(Theorem
6.13
).
The construction is equivariant with respect to the
{\rm Aut}(D^{b}(X))
-action.
We calculate the line
\ell(F)
when
F
is a sky-scraper sheaf (Example
6.16
) and when
F
is the structure sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
of
X
of
K3^{[n]}
and generalized Kummer deformation types (Lemma
6.18
).

In Section
6.5

we show that if a torsion sheaf
F
has a rank
1
obstruction map and is
supported on a proper irreducible subvariety
Z
of
X
, then
Z
is either a lagrangian subvariety or a point (Theorem
6.24
). We calculate the line
\ell(F)
for the structure sheaf of a lagrangian subvariety (Lemma
6.25
).

6.1.
The Hochschild Lie algebra of a holomorphic symplectic manifold

Let
X
be a
2n
-dimensional irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifold. Let
h
be the endomorphism of
H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
acting on
H^{k}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
by multiplication by
k-2n
.
Let
h^{\prime}
be the endomorphism of
H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
acting on
H^{p,q}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
by multiplication by
q-p
.
Choose a holomorphic symplectic form
\sigma
in
H^{0}(X,\Omega^{2}_{X})
.
The volume form
\sigma^{n}
induces the isomorphism of vector bundles
\gamma:\wedge^{i}TX\rightarrow\Omega^{2n-i}_{X}
.
Denote by
\gamma:HT^{*}(X)\rightarrow H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
the induced isomorphism. Set

H\Omega_{*}(X):=\oplus_{p,q}H^{p,q}(X)
.
We identify
H\Omega_{*}(X)
with
H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
via the Hodge decomposition, but the grading of
H\Omega_{*}(X)
is determined by
h^{\prime}
, i.e.,

H\Omega_{d}(X)=\bigoplus_{q-p=d}H^{p,q}(X).

Let

(6.1)

m:HT^{*}(X)\rightarrow{\rm End}(H\Omega_{*}(X))

be the associative algebras homomorphism corresponding to the
HT^{*}(X)
-module structure.
The isomorphism
\gamma
relates the ring structure of
HT^{*}(X)
with the
HT^{*}(X)
-module structure of
H\Omega_{*}(X)
as follows. Given
a\in HT^{2i}(X)
and
b\in HT^{*}(X)
we have

(6.2)

m_{a}(\gamma(b))=\gamma(a\wedge b).

For
a\in HT^{k}(X)
, the endomorphism
m_{a}
has degree
k
with respect to the grading
h^{\prime}
, i.e.

m_{a}:H\Omega_{i}(X)\rightarrow H\Omega_{i+k}(X)
.

We say that
m_{e}
,
e\in HT^{2}(X)
, has the
hard Lefschetz property
with respect to
h^{\prime}
, if there exists an element
f\in{\rm End}(H\Omega_{*}(X))
, such that the triple
(m_{e},h^{\prime},f)
is an
{\mathfrak{s}l}_{2}
triple, i,e.,

[h^{\prime},m_{e}]=2m_{e},\ [h^{\prime},f]=-2f,\ [m_{e},f]=h^{\prime}.

The symplectic form induces the isomorphism
\sigma:\wedge^{i}TX\rightarrow\Omega^{i}_{X}
. On the level of cohomology it induces a graded ring isomorphism
\sigma:HT^{*}(X)\rightarrow H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
.
A Zariski dense open subset of
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
consists of elements
e
which have the Hard Lefschetz property with respect to
h
,
by the Hard Lefschetz Theorem.
It follows that a Zariski dense open subset of
HT^{2}(X)
consists of elements
e
, such that the endomorphism of
HT^{*}(X)
of multiplication by
e
has the Hard Lefschetz property with respect to the grading of
HT^{*}(X)
. Consequently, a Zariski dense open subset of
HT^{2}(X)
consists of elements
e
, such that the endomorphism
m_{e}
of
H\Omega_{*}(X)
has the Hard Lefschetz property with respect to
the grading
h^{\prime}
, since
\gamma
is an isomorphism of graded
HT^{*}(X)
-modules, by Equation (
6.2
).
Let
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
be the subalgebra of
{\rm End}(H\Omega_{*}(X))
generated by all
{\mathfrak{s}l}_{2}
triples
(m_{e},h^{\prime},f)
, where
e
is in
HT^{2}(X)
.

Composing the isomorphism
\gamma
with the inverse of
\sigma
we get the isomorphism
\eta=\gamma\circ\sigma^{-1}:\Omega^{j}_{X}\rightarrow\Omega^{2n-j}_{X}
inducing the isomorphism

\eta^{i,j}:H^{i}(\Omega^{j}_{X})\rightarrow H^{i}(\Omega^{2n-j}_{X}).

Set
\eta:=\oplus_{0\leq i,j\leq 2n}\eta^{i,j}:H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{C}})\rightarrow H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
. The following equality is evident

\eta\circ h\circ\eta^{-1}=h^{\prime}.

Equation (
6.2
) translates to

(6.3)

m_{a}(\gamma(b))=\eta(\sigma(a)\cup\sigma(b)),

for
a\in HT^{2i}(X)
,
where on the right the cup product is that of the cohomology ring structure.
Denote by
e_{\sigma(a)}
the endomorphism of
H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
of multiplication by
\sigma(a)
, for
a\in HT^{2}(X)
.
Equation (
6.3
) yields

(6.4)

\eta\circ e_{\sigma(a)}\circ\eta^{-1}=m_{a}

and
(e_{\sigma(a)},h,f)
is a
{\mathfrak{s}l}_{2}
triple, if and only if
(m_{a},h^{\prime},\eta\circ f\circ\eta^{-1})

is. Thus,
\eta
conjugates
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
to
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
.

(6.5)

\eta{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}\eta^{-1}={\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}.

The group
{\rm Spin}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}}))
acts on
H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
integrating the infinitesimal action of the LLV Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}:={\mathfrak{s}o}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}})).

Denote the resulting representation by

(6.6)

\rho:{\rm Spin}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}}))\rightarrow GL(H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{C}})).

It is known that
\rho
is injective, if the odd cohomology of
X
is non trivial. Furthermore, the restriction of
\rho
to the even cohomology factors through an injective homomorphism

(6.7)

\bar{\rho}:SO(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}}))\rightarrow GL(H^{ev}(X,{\mathbb{C}})).

The following is a fundamental result of Verbitsky.

Theorem 6.1
.

[
Ve1
, Theorem 9.1 and 9.7(i)]

The operator
\eta
is an involution and it belongs to the image of
\rho
.

Consequently, we get the equality

(6.8)

{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}={\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},

since the left hand side of (
6.5
) is equal to
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
. An alternative proof of Equality (
6.8
) is provided in
[
Ta
, Prop. 2.9]
.

Corollary 6.2
.

The image
m(HT^{2}(X)))
of
HT^{2}(X)
in
{\rm End}(H\Omega_{*}(X))
, via the homomorphism
m
given in (
6.1
), is contained in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
, yielding

(6.9)

m:HT^{2}(X)\rightarrow{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}.

The operator
h^{\prime}
belongs to
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

The operator
h^{\prime}
belongs to
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
, by definition, hence also to
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
.
Similarly,
m_{a}
belongs to
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
, for a Zariski dense open subset of
HT^{2}(X)
, by definition, hence also to
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
.
∎

The
{\mathfrak{g}}
-equivariance of
\Psi
, given in (
5.4
), and the fact that
\Psi
maps the class
1\in SH^{0}(X,{\mathbb{C}})

to
\alpha^{n}/n!\in{\rm Sym}^{n}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}}))
and the class
[pt]\in SH^{4n}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
to
\beta^{n}/c_{X}
, induces a Hodge structure on the image of
\Psi
such that
\alpha^{n}
is of type
(0,0)
,
\beta^{n}
is of type
(2n,2n)
,
\Psi
is a morphism of Hodge structures, and the Hodge direct summand of weight
(p,q)
in
Im(\Psi)

is the common eigenspace of
h
and
h^{\prime}
of weights
p+q-2n
and
q-p
respectively. The Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
is a sub Hodge structure of
{\rm End}(H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))
and, given a class
\lambda\in H^{p,q}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
with
p+q=2
, the operator

e_{\lambda}:SH^{*}(X,{\mathbb{C}})\rightarrow SH^{*}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
has Hodge type
(p,q)
. Hence, the same holds for the operator
e_{\lambda}
acting on
Im(\Psi)
with respect to the induced Hodge structure. Any
{\mathfrak{g}}
-module gets endowed with a Hodge structure and in particular so does the rational Hodge lattice
\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
and the latter induces one on
{\rm Sym}^{n}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}}))
of which the image of
\Psi
is a sub Hodge structure with respect to the same Hodge structure induced above.
We will denote by
\tilde{H}^{p,q}_{{\rm fine}}(X)
the Hodge direct summand of weight
(p,q)
, in order to distinguish it from the Hodge decomposition of the pure Hodge structure of weight
0
on
\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
introduced in Section
2.1
.
The class
\alpha
has weight
(0,0)
and the class
\beta
has weight
(2,2)
and the direct summand
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
in
\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
is a sub Hodge structure with its original weight
2
Hodge structure.

Theorem
6.1
determines an action of
\eta
on
\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
,
by the inverse of
\bar{\rho}
, given in (
6.7
), of the restriction of
\eta
to the even cohomology of
X
.
Conjugation by the operator
\eta
interchanges
h
and
h^{\prime}
. Hence,
\eta(\tilde{H}^{p,q}_{\rm fine}(X))=\tilde{H}^{2-p,q}_{\rm fine}(X)
.
Denote by
\sigma
the class in
\tilde{H}^{2,0}_{\rm fine}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
corresponding to the chosen symplectic form under the identification

\tilde{H}^{2,0}_{\rm fine}(X,{\mathbb{C}})=H^{2,0}(X)
.
Given
\lambda\in H^{i}(\wedge^{j}TX)
,
i+j=2
, the operator
m_{\lambda}
, given in (
6.9
), has Hodge type
(-j,i).

Hence,
m_{\lambda}(\sigma)
has Hodge type
(i,i)
. We get the homomorphism

(6.10)

\displaystyle\mu:HT^{2}(X)

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}})

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle m_{\lambda}(\sigma).

We have
(\eta\circ e_{\sigma(\lambda)}\circ\eta^{-1})(\sigma)=m_{\lambda}(\sigma)
, by
(
6.4
). Now
\eta^{-1}(\sigma)=c\alpha
, for a non-zero scalar
c
, and the homomorphism

\lambda\mapsto e_{\sigma(\lambda)}(\alpha)
is injective. Hence, the homomorphism

\mu
is injective as well. We have the commutative diagram

\textstyle{H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{ev_{\alpha}}
\textstyle{\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}})}
\textstyle{HT^{2}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\scriptstyle{m}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Ad_{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{ev_{\sigma}}
\textstyle{\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}}),\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\frac{1}{c}\eta}

where
\iota
is the natural inclusion and
ev_{\alpha}
and
ev_{\sigma}
are the evaluations of an endomorphism of
\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
on the elements
\alpha
and
\sigma
. The top dome commutes by Equation (
2.2
) and the bottom by definition of
\mu
.
The left square commutes by Equation
(
6.4
) and the right by the equality
\eta^{-1}(\sigma)=c\alpha
and the fact that
\eta=\eta^{-1}

(Theorem
6.1
). Note the equalities

(6.11)

\mu(H^{0}(\wedge^{2}TX))={\mathbb{C}}\alpha,\ \ \ \mu(H^{1}(TX))=H^{1,1}(X),\ \ \ \mbox{and}\ \ \ \mu(H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}))={\mathbb{C}}\beta.

The Hodge structure of Section
2.1
is related to the above by setting

\tilde{H}^{1,-1}(X)=\tilde{H}^{2,0}_{\rm fine}(X)
,
\tilde{H}^{-1,1}(X)=\tilde{H}^{0,2}_{\rm fine}(X)
, and

\tilde{H}^{0,0}(X)=\tilde{H}^{0,0}_{\rm fine}(X)\oplus\tilde{H}^{1,1}_{\rm fine}(X)\oplus\tilde{H}^{2,2}_{\rm fine}(X).

In particular, the homomorphism
\mu
, given in (
6.10
), is an isomorphism
between
HT^{2}(X)
and
\tilde{H}^{0,0}(X)
. The homomorphism
\mu
thus pulls back the Mukai pairing to a non-degenerate pairing on
HT^{2}(X)
satisfying

(6.12)

(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}):=(m_{\lambda_{1}}(\sigma),m_{\lambda_{2}}(\sigma)),

for all
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in HT^{2}(X)
.
The homomorphism
\mu

depends only on the choice of
\sigma
, and is thus canonical up to a scalar multiple. Hence, so is the pairing (
6.12
). In particular, a hyperplane
\Sigma
in

HT^{2}(X)
determines the orthogonal line
\Sigma^{\perp}
in
HT^{2}(X)
, and hence the line
\ell(\Sigma)
in

\tilde{H}^{0,0}(X)
, which is the image of
\Sigma^{\perp}
via (
6.10
).

(6.13)

\ell(\Sigma):=\mu(\Sigma^{\perp}):=\{m_{\lambda}(\sigma)\ :\ \lambda\in\Sigma^{\perp}\}.

Assume
\sigma
is normalized so that
(\sigma,\bar{\sigma})=1
.

Lemma 6.3
.

The following equality holds for all
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in HT^{2}(X)
.

(6.14)

m_{\lambda_{1}}(m_{\lambda_{2}}(\sigma))=-(\mu(\lambda_{1}),\mu(\lambda_{2}))\bar{\sigma}.

Proof.

We have

(m_{\lambda_{1}}(m_{\lambda_{2}}(\sigma)),\sigma)=-(m_{\lambda_{2}}(\sigma),m_{\lambda_{1}}(\sigma))=-(\mu(\lambda_{1}),\mu(\lambda_{2})),

where the first equality is due to the fact that
m_{\lambda_{1}}
belongs to
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
, by Corollary
6.2
, and the second equality follows by the definition of
\mu
.
Now
m_{\lambda}
has degree
2
with respect to
h^{\prime}
, for all
\lambda\in HT^{2}(X)
,
and so
m_{\lambda_{1}}(m_{\lambda_{2}}(\sigma))
belongs to
\tilde{H}^{0,2}_{\rm fine}(X)
and is thus of the form
t\bar{\sigma}
, for a scalar
t
. We get that
t=(\sigma,t\bar{\sigma})=(\sigma,m_{\lambda_{1}}(m_{\lambda_{2}}(\sigma)))=-(\mu(\lambda_{1}),\mu(\lambda_{2}))
.
∎

6.2.
Subalgebras of the Hochschild Lie algebra generated by a hyperplane in
HT^{2}(X)

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
\Sigma
be a hyperplane in
HT^{2}(X)
. Let
\ell_{0}
be a line in
\tilde{H}^{0,0}(X)={\mathbb{C}}\alpha\oplus H^{1,1}(X)\oplus{\mathbb{C}}\beta
. The image
m(\Sigma)
of
\Sigma
in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
via (
6.9
) is contained in the subalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},\ell_{0}}
of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
annihilating
\ell_{0}
, if and only if
\ell_{0}=\ell(\Sigma)
.

Proof.

Let
\lambda_{0}
be a non-zero class in
\Sigma^{\perp}
. Then
m_{\lambda_{0}}(\sigma)
spans
\ell(\Sigma)
. Given a class
\lambda\in HT^{2}(X)
we have

m_{\lambda}(m_{\lambda_{0}}(\sigma))=-(\lambda_{0},\lambda)\bar{\sigma},

by Equation (
6.14
) and the definition of the pairing on
HH^{2}(T)
.
We conclude that
m_{\lambda}
annihilates
\ell(\Sigma)
, if and only if
\lambda
belongs to
\Sigma
.
∎

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
g\in O(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}}))
be an isometry which leaves invariant every element of
{\rm span}\{\sigma,\bar{\sigma}\}
.
Then

(6.15)

gm_{\lambda}g^{-1}=m_{\mu^{-1}(g(\mu(\lambda)))},

where
\mu^{-1}:\tilde{H}^{0,0}(X)\rightarrow HT^{2}(X)
is the inverse of
\mu
.

Proof.

We have

(gm_{\lambda}g^{-1})(\sigma)=g(m_{\lambda}(\sigma))=g(\mu(\lambda))=m_{\mu^{-1}(g(\mu(\lambda)))}(\sigma).

Every element of
\tilde{H}^{0,0}(X)
is of the form
\mu(\lambda^{\prime})
, for some
\lambda^{\prime}\in HT^{2}(X)
, and

m_{\mu^{-1}(g(\mu(\lambda)))}(\mu(\lambda^{\prime}))=-(g(\mu(\lambda)),\mu(\lambda^{\prime}))\bar{\sigma}=-(\mu(\lambda),g^{-1}(\mu(\lambda^{\prime})))\bar{\sigma},

(gm_{\lambda}g^{-1})(\mu(\lambda^{\prime}))=-g[(\mu(\lambda),g^{-1}(\mu(\lambda^{\prime})))\bar{\sigma}]=-(\mu(\lambda),g^{-1}(\mu(\lambda^{\prime})))\bar{\sigma}.

Finally, both sides of (
6.15
) annihilate
\bar{\sigma}
.
∎

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
U_{0}\subset HT^{2}(X)
be a
2
-dimensional subspace such that the subspace
\mu(U_{0})
of
\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
is defined over
{\mathbb{R}}
and the restriction of the pairing to
\mu(U_{0})
is non-degenerate.
Let
U_{0}^{\perp}
be the subspace of
HT^{2}(X)
which is the orthogonal complement of
U_{0}
with respect to the pairing (
6.12
). Then the subalgebra of

{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}:={\mathfrak{s}o}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}}))
generated by
m(U_{0}^{\perp})
and its complex conjugate

\overline{m(U_{0}^{\perp})}
is equal to the subalgebra

{\mathfrak{s}o}[\tilde{H}^{1,-1}(X)\oplus\mu(U_{0}^{\perp})\oplus\tilde{H}^{-1,1}(X)]
annihilating
\mu(U_{0})
.

Proof.

The proof is identical to Step 2 in the proof of Lemma
2.3
replacing
\iota:H^{1}(TX)\rightarrow{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
by
m:U_{0}^{\perp}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

∎

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
\ell\subset HT^{2}(X)
be a
1
-dimensional subspace such that the line
\mu(\ell)
in
\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
is defined over
{\mathbb{R}}
. Let
\Sigma
be the subspace of
HT^{2}(X)
which is the orthogonal complement of
\ell
with respect to the pairing (
6.12
). Then the subalgebra of

{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
generated by
m(\Sigma)
and its complex conjugate

\overline{m(\Sigma)}
is equal to the subalgebra

{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},\ell}
annihilating
\mu(\ell)
.

Proof.

The subspace
m(\Sigma)
of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
is contained in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},\ell}
,
by Lemma
6.4
, and its complex conjugate
\overline{m(\Sigma)}

is contained in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},\ell}
, since
\mu(\ell)
is defined over
{\mathbb{R}}
. Hence, the subalgebra
{\mathfrak{a}}
of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
generated by
m(\Sigma)
and

\overline{m(\Sigma)}
is contained in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},\ell}
.
Furthermore,
{\mathfrak{a}}
contains the subalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},U_{0}}
annihilating
U_{0}
, for every two dimensional subspace
U_{0}
of
\tilde{H}^{0,0}(X)
, which is defined over
{\mathbb{R}}
, contains
\mu(\ell)
,
and such that the induced pairing on
U_{0}
is non-degenerate,
by Lemma
6.6
.

Choose such a
U_{0}
. We get the flag
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},U_{0}}\subset{\mathfrak{a}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},\ell}
, where the first inclusion is strict.
The quotient

{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}/{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},U_{0}}
is isomorphic to the direct sum of
{\mathfrak{s}o}(U_{0})
and
U_{0}\otimes U_{0}^{\perp}
.
If
{\mathfrak{a}}
contains
U_{0}\otimes U_{0}^{\perp}
then it is the whole of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
, since

{\mathfrak{s}o}(U_{0})
is contained
11
11

11

Choose and orthogonal basis
\{e_{i}\}
of
\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
with
e_{1},e_{2}\in U_{0}
. Let

\{e_{i}^{*}\}
be the dual basis. Set
A=(e_{1},e_{1})e_{1}^{*}\otimes e_{3}-(e_{3},e_{3})e_{3}^{*}\otimes e_{1}
,

B=(e_{2},e_{2})e_{2}^{*}\otimes e_{3}-(e_{3},e_{3})e_{3}^{*}\otimes e_{2}
, and

C=(e_{1},e_{1})e_{1}^{*}\otimes e_{2}-(e_{2},e_{2})e_{2}^{*}\otimes e_{1}
. Then
A
and
B
belong to the submodule of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
isomorphic to

U_{0}\otimes U_{0}^{\perp}
,
C
spans
{\mathfrak{s}o}(U_{0})
, and

[A,B]=(e_{3},e_{3})C
.

in the Lie bracket of
U_{0}\otimes U_{0}^{\perp}
with itself.
Hence,
{\mathfrak{a}}
intersects
U_{0}\otimes U_{0}^{\perp}
either trivially, or along

\ell_{0}\otimes U_{0}^{\perp}
, for some line
\ell_{0}\subset U_{0}
.
If the intersection is trivial, then
{\mathfrak{a}}={\mathfrak{s}o}(U_{0})\times{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},U_{0}}
, which is impossible, since

{\mathfrak{s}o}(U_{0})
is not contained in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},\ell}
. Hence, the intersection is

\ell_{0}\otimes U_{0}^{\perp}
, for some line
\ell_{0}\subset U_{0}
.
Finally,
\ell_{0}
must be orthogonal to
\mu(\ell)
, since
{\mathfrak{a}}
is contained in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},\ell}
.
∎

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
K
be a subfield of
{\mathbb{C}}
,
let
\ell
be a line in
\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
, and let
j
a positive integer. If the line
\ell^{j}
in
{\rm Sym}^{j}\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}})

is defined over
K
, then so does
\ell
.

Proof.

The Veronese embedding
\varphi:{\mathbb{P}}(\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}}))\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}({\rm Sym}^{j}\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}}))
,
\ell\mapsto\ell^{j}
, is defined over
{\mathbb{Q}}
and is thus equivariant with respect to every automorphism
g
of
{\mathbb{C}}
.
Assume that
\ell^{j}
is defined over
K
.
The injectivity of
\varphi
implies that
\ell
is invariant with respect to every automorphism of
{\mathbb{C}}
leaving
K
invariant.
Hence,
\ell
is defined over
K
.
∎

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
f
be a non-zero element of the kernel of
\Delta:{\rm Sym}^{n}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))\rightarrow{\rm Sym}^{n-2}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))
of Hodge type
(0,0)
.
Let
\Sigma
be a subspace of
HT^{2}(X)
of co-dimension
1
.
Assume that
m(\Sigma)
is contained in the subalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},f}
annihilating
f
. Then the line
\ell(\Sigma)\subset\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
, given in (
6.13
), is defined over
{\mathbb{Q}}
, is contained in
\tilde{H}^{0,0}(X)
, and

f
spans the image of the projection of
\ell(\Sigma)^{n}
to
\ker(\Delta)
. Furthermore,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},f}={\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},\ell(\Sigma)}
.

Proof.

The element
f
has the form

{\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(\sigma\bar{\sigma})^{i}f_{i},}

where
f_{i}
belongs to
{\rm Sym}^{n-2i}(\tilde{H}^{0,0}(X))
, since
f
has Hodge type
(0,0)
.

Step 1:
We derive a system (
6.16
) and (
6.17
)
of partial differential equations that the
f_{i}
satisfy.
Let
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{N}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
\tilde{H}^{0,0}(X)
.
We have

\Delta\left((\sigma\bar{\sigma})^{i}f_{i}\right)=i^{2}(\sigma\bar{\sigma})^{i-1}f_{i}+(\sigma\bar{\sigma})^{i}\Delta(f_{i}).

Hence,

(6.16)

\Delta(f_{i-1})+i^{2}f_{i}=0,

for
1\leq i\leq\lfloor n/2\rfloor
,
since
f
belongs to the kernel of
\Delta
.
Given
\lambda\in HT^{2}(X)
, we have
m_{\lambda}(x_{i})=-(\mu(\lambda),x_{i})\bar{\sigma}
, by Equation (
6.14
). Thus,

m_{\lambda}\left((\sigma\bar{\sigma})^{i}x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{n-2i}}\right)=i\mu(\lambda)\sigma^{i-1}\bar{\sigma}^{i}x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{n-2i}}-\sigma^{i}\bar{\sigma}^{i+1}\sum_{j=1}^{n-2i}(\mu(\lambda),x_{i_{j}})x_{i_{1}}\cdots\hat{x}_{i_{j}}\cdots x_{i_{n-2i}}.

Let
\mu(\lambda)^{*}
be the element of
\tilde{H}^{0,0}(X)^{*}
satisfying
\mu(\lambda)^{*}(\lambda^{\prime})=(\mu(\lambda),\lambda^{\prime})
, for all
\lambda^{\prime}\in\tilde{H}^{0,0}(X)
. Then

m_{\lambda}((\sigma\bar{\sigma})^{i}f_{i})=i\mu(\lambda)\sigma^{i-1}\bar{\sigma}^{i}f_{i}-\sigma^{i}\bar{\sigma}^{i+1}[f_{i}\lrcorner\mu(\lambda)^{*}].

m_{\lambda}(f)=\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1}\left(i\mu(\lambda)f_{i}-[f_{i-1}\lrcorner\mu(\lambda)^{*}]\right)\sigma^{i-1}\bar{\sigma}^{i}=0,

for all
\lambda\in\Sigma
, where we set
f_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1}=0
.
Hence, for all
\lambda\in\Sigma
and
0<i\leq\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1
we have

(6.17)

i\mu(\lambda)f_{i}-[f_{i-1}\lrcorner\mu(\lambda)^{*}]=0.

So

ix_{j}f_{i}=\frac{\partial f_{i-1}}{\partial x_{j}},

if
x_{j}
belongs to
\mu(\Sigma)
.

Step 2:
We prove that
\ell(\Sigma)
is defined over
{\mathbb{R}}
.
If
n
is odd, then
f_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
is linear and Equation (
6.17
) for
i=1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor
implies that
f_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
is orthogonal to
\mu(\Sigma)
and so belongs to
\ell(\Sigma)
. Note that
f_{i}
is real, for all
i
, since
f
is rational.
If
f_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
does not vanish, then
\ell(\Sigma)
is defined over
{\mathbb{R}}
.
If
f_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
does vanish, let
j
be the maximal index, such that
f_{j}\neq 0
, but
f_{j+1}=0
.
Equation (
6.17
) for
i=j+1
implies that
f_{j}
belongs to
\ell(\Sigma)^{n-2j}
, which is thus defined over
{\mathbb{R}}
, and again we get that

\ell(\Sigma)
is defined over
{\mathbb{R}}
, by Lemma
6.8
.

If
n
is even, then
f_{n/2}
is constant. If it vanishes, then the above argument proves that
\ell(\Sigma)
is defined over
{\mathbb{R}}
. Assume that
f_{n/2}=t
, where
t\neq 0
. Set
g:=f_{n/2-1}
. Assume first that
\ell(\Sigma)
is not isotropic. We can then assume that
x_{1}
belongs to
\ell(\Sigma)
.
Equation (
6.17
) for
i={n/2}
implies that

\frac{\partial g}{\partial x_{j}}=\frac{nt}{2}x_{j}
, for
j\geq 2
. So
g=\frac{nt}{4}(x_{2}^{2}+\ \cdots\ +x_{N}^{2})+cx_{1}^{2}
.
Equation (
6.16
) yields
-\frac{tn^{2}}{4}=\Delta(g)=\frac{nt(N-1)}{4}+c
, so

c=\frac{-tn(n+N-1)}{4}
.
We get

f_{n/2-1}=\frac{nt}{4}(x_{1}^{2}+\ \cdots\ +x_{N}^{2})-\left(\frac{tn(N+n)}{4}\right)x_{1}^{2}.

Now,
t
is real, as is
f_{n/2-1}
. The element
x_{1}^{2}+\ \cdots\ +x_{N}^{2}
is independent of the choice of an orthonormal basis and is thus real. The coefficient
\frac{tn(N+n)}{4}
is a non-zero real number. Hence,
x_{1}^{2}
is real and the line

\ell(\Sigma)
is defined over
{\mathbb{R}}
.

Assume that
n
is even and
\ell(\Sigma)
is isotropic. We claim that
f_{n/2}=0.

Set
t:=f_{n/2}
and
g:=f_{n/2-1}
. We may assume that
\ell(\Sigma)
is spanned by
x_{1}+\sqrt{-1}x_{2}
. Then

x_{1}+\sqrt{-1}x_{2}
belongs to
\mu(\Sigma)
.
Equation (
6.17
) for
i={n/2}
implies that

\frac{\partial g}{\partial x_{j}}=\frac{nt}{2}x_{j}
, for
j\geq 3
and

(n/2)(x_{1}+\sqrt{-1}x_{2})t=g\lrcorner(x_{1}+\sqrt{-1}x_{2})^{*}

So

f_{n/2-1}=\frac{nt}{4}(x_{3}^{2}+\ \cdots\ +x_{N}^{2})+\frac{nt}{4}(x_{1}+\sqrt{-1}x_{2})(x_{1}-\sqrt{-1}x_{2})+a(x_{1}+\sqrt{-1}x_{2})^{2},

for some scalar
a
. Equation (
6.16
) yields

-\frac{tn^{2}}{4}=\Delta(f_{n/2-1})=\frac{nt}{4}(N-2)+\frac{nt}{2}=\frac{nNt}{4}.

Hence,
t=0
as claimed. We conclude once again that
\ell(\Sigma)
is defined over
{\mathbb{R}}
.

Step 3:
The subalgebra
{\mathfrak{a}}
of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
generated by
m(\Sigma)
and its complex conjugate

\overline{m(\Sigma)}
is equal to the subalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},\ell(\Sigma)}
of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
annihilating
\ell(\Sigma)
,
by Lemma
6.7
. The subalgebra
\overline{m(\Sigma)}

is contained in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},f}
, since
f
is rational and
m(\Sigma)
is contained in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},f}
. Hence,

{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},\ell(\Sigma)}
is contained in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},f}
.

Let
\lambda
be a non-zero element of
\ell(\Sigma)
.
The trivial
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},\ell(\Sigma)}
-submodule in
{\rm Sym}^{n}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}}))
has the basis

\{\lambda^{n-2i}\tilde{q}^{i}\}_{i=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
, where
\tilde{q}
is the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
-invariant degree
2

element given in Example
5.2
.

\Delta:{\rm Sym}^{n}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}}))\rightarrow{\rm Sym}^{n-2}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}}))
is a surjective
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
-modules homomorphism, hence it induces a surjective homomorphism of the respective trivial
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},\ell(\Sigma)}
-submodules.
We conclude that the trivial
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},\ell(\Sigma)}
-submodule
\ker(\Delta)^{{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},\ell(\Sigma)}}

of
\ker(\Delta)
is one-dimensional.
If
\ell(\Sigma)
is isotropic, then
\lambda^{n}
spans
\ker(\Delta)^{{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},\ell(\Sigma)}}
,
and so
f
is a scalar multiple of
\lambda^{n}
. We conclude that
\ell(\Sigma)^{n}
is defined over
{\mathbb{Q}}
, hence so is
\ell(\Sigma)
, by Lemma
6.8
.

If
\ell(\Sigma)
is non-isotropic, then the projection of
\lambda^{n}
to
\ker(\Delta)
spans

\ker(\Delta)^{{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},\ell(\Sigma)}}
, and so
f
belongs to the projection of
\ell(\Sigma)^{n}.

Hence,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},f}
is contained in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},\ell(\Sigma)}
.
The equality
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},\ell(\Sigma)}={\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},f}
follows, regardless of whether
\ell(\Sigma)
is isotropic.

It remains to prove that
\ell(\Sigma)
is defined over
{\mathbb{Q}}
when
\ell(\Sigma)
is non-isotropic.
The quadric
Q
in
{\mathbb{P}}(\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}}))
of isotropic lines is defined over
{\mathbb{Q}}
and the morphism

{\mathbb{P}}(\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}}))\setminus Q\rightarrow Gr\left(\left(\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}N+1\\
2\end{array}\!\!\right),{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}\right),

sending
\ell
to
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},\ell}
, is defined over
{\mathbb{Q}}
(and is thus equivariant with respect to the group of field automorphisms of
{\mathbb{C}}
, and is injective. Hence, if
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},\ell}
is defined over
{\mathbb{Q}}
, then so is
\ell
, as both are invariant under all field automorphisms of
{\mathbb{C}}
. We already know that

{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},\ell(\Sigma)}={\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},f}
is defined over
{\mathbb{Q}}
. Hence, so it
\ell(\Sigma)
.
∎

6.3.
The LLV subspace of an object in
D^{b}(X)

The
Mukai vector
of an object
F\in D^{b}(X)
is the class
v(F):=ch(F)\sqrt{td_{X}}
in
H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
.
Let
{\mathfrak{g}}_{v(F)}
be the subalgebra of
{\mathfrak{g}}
annihilating
v(F)
.
Let
{\rm obs}^{HT}_{F}
be the obstruction map (
3.1
) and set
\Sigma(F):=\ker\left({\rm obs}^{HT}_{F}\right)
.
Let
\tilde{\Sigma}(F)
be the image of the subspace
\Sigma(F)
of
HT^{2}(X)
via the graded Duflo isomorphism

D:HT^{*}(X)\rightarrow HT^{*}(X)
, given by contraction with
\sqrt{td_{X}}
.

\tilde{\Sigma}(F):=D(\Sigma(F))=D\left(\ker\left({\rm obs}^{HT}_{F}\right)\right).

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
X
be a projective irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifold and
F\in D^{b}(X)
an object.
The image
m(\tilde{\Sigma}(F))
of the subspace
\tilde{\Sigma}(F)
of
HT^{2}(X)
via (
6.9
) is contained in the complexification of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{v(F)}
.

Proof.

The image
m(\tilde{\Sigma}(F))
is contained in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
, by Corollary
6.2
. We prove that it annihilates
v(F)
in two steps.

Step 1:
Let
\Delta:X\rightarrow X\times X
be the diagonal embedding.
Then
HH^{i}(X):={\rm Hom}(\Delta_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X},\Delta_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[i])
and

HH_{i}(X):={\rm Hom}(\Delta_{!}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}[i],\Delta_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
, where
\Delta_{!}
is the left adjoint of
\Delta^{*}
.
We have
\Delta_{!}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\cong\Delta_{*}\omega_{X}^{-1}[-2n]
, where
\dim(X)=2n
.
Hence,
HH_{0}(X)={\rm Hom}(\Delta_{*}\omega_{X}^{-1}[-2n],\Delta_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
.
Elements of
HH^{i}(X)
yield natural transformations from the identity functor
id
of
D^{b}(X)
to
id[i]
. We get the homomorphism

{\rm obs}_{F}:HH^{2}(X)\rightarrow{\rm Hom}(F,F[2])

sending
\alpha
to the value
\alpha_{F}
of the natural transformation on the object
F
.
The Chern character
\widetilde{ch}(F)
is defined as an element of
HH_{0}(X)

[
Ca2
, Def. 6.2]
.
We prove first that the kernel
\Sigma^{\prime}(F)
of
{\rm obs}_{F}
is contained in the ideal of
HH^{*}(X)
annihilating
\widetilde{ch}(F)
.

Given a smooth complex projective variety
Y
and an object
E
of
D^{b}(Y)
, let
Let
Tr_{Y}:{\rm Hom}(E,E\otimes\omega_{Y}[\dim Y])\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
be the Serre’s Duality trace (see
[
Ca2
, Sec. 2]
).
The Chern character
\widetilde{ch}(F)
satisfies

Tr_{X\times X}(\widetilde{ch}(F)\circ\nu)=Tr_{X}(\nu_{F}),

for all
\nu\in{\rm Hom}(\Delta_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X},\Delta_{*}\omega_{X}[2n])
, where
\nu_{F}\in{\rm Hom}(F,F\otimes\omega_{X}[2n])

is the value on
F
of the natural transformation
\nu
from
id
to the Serre functor of tensorization by
\omega_{X}[2n]

[
Ca2
, Def. 6.2]
. In particular, for every
\alpha\in HH^{2}(X)
and
\beta\in{\rm Hom}(\Delta_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X},\Delta_{*}\omega_{X}[2n-2])
, setting
\nu=\alpha\circ\beta
above we get

Tr_{X\times X}([\widetilde{ch}(F)\circ\alpha]\circ\beta)=Tr_{X}(\alpha_{F}\circ\beta_{F}).

If
\alpha_{F}=0
, then the element
\widetilde{ch}(F)\circ\alpha
of
HH_{-2}(X)={\rm Hom}(\Delta_{*}\omega_{X}^{-1}[-2-2n],\Delta_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
pairs to zero with every class
\beta
as above. Hence,
\widetilde{ch}(F)\circ\alpha=0,

because the trace pairing is non-degenerate.

Step 2:

Consider the Hochschild-Kostant-Rosenberg isomorphisms

\displaystyle I^{HKR}:HT^{*}(X)

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle HH^{*}(X),

\displaystyle I_{HKR}:HH_{*}(X)

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle H\Omega_{*}(X).

Set
I^{K}:=I^{HKR}\circ D^{-1}
and let
I_{K}
be given by
I_{K}(\alpha)=I_{HKR}(\alpha)\sqrt{td_{X}}
.
We have
I_{K}(\widetilde{ch}(F))=ch(F)\sqrt{td_{X}}=v(F)
, by
[
Ca3
, Theorem 4.5]
,
and

I_{K}(I^{K}(\alpha)\circ\widetilde{ch}(F))=\alpha\cdot I_{K}(\widetilde{ch}(F))
, for all
\alpha\in HT^{*}(X)
, by
[
CBR
, Theorem 1.4]
.
Hence, the image via
(I^{K})^{-1}
of
the kernel
\Sigma^{\prime}(F)
of
{\rm obs}_{F}
annihilates
v(F)
, by Step 1 of the proof.

(6.18)

m\left((I^{K})^{-1}(\Sigma^{\prime}(F))\right)\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},v(F)}.

We have
{\rm obs}_{F}(I^{HKR}(\alpha))=(\exp a_{F})(\alpha)
, for all
\alpha\in HT^{2}(X)
, by
[
Hua
, Theorem A]
.
Hence,
I^{HKR}
maps
the kernel
\Sigma(F)
of the obstruction map
{\rm obs}^{HT}_{F}
, given in (
3.1
), to
the kernel
\Sigma^{\prime}(F)
of
{\rm obs}_{F}
. So
(I^{K})^{-1}(\Sigma^{\prime}(F))=D(\Sigma(F))=\tilde{\Sigma}(F)
.
We conclude that
m(\tilde{\Sigma}(F))
annihilates
v(F)
, by Equation (
6.18
).
∎

Let
X
be a
2n
-dimensional irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifold. Composing the homomorphism
m:HT^{2}(X)\rightarrow{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
, given in Equation (
6.9
), with the inclusion

{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}\subset{\rm End}(H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{C}}))
followed by evaluation at
v(F)
we get the
cohomological obstruction map

{\rm obs}_{F}^{Coh}:HT^{2}(X)\rightarrow H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{C}}).

Definition 6.11
.

(1)

We say that an object
F
in
D^{b}(X)

deforms in co-dimension
c
, if the kernel
\Sigma(F)
of the obstruction map (
3.1
) has co-dimension
c
in
HT^{2}(X)
.

(2)

We say that
F

has a rank
c
cohomological obstruction map
, if
{\rm rank}({\rm obs}_{F}^{Coh})=c
, i.e., if
the annihilator
{\mathfrak{g}}_{v(F)}
of the Mukai vector
v(F)
intersects
m(HT^{2}(X))
in a co-dimension
c
subspace of the latter.

(3)

If
F
is
\theta
-twisted of non-zero rank, we say that

F

has a rank
c
cohomological obstruction map

if the assumption in Part (
2
) holds with
v(F)
replaced by

\bar{v}(F):=\kappa(F)\sqrt{td_{X}}
.

If
F
deforms in co-dimension
c
, then
F
has a rank
\leq c
cohomological obstruction map,
since
m(\tilde{\Sigma}(F))
is contained in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{v(F)}

by Lemma
6.10
.
Important examples of very modular sheaves, which deform in co-dimension
2
, are constructed in
[
Ma4
]
in the
K3^{[n]}
deformation type and in
[
Ma9
]
in the generalized Kummer deformation type.

Let
k
be a positive integer. If
F_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq k
, each deforms in co-dimension one and each has positive rank, then
\otimes_{i=1}^{k}F_{i}
deforms in co-dimension
\leq 2
. Indeed, in this case
\Sigma(F_{i})
does not contain
H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
, as was explained in the proof of
Proposition
3.3
,
and so the intersection
\Sigma(F_{i})\cap[H^{1}(TX)\oplus H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})]

is the graph of a linear homomorphism
\theta_{i}:H^{1}(TX)\rightarrow H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})

and
F_{i}
deforms in the direction
\xi+\theta_{i}(\xi)
for all
\xi\in H^{1}(TX)
. Then
\otimes_{i=1}^{k}F_{i}
deforms in the direction

\xi+(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\theta_{i})(\xi)
, for each
\xi\in H^{1}(TX)
. So
\Sigma(\otimes_{i=1}^{k}F_{i})\cap[H^{1}(TX)\oplus H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})]
contains the graph of
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\theta_{i}
.
The linear homomorphism
\theta_{i}
is computed in Lemma
6.22
below.

Definition 6.12
.

The
LLV subspace
of an object
F
in
D^{b}(X)
, is the subspace of
\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
given by

W(F):=\mu(\tilde{\Sigma}(F)^{\perp})

generalizing Equation (
6.13
).

Note that
F
deforms in co-dimension
\dim(W(F))
.
If one-dimensional, the LLV subspace
W(\otimes_{i=1}^{k}F_{i})
of the tensor product discussed above is described by
Theorem
6.13
(
3
), while if it is two dimensional it is computed in Lemma
6.22
.
Given a subspace
W\subset\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
, let
{\mathfrak{g}}_{W}
be the subalgebra of
{\mathfrak{g}}
annihilating
W
.
If
F
has a rank
1
cohomological obstruction map,
and the projection
\hat{v}(F)
of
v(F)
to
SH^{*}(X)
does not vanish,
then
{\mathfrak{g}}_{v(F)}={\mathfrak{g}}_{\ell(F)}
, for a rational line
\ell(F)
in

\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
, by Lemma
6.9
applied with
f=\Psi(\hat{v}(F))
. The equality
\ell(F)=W(F)
holds, if furthermore
F
deforms in co-dimension
1
.

6.4.
The LLV line

Theorem 6.13
.

Let
X
be a projective irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifold and
F\in D^{b}(X)
