an object which deforms in co-dimension
1
, so that
\tilde{\Sigma}(F)
has co-dimension one in
HT^{2}(X)
.
Denote by
\ell(F)
the line
\ell(\tilde{\Sigma}(F))
in
\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
given in (
6.13
).
Assume
12
12

12

The assumption holds if
{\rm rank}(F)\neq 0
, or
c_{1}(F)\neq 0
. The assumption depends only on the orbit of
v(F)
under the derived monodromy group.

that the projection
\hat{v}(F)
of
v(F)
to
SH^{*}(X)
via (
5.8
) does not vanish.

(1)

The line
\ell(F)
is defined over
{\mathbb{Q}}
and

{\mathfrak{g}}_{v(F)}
is equal to the Lie subalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\ell(F)}
annihilating
\ell(F)
.

(2)

The embedding

\Psi
, given in (
5.4
), maps
\hat{v}(F)

into the image of
\ell(F)^{n}
via the projection (
5.7
).

(3)

If the rank
r
of
F
does not vanish, then
the line
\ell(F)
is spanned by an element of the form
r\alpha+c_{1}(F)+s(F)\beta
,
for some
s(F)\in{\mathbb{Q}}
.

(4)

Given an equivalence
\Phi:D^{b}(X)\rightarrow D^{b}(Y)
, let
\tilde{H}(\phi):\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow\tilde{H}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})
be its associated isometry via the functor (
5.1
).
We have

\ell(\Phi(F))=\tilde{H}(\phi)(\ell(F))
.

Proof.

Part
(
1
)
The inclusion

{\mathfrak{g}}_{\ell(F)}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{v(F)}

follows from the assumption that
\Sigma(F)
has co-dimension one in
HT^{2}(X)
, Lemma
6.7
, and Lemma
6.10
.
The equality
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\ell(F)}={\mathfrak{g}}_{\hat{v}(F)}
and the fact that
\ell(F)
is defined over
{\mathbb{Q}}
both follow from
Lemma
6.9
, by letting
f
be
\Psi(\hat{v}(F))
.
We conclude that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\ell(F)}={\mathfrak{g}}_{v(F)}
from the evident inclusion
{\mathfrak{g}}_{v(F)}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{\hat{v}(F)}
.

Part (
2
)
follows from Lemma
6.9
, by letting
f
be
\Psi(\hat{v}(F))
.

(
3
)
The class
\kappa(F)
is
\bar{{\mathfrak{g}}}
-invariant, by Proposition
3.3
.
Hence, so is
\kappa(F)\sqrt{td_{X}}
.
Set
g:=\exp(e_{-c_{1}(F)/r})\in SO(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))
. Then
g
acts on
H^{ev}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
, via (
6.7
), and
\kappa(F)\sqrt{td_{X}}=g(v(G)),
by Theorem
2.2
.
The map
F\mapsto\ell(F)
factors through
v
and the map
v(F)\mapsto\ell(F)
extends to an
SO(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}}))
-equivariant map from the
SO(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}}))
-orbit of
v(F)
to the
SO(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{C}}))
-orbit of
\ell(F)
,
since the embedding
\Psi
, the two projections
(
5.7
) and
(
5.8
), and the
n
-power map
\ell(F)\mapsto\ell(F)^{n}
, are all

SO(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))
-equivariant.
Hence,
g(\ell(F))
is
\bar{{\mathfrak{g}}}
-invariant. So

g(\ell(F))
is contained in
U_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Let
w
be a non-zero vector in
\ell(F)
.
Write
w=r_{0}\alpha+\lambda+s\beta\in\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
, with
r_{0},s\in{\mathbb{Q}}
, and
\lambda\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
.
Note that
r_{0}\neq 0
. Indeed,
\exp(e_{\lambda^{\prime}})(v(F))

is not
\bar{{\mathfrak{g}}}
-invariant, for
\lambda^{\prime}\neq-c_{1}(F)/r
,
\lambda^{\prime}\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
, and so
neither is
\exp(e_{\lambda^{\prime}})(\ell(F))
,
by the equivariance of the map
v(F)\mapsto\ell(F)
. But if
r_{0}=0
, then
\ell(F)
is
\bar{{\mathfrak{g}}}
-invariant, if and only if

\exp(e_{\lambda^{\prime}})(\ell(F))
is, for all
\lambda^{\prime}\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
.
Now,

\exp(e_{-\lambda^{\prime}/r^{\prime}})w=r_{0}\alpha+\left(\lambda-\frac{r_{0}}{r^{\prime}}\lambda^{\prime}\right)+\left(s-\frac{(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})}{r^{\prime}}+\frac{r_{0}(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})}{2(r^{\prime})^{2}}\right)\beta

belongs to
U_{\mathbb{Q}}
if and only if
\lambda^{\prime}/r^{\prime}=\lambda/r_{0}
. We know that

\exp(e_{-c_{1}(F)/r})w
belongs to
U_{\mathbb{Q}}
. Hence,
\lambda/r_{0}=c_{1}(F)/r
and
(r/r_{0})w
has the stated form.

(
4
) Let
\phi:H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow H^{*}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})
be the isomorphism associated to
\Phi
.
Then
\phi(v(F))=v(\Phi(F))
and so
\phi(\hat{v}(F))=\hat{v}(\Phi(F))
.
Theorem
5.3
yields the commutative diagram

\textstyle{SH^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon(\tilde{H}(\phi))\phi}
\textstyle{SH^{*}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{Y}}
\textstyle{\ker(\Delta_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Sym}^{n}(\tilde{H}(\phi))}
\textstyle{\ker(\Delta_{Y}),}

where
\Delta_{X}:{\rm Sym}^{n}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))\rightarrow{\rm Sym}^{n-2}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))
is given in (
5.6
) and
\Delta_{Y}
is its analogue.
Hence,
{\rm Sym}^{n}(\tilde{H}(\phi))
maps
\Psi(\hat{v}(F))
to
\Psi(\hat{v}(\Phi(F)))
.
This means that
{\rm Sym}^{n}(\tilde{H}(\phi))
maps the projection of
\ell(F)^{n}
in
\ker(\Delta_{X})
to the projection of
\ell(F)^{n}

in
\ker(\Delta_{Y})
. The map
sending
\ell(F)
to the projection of
\ell(F)^{n}
, is injective, by the equality
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},\hat{v}(F)}={\mathfrak{g}}_{{\mathbb{C}},\ell(F)}
in Lemma

6.9
. Hence,
\tilde{H}(\phi)(\ell(F))=\ell(\Phi(F))
.

∎

Remark 6.14
.

Assume that the rank of
F
is non-zero and
X
is of
K3^{[2]}
or
K3^{[3]}
-type.
Then
v(F)
is determined by its rank and the line
\ell(F)
, by parts
(
2
) and (
3
) of
Theorem
6.13
, since
\hat{v}(F)=v(F)
in this case.
If
X
is of
K3^{[2]}
-type the equality
\hat{v}(F)=v(F)
follows from the equality
H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})=SH^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
.
When
X
is of
K3^{[3]}
-type,
H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
decomposes as the direct sum of two irreducible
{\mathfrak{g}}
-module

H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\cong SH^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\oplus\wedge^{2}\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
, by
[
GKLR
, Cor. 3.2]
.
The projection of
v(F)
to
\wedge^{2}\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
must vanish, since the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\ell}
-invariant subspace
of the latter vanishes. Hence,
v(F)
is contained in
SH^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
and again we get the equality
\hat{v}(F)=v(F)
.

Let
\tilde{D}:\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
be given by
\tilde{D}(r\alpha+\lambda+s\beta)=r\alpha-\lambda+s\beta
.
Let
D:H^{ev}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow H^{ev}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
be the automorphism of the even cohomology ring acting on

H^{2i}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
by
(-1)^{i}
.
If
\Sigma(F)
has codimension one in
HT^{2}(X)
, then so does the subspace
\Sigma(F^{\vee})
of the dual object
F^{\vee}
.

Lemma 6.15
.

\ell(F^{\vee})=\tilde{D}(\ell(F))
.

Proof.

Let
\Pi:{\rm Sym}^{n}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))\rightarrow\ker(\Delta)
be the projection (
5.7
).
Then
\Pi
commutes with
{\rm Sym}^{n}(\tilde{D})
. The projection (
5.8
) commutes with
D
.
The isometry
\Psi:SH^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow\ker(\Delta)
, given in (
5.4
),
satisfies
\Psi(D(\theta))={\rm Sym}^{n}(\tilde{D})(\Psi(\theta))
, for all
\theta\in SH^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
.
The
n
-th power map satisfies
{\rm Sym}^{n}(\tilde{D})(\lambda^{n})=(\tilde{D}(\lambda))^{n}
, for all

\lambda\in\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
. Let
\lambda
be a non-zero vector in
\ell(F)
satisfying

\Psi(\hat{v}(F))=\Pi(\lambda^{n})
. Then

\Psi(\hat{v}(F^{\vee}))=\Psi(D(\hat{v}(F)))={\rm Sym}^{n}(\tilde{D})(\Psi(\hat{v}(F)))={\rm Sym}^{n}(\tilde{D})(\Pi(\lambda^{n}))=\Pi(\tilde{D}(\lambda)^{n}).

Hence,
\tilde{D}(\lambda)
spans
\ell(F^{\vee})
.
∎

Example 6.16
.

Let
X
be a
2n
-dimensional irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifold.
Given a closed point
z\in X
we have
\ell({\mathcal{O}}_{z})={\rm span}_{\mathbb{Q}}\{\beta\}.

Indeed,
v({\mathcal{O}}_{z})=[pt]
belongs to
SH^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
and
\Psi(1)=\beta^{n}/c_{X}
, by (
5.5
).

We will need the following
multiplication table in the cohomology of
X
of
K3^{[2]}
type, where
\alpha
is a class in
H^{2}(X)
and
c_{2}
is
c_{2}(TX)
.

(6.19)

\left[\begin{array}[]{cccccc}1&\alpha&\alpha^{2}&c_{2}&c_{2}\alpha&[\mbox{pt}]\\
\alpha&\alpha^{2}&\frac{(\alpha,\alpha)}{10}c_{2}\alpha&c_{2}\alpha&30(\alpha,\alpha)[\mbox{pt}]&0\\
\alpha^{2}&\frac{(\alpha,\alpha)}{10}c_{2}\alpha&3(\alpha,\alpha)^{2}[\mbox{pt}]&30(\alpha,\alpha)[\mbox{pt}]&0&0\\
c_{2}&c_{2}\alpha&30(\alpha,\alpha)[\mbox{pt}]&828[\mbox{pt}]&0&0\\
c_{2}\alpha&30(\alpha,\alpha)[\mbox{pt}]&0&0&0&0\\
\mbox{[pt]}&0&0&0&0&0\end{array}\right]

Given classes
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\gamma
,
\delta
in
H^{2}(X)
, we have

\alpha\beta\gamma\delta=[(\alpha,\beta)(\gamma,\delta)+(\alpha,\gamma)(\beta,\delta)+(\alpha,\delta)(\beta,\gamma)][pt].

Example 6.17
.

Assume
X
is of
K3^{[2]}
-type. We claim that

\ell({\mathcal{O}}_{X})={\rm span}_{\mathbb{Q}}\{4\alpha+5\beta\}.

We have
v({\mathcal{O}}_{X})=\sqrt{td_{X}}=1+\frac{1}{24}c_{2}(X)+\frac{25}{32}[pt].

Set

(6.20)

b_{X}:=\frac{1}{23}\sum_{1}^{23}y_{i}^{2}/(y_{i},y_{i}),

where
\{y_{i}\}
is an orthogonal basis of
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
.
We have
\int_{X}y_{i}^{4}=3(y_{i},y_{i})^{2}
and for
i\neq j
we have
\int_{X}y_{i}^{2}y_{j}^{2}=(y_{i},y_{i})(y_{j},y_{j})
, due to the vanishing of
(y_{i},y_{j})
. Hence,

\displaystyle\int_{X}b_{X}^{2}=\frac{1}{23^{2}}\left(\sum_{i<j}\frac{2}{(y_{i},y_{i})(y_{j},y_{j})}\int_{X}y_{i}^{2}y_{j}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{23}\frac{1}{(y_{i},y_{i})^{2}}\int_{X}y_{i}^{4}\right)=\frac{25}{23},

\displaystyle\int_{X}b_{X}y_{i}^{2}=\frac{1}{23(y_{i},y_{i})}\int_{X}y_{i}^{4}+\frac{1}{23}\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{23}\frac{1}{(y_{j},y_{j})}\int_{X}y_{i}^{2}y_{j}^{2}=\frac{25}{23}(y_{i},y_{i})=\frac{5}{23\cdot 6}\int_{X}c_{2}(X)y_{i}^{2}.

It follows that

(6.21)

c_{2}(X)=\frac{23\cdot 6}{5}b_{X},

as both span the one-dimensional space of
O(H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))
-invariant classes.
We have

(6.22)

\Psi(1)=\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2},\ \Psi([pt])=\beta^{2},\ \mbox{and}\ \Psi(b_{X})=\tilde{b}_{X}+\alpha\beta,

where
\tilde{b}_{X}
denotes the element of
{\rm Sym}^{2}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))
given by the right hand side of Equation (
6.20
).
Hence,

(6.23)

\displaystyle\Psi(c_{2}(TX))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 30(\tilde{q}+\alpha\beta),

(6.24)

\displaystyle\Psi(\sqrt{td_{X}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[\alpha^{2}+\frac{5}{2}(\tilde{q}+\alpha\beta)+\frac{25}{16}\beta^{2}\right]=\frac{1}{2}\left[(\alpha+\frac{5}{4}\beta)^{2}+\frac{5}{2}\tilde{q}\right],

where
\tilde{q}
is defined in Example
5.2
and we used the equality

(6.25)

\tilde{q}=\frac{1}{25}[23\tilde{b}_{X}-2\alpha\beta].

The right hand side of the latter displayed equation is the projection of
\frac{1}{2}(\alpha+\frac{5}{4}\beta)^{2}
from
{\rm Sym}^{2}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))

to the image of
\Psi
, by Example
5.2
. So

\ell({\mathcal{O}}_{X})={\rm span}_{\mathbb{Q}}\{4\alpha+5\beta\}
as claimed.

More generally, we have the following.

Lemma 6.18
.

Let
X
be a
2n
-dimensional irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifold. There exists a rational number
t
, such that

t^{n}=\frac{n!}{c_{X}}\int_{X}\sqrt{td_{X}}
and

\ell({\mathcal{O}}_{X})={\rm span}_{\mathbb{Q}}\{\alpha+t\beta\}.

If
X
is of
K3^{[n]}
-type,
n\geq 2
, then
\ell({\mathcal{O}}_{X})={\rm span}_{\mathbb{Q}}\{4\alpha+(n+3)\beta\}
.
If
X
is deformation equivalent to a
2n
-dimensional generalized Kummer,
n\geq 2
, then
\ell({\mathcal{O}}_{X})={\rm span}_{\mathbb{Q}}\{4\alpha+(n+1)\beta\}
.

Proof.

Let
\tau_{2i}
be the graded summand of
\sqrt{td_{X}}
in
H^{4i}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
. So
\tau_{0}=1
and
\tau_{2n}=s[pt]
for
s:=\int_{X}\sqrt{td_{X}}
.
It is known that
s>0
, by
[
HSa
, Sec. 6]
.
If
X
is of
K3^{[n]}
-type, then
s=\frac{(n+3)^{n}}{4^{n}n!}
, by
[
Sa
, Prop. 19]
.
If
X
is deformation equivalent to a
2n
-dimensional generalized Kummer, then

s=\frac{(n+1)^{n+1}}{4^{n}(n!)}
,
by
[
Sa
, Prop. 21]
.
Choose a basis of
\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
consisting of
\alpha
,
\beta
, and a basis of
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
.
We get a well defined notion of the degree
\deg_{\alpha}(f)
of an element of
{\rm Sym}^{n}\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
in
\alpha
. Define

\deg_{\beta}(f)
similarly.
Let
\bar{\tau}_{2i}
be the projection of
\tau_{2i}
to
SH^{*}(X)
. Note that
\bar{\tau}_{0}=\tau_{0}=1
and

\bar{\tau}_{2n}=\tau_{2n}=s[pt]
.
Then
\Psi(\bar{\tau}_{0})=\alpha^{n}/n!
,

\Psi(\bar{\tau}_{2n})=s\beta^{n}/c_{X}
, and for
1\leq i\leq n-1
we have
\deg_{\alpha}(\Psi(\bar{\tau}_{2i}))<n

and
\deg_{\beta}(\Psi(\bar{\tau}_{2i}))<n
, by definition of
\Psi
. We know that

\ell({\mathcal{O}}_{X})={\rm span}\{\alpha+t\beta\}
, for some
t\in{\mathbb{Q}}
, by Theorem
6.13
.
Furthermore, the projection of
(\alpha+t\beta)^{n}
to
\ker(\Delta)
is a rational scalar multiple of
\Psi(\sum_{i=0}^{n}\bar{\tau}_{2i})
,
by Theorem
6.13
. Let
V(d)
be the kernel of
\Delta:{\rm Sym}^{d}(\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))\rightarrow{\rm Sym}^{d-2}(\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))
.
We have the decomposition into irreducible
{\mathfrak{s}o}(\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))
-sub-representations

{\rm Sym}^{n}(\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))=\oplus_{j=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\tilde{q}^{j}V(n-2j),

where
\tilde{q}
is defined in Example
5.2
(see the proof of
[
LL
, Prop. 2.14]
).
Now
\deg_{\alpha}(\tilde{q})=\deg_{\beta}(\tilde{q})=1
, and so
only the direct summand
V(n)
in the above decomposition of
{\rm Sym}^{n}(\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))
contains elements
f
with

\deg_{\alpha}(f)=n
and similarly for
\deg_{\beta}
.
Thus the coefficient of
\alpha^{n}

in the projection of
\frac{1}{n!}(\alpha+t\beta)^{n}
to
\ker(\Delta)=V(n)
is
\frac{1}{n!}
and the coefficient of
\beta^{n}
is

\frac{t^{n}}{n!}
.
Hence,
t^{n}=\frac{s(n!)}{c_{X}}
. We see that
t^{n}=\left(\frac{n+3}{4}\right)^{n}
in the
K3^{[n]}
-type and
t^{n}=\left(\frac{n+1}{4}\right)^{n}
in the generalized Kummer case.
If
n
is odd, we are done. If
n
is even it remains to prove that
t>0
.
The inequality
t>0
is
equivalent to
(\alpha+t\beta,\alpha+t\beta)<0
.
The inequality
(\alpha+t\beta,\alpha+t\beta)<0
follows from Remark
6.26
for
X
of
K3^{[n]}
-type. Below we will prove the inequality
t>0
directly in the
K3^{[n]}
and generalized Kummer deformation types.

The coefficient of
\alpha^{n-1}\beta

in the projection of
(\alpha+t\beta)^{n}
to
\ker(\Delta)=V(n)
has the same sign as
t
.
Indeed, we have
\Delta(\tilde{b}_{X})=\Delta(\tilde{q})=1
,

\displaystyle\Delta\left(\alpha^{n-1}\beta+(n-1)\alpha^{n-2}\tilde{b}_{X}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n-1)(\alpha,\beta)\alpha^{n-2}+(n-1)\alpha^{n-2}\Delta(\tilde{b}_{X})=0,

\displaystyle\tilde{b}_{X}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{b_{2}(X)+2}{b_{2}(X)}\right)\tilde{q}+\left(\frac{2}{b_{2}(X)}\right)\alpha\beta,

\displaystyle\alpha^{n-1}\beta+(n-1)\alpha^{n-2}\tilde{b}_{X}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{(n-1)(b_{2}(X)+2)}{b_{2}(X)}\right)\alpha^{n-2}\tilde{q}+\left(\frac{b_{2}(X)+2n-2}{b_{2}(X)}\right)\alpha^{n-1}\beta.

Multiplying both sides of the last equation by

\frac{b_{2}(X)}{b_{2}(X)+2n-2}
we see that the projection of
\alpha^{n-1}\beta
to
\ker(\Delta)
is

\left(\frac{b_{2}(X)}{b_{2}(X)+2n-2}\right)\left[\alpha^{n-1}\beta+(n-1)\alpha^{n-2}\tilde{b}_{X}\right],

and the coefficient of
\alpha^{n-1}\beta
is indeed positive.

The only term in
\sum_{i=0}^{n}\Psi(\bar{\tau}_{2i})
that contributes to the coefficient of
\alpha^{n-1}\beta
is
\Psi(\bar{\tau}_{2})
, by definition of
\Psi
. It suffices to show that the coefficient of
\alpha^{n-1}\beta
in
\Psi(\bar{\tau}_{2})
is positive.
Now,
\tau_{2}=c_{2}(TX)/24
. The projection of
c_{2}(TX)
to
SH^{4}(X)
is monodromy invariant, hence a multiple of
b_{X}
, and we claim that it is a positive multiple.
Indeed, given a line bundle
L
on
X
, the coefficient of
(c_{1}(L),c_{1}(L))^{n-1}
in the formula for

\int_{X}ch(L)td_{X}
depends only on
\int_{X}\frac{c_{1}(L)^{2n-2}}{(2n-2)!}\frac{c_{2}(TX)}{12}
and it is
positive, by
[
Hu4
, Examples 7 and 8]
, as is the coefficient of
(c_{1}(L),c_{1}(L))^{n-1}
in the formula for

\int_{X}b_{X}c_{1}(L)^{2n-2}
, by
[
O4
, Eq. (2.1.4)]
.
We conclude that
\Psi(\bar{\tau}_{2})
is a positive scalar multiple of
\Psi(b_{X})
.
Finally, the coefficient of
\alpha^{n-1}\beta
in
\Psi(b_{X})=\frac{1}{(n-2)!}\alpha^{n-2}[\tilde{b}_{X}+\alpha\beta]
is positive.
∎

The computations of Example
6.17
generalize to yield the following statement.

Lemma 6.19
.

Let
X
be of
K3^{[2]}
-type and
F
an object of
D^{b}(X)
of non-zero rank.

(1)

If
F
deforms in co-dimension
1
, then
\kappa_{3}(F)=0
and

\kappa(F)=x+yc_{2}(TX)+z[pt],

where
x,y,z\in{\mathbb{Q}}
and

(6.26)

450y^{2}+3xy-xz=0.

In this case,

(6.27)

\ell(F)={\rm span}\left\{x^{2}\alpha+xc_{1}(F)+\left(\frac{5x^{2}}{4}+30xy+\frac{(c_{1}(F),c_{1}(F))}{2}\right)\beta\right\}.

(2)

Conversely, if
\kappa_{3}(F)=0
,

\kappa(F)=x+yc_{2}(TX)+z[pt],

and
450y^{2}+3xy-xz=0
, then

F
has a rank
1
cohomological obstruction map.

(3)

If
F_{1},F_{2}\in D^{b}(X)
are of non-zero rank and
F_{1}
,
F_{2}
, and
F_{1}\otimes F_{2}
, all deform in co-dimension
1
,
then
\kappa(F_{i})
is a scalar multiple of
\kappa({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
, for
i=1
or
i=2
.

Proof.

1
)
The class
\kappa(F)
remains of Hodge type under every Kähler deformation of
X
, by Proposition
3.3
. Hence,
\kappa_{3}(F)=0
and
\kappa_{2}(F)
is a scalar multiple of
c_{2}(TX)
.
We have

\displaystyle\kappa(F)\sqrt{td_{X}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x+yc_{2}(X)+z[pt])\left(1+\frac{1}{24}c_{2}(X)+\frac{25}{32}[pt]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x+(x/24+y)c_{2}(X)+(z+25x/32+69y/2)[pt].

Set
t=x/24+y
and
s=z+25x/32+69y/2
.
We have

\displaystyle\Psi(x+tc_{2}(X)+s[pt])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x\alpha^{2}/2+30t(\tilde{q}+\alpha\beta)+s\beta^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 30t\tilde{q}+\frac{x}{2}\left(\alpha^{2}+\frac{60t}{x}\alpha\beta+\frac{2s}{x}\beta^{2}\right).

The latter belongs to the projection via (
5.7
) of the square of a line, if and only if
450t^{2}=sx
, in which case it belongs to the projection of the square of

{\rm span}\{\alpha+\frac{30t}{x}\beta\}={\rm span}\{\alpha+\left(\frac{5}{4}+\frac{30y}{x}\right)\beta\}
, by Example
5.2
. This is the case, if and only if

450y^{2}+3xy-xz=0
. The equality
v(F)=\exp(e_{c_{1}(F)/x})\kappa(F)\sqrt{td_{X}}
implies that
\ell(F)
is spanned by

\exp(e_{c_{1}(F)/x})\left(\alpha+\left(\frac{5}{4}+\frac{30y}{x}\right)\beta\right)
, by the
SO(\widetilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))
-equivariance of
\Psi
.
Finally observe that

\exp(e_{c_{1}(F)/x})\left(\alpha+\left(\frac{5}{4}+\frac{30y}{x}\right)\beta\right)=\alpha+c_{1}(F)/x+\left(\frac{5}{4}+30y/x+\frac{(c_{1}(F),c_{1}(F))}{2x^{2}}\right)\beta.

2
)
The assumptions imply that
\Psi(\kappa(F)\sqrt{td_{X}})
is invariant under the stabilizer of the line in Equation (
6.27
) in the LLV algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}:={\mathfrak{s}o}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))
. Hence,

\kappa(F)\sqrt{td_{X}}
is invariant under this subalgebra, by the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-equivariance of
\Psi
.

3
)
Set
\kappa(F_{i})=x_{i}+y_{i}c_{2}(X)+z_{i}[pt]
. Then

\kappa(F_{1}\otimes F_{2})=x_{1}x_{2}+[x_{1}y_{2}+x_{2}y_{1}]c_{2}(X)+[x_{1}z_{2}+x_{2}z_{1}+828y_{1}y_{2}][pt].

Set
\bar{y}_{i}:=y_{i}/x_{i}
and
\bar{z_{i}}=z_{i}/x_{i}
.
Equation (
6.26
) for
F_{1}\otimes F_{2}
translates to the vanishing of

\displaystyle 450(\bar{y}_{1}+\bar{y_{2}})^{2}+3(\bar{y}_{1}+\bar{y}_{2})-(\bar{z}_{1}+\bar{z}_{2}+828\bar{y}_{1}\bar{y}_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(450\bar{y}_{1}^{2}+3\bar{y}_{1}-\bar{z}_{1})+(450\bar{y}_{2}^{2}+3\bar{y}_{2}-\bar{z}_{2})+72\bar{y}_{1}\bar{y}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 72\bar{y}_{1}\bar{y}_{2}.

Hence,
y_{i}=0
, for
i=1
or
i=2
. Equation (
6.26
) and the vanishing of
y_{i}
imply that
z_{i}=0
as well.
∎

Remark 6.20
.

Theorem
6.13
imposes the following cohomological constraint on the Todd class of a
2n
dimensional irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifold
X
.
Denote by
\hat{c}_{2k}

the projection of
c_{2k}(X)
to the primitive subspace of
H^{4k}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
defined in
[
LL
, Cor. 1.13]
.
Then
\hat{c}_{2k}
generates an irreducible subrepresentation
{\mathfrak{g}}(\hat{c}_{2k})
of the LLV algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
isomorphic to the subspace
V(n\!-\!2k)
of

{\rm Sym}^{n-2k}\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
spanned by powers of isotropic classes, by
[
LL
, Lemma 2.8]
.
The direct sum
\oplus_{k=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}{\mathfrak{g}}(\hat{c}_{2k})
is the minimal
{\mathfrak{g}}
invariant subspace containing
\sqrt{td_{X}}
. Theorem
6.13
implies that
the projection of
\sqrt{td_{X}}
to
{\mathfrak{g}}(\hat{c}_{2k})
spans the image of the power
\ell({\mathcal{O}}_{X})^{n-2k}
of the same line
\ell({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
under the unique, up to scalar, isomorphism
{\mathfrak{g}}(\hat{c}_{2k})\cong V(n\!-\!2k)
. Note that when
X
is of
K3^{[n]}
-type and
n\geq 4
, then
\hat{c}_{2k}
is non-trivial
for
1\leq k\leq n/2
, by
[
Ma2
, Theorem 1.10]
.

Theorem
6.13
lists topological consequences about the Mukai vector of an object
F

deforming in co-dimension
1
(Definition
6.11
).
We emphasize next the evident fact that these topological consequences remain valid under deformations of the pair
(X,F)
. For simplicity we restrict to the case of locally free sheaves.
Let
\pi:{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow B
be a smooth and proper morphism over a connected analytic space
B
, all of which fibers are irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifolds. Let
\theta
be a class in
H^{2}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}^{*})
, in the analytic topology,
and let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a locally free
\theta
-twisted sheaf of rank
r
. Denote by
X_{b}
the fiber of
\pi
over a point
b
of
B
and by
\theta_{b}\in H^{2}(X_{b},{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{b}}^{*})
and
F_{b}
the restrictions of
\theta
and
{\mathcal{F}}
to
X_{b}
. The rational LLV lattices
\tilde{H}(X_{b},{\mathbb{Q}})
then form a local system
\underline{{\mathbb{Q}}\alpha}\oplus R^{2}\pi_{*}{\mathbb{Q}}\oplus\underline{{\mathbb{Q}}\beta}
, where

\underline{{\mathbb{Q}}\alpha}\oplus\underline{{\mathbb{Q}}\beta}
is a trivial local subsystem.
Denote by
{\mathfrak{g}}(X_{b})
the LLV Lie algebra of
X_{b}
.
Set
\bar{v}(F_{b}):=\kappa(F_{b})\sqrt{td_{X_{b}}}
and let
\hat{\bar{v}}(F_{b})
be its projection to
SH^{*}(X_{b},{\mathbb{Q}})
.

Corollary 6.21
.

Assume that for some point
b_{0}
of
B
the class
\theta_{b_{0}}
is trivial
13
13

13

Strictly speaking,
{\mathcal{F}}
is a twisted sheaf with respect to a co-cycle
\tilde{\theta}
representing the class
\theta
and
F_{b_{0}}
is a twisted sheaf with respect to the restriction
\tilde{\theta}_{b_{0}}
of
\tilde{\theta}
. The co-cycle
\tilde{\theta}_{b_{0}}
is a co-boundary. We then abuse notation and denote by
F_{b_{0}}
also an untwisted coherent sheaf under an equivalence of the category of coherent sheaves on
X_{b_{0}}
with the category of
\tilde{\theta}_{b_{0}}
-twisted sheaves. The isomorphism depends on a choice of a
1
-co-chain whose co-boundary is
\tilde{\theta}_{b_{0}}
, and so is unique up to tensorization by a line-bundle. In particular, the condition that

F_{b_{0}}
deforms in co-dimension
1

is independent of the choice of the equivalence.

and that
F_{b_{0}}
deforms in co-dimension
1
.
Then

\ell_{0}:=\exp\left(e_{-c_{1}(F_{b_{0}})/r}\right)(\ell(F_{b_{0}}))

is a line in
{\mathbb{Q}}\alpha\oplus{\mathbb{Q}}\beta
and the following statements hold for every
b\in B
.

(1)

The subalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}(X_{b})_{\ell_{0}}
of
{\mathfrak{g}}(X_{b})
annihilating the line
\ell_{0}
is equal to the subalgebra

{\mathfrak{g}}(X_{b})_{\bar{v}(F_{b})}
annihilating the class
\bar{v}(F_{b})
. In particular,
\bar{v}(F_{b})
is annihilated by

{\mathfrak{s}o}(H^{2}(X_{b},{\mathbb{Q}}))
.

(2)

The embedding

\Psi:SH^{*}(X_{b},{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow{\rm Sym}^{n}\tilde{H}(X_{b},{\mathbb{Q}})
, given in (
5.4
), maps
\hat{\bar{v}}(F_{b})

into the image of the
n
-th power
\ell_{0}^{n}
of
\ell_{0}
via the projection (
5.7
).

Proof.

The line
\ell_{0}
is in
{\mathbb{Q}}\alpha\oplus{\mathbb{Q}}\beta
, by Theorem
6.13
(
3
).
The statements hold for
X_{b_{0}}
, by the equivariance of
\Psi
with respect to the isometry

\exp\left(e_{-c_{1}(F_{b_{0}})/r}\right)
and by Theorem
6.13
parts (
1
) and (
2
). It follows for all
b\in B
, by the flatness of the section associated to
\kappa({\mathcal{F}})\sqrt{td_{\pi}}

in
R\pi_{*}{\mathbb{Q}}
and the assumption that the base
B
is connected.
∎

Lemma 6.22
.

Assume that
F_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq k
, each deforms in co-dimension one and its rank
r_{i}
is positive.
Then the following statements hold.

(1)

\Sigma(F_{i})\cap[H^{1}(TX)\oplus H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})]
is the graph of the homomorphism

\theta_{i}:H^{1}(TX)\rightarrow H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
given by

\lambda\mapsto-m_{\lambda}\left(c_{1}(F_{i})/r_{i}\right)
.

(2)

The LLV subspace
W(\otimes_{i=1}^{k}F_{i})
is contained in

{\displaystyle\exp\left(e_{\sum_{i=1}^{k}c_{1}(F_{i})/r_{i}}\right)({\rm span}_{\mathbb{Q}}\{\alpha,\beta\}).}

Proof.

(
1
)
The isomorphism
\mu:HT^{2}(X)\rightarrow\tilde{H}^{0,0}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
, given by
\mu(\lambda)=m_{\lambda}(\sigma)

in (
6.10
),
maps
H^{1}(TX)\oplus H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
to
H^{1,1}(X)\oplus{\mathbb{C}}\beta

(see Equation (
6.11
)).
The homomorphism
\mu
maps
\Sigma(F_{i})\cap[H^{1}(TX)\oplus H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})]
to

\ell(F_{i})^{\perp}\cap[H^{1,1}(X)\oplus{\mathbb{C}}\beta]
, hence to
the graph of
\tilde{\theta}_{i}:H^{1,1}(X)\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}\beta
given by

\tilde{\theta}_{i}(x)=(x,c_{1}(F_{i})/r_{i})\beta
, by Theorem
6.13
(
3
).
We have
m_{\theta_{i}(\lambda)}(\sigma)=\mu(\theta_{i}(\lambda))=\tilde{\theta}_{i}(\mu(\lambda))=(m_{\lambda}(\sigma),c_{1}(F_{i})/r_{i})\beta
, for all
\lambda\in H^{1}(TX)
. Pairing with
\alpha
, we get:

\displaystyle(\alpha,\mu(\theta_{i}(\lambda)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(m_{\theta_{i}(\lambda)}(\alpha),\sigma),

\displaystyle(\alpha,\tilde{\theta}_{i}(\mu(\lambda)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(m_{\lambda}(\sigma),c_{1}(F_{i})/r_{i})=(\sigma,m_{\lambda}(c_{1}(F_{i})/r_{i})).

It remains to prove that
m_{\theta_{i}(\lambda)}(\alpha)=\theta_{i}(\lambda)
.
Now,
m_{\theta_{i}(\lambda)}=\eta\circ e_{\theta_{i}(\lambda)}\circ\eta^{-1}
,
by Equation (
6.4
), and
\eta\circ e_{\theta_{i}(\lambda)}\circ\eta^{-1}=e_{\theta_{i}(\lambda)},

since
\theta_{i}(\lambda)
belongs to
H^{2}(\wedge^{0}TX)
, and
e_{\theta_{i}(\lambda)}(\alpha)=\theta_{i}(\lambda)
, by definition
(Eq. (
2.2
)).

(
2
)
The intersection
\Sigma(\otimes_{i=1}^{k}F_{i})\cap[H^{1}(TX)\oplus H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})]

is the graph of
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\theta_{i}:H^{1}(TX)\rightarrow H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
,
as observed in Section
6.3
. Hence,

\mu
maps the intersection to the graph of the homomorphism from
H^{1,1}(X)
to
{\mathbb{C}}\beta
given by

x\mapsto\left(x,\sum_{i=1}^{k}c_{1}(F_{i})/r_{i}\right)\beta,

by the proof of Part (
1
).
The latter graph is precisely

\exp\left(e_{\sum_{i=1}^{k}c_{1}(F_{i})/r_{i}}\right)(H^{1,1}(X))
. The subspace

W(\otimes_{i=1}^{k}F_{i})
is thus contained in the subspace
{\displaystyle\exp\left(e_{\sum_{i=1}^{k}c_{1}(F_{i})/r_{i}}\right)({\rm span}_{\mathbb{Q}}\{\alpha,\beta\})}

orthogonal to
\exp\left(e_{\sum_{i=1}^{k}c_{1}(F_{i})/r_{i}}\right)(H^{1,1}(X))
.
∎

6.5.
Torsion sheaves which deform in co-dimension
1
are supported on lagrangian subvarieties or points

Let
X
be a
2n
-dimensional irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifold.
Let
\sigma
be a non-zero class in
H^{0}(X,\Omega^{2}_{X})
.

Lemma 6.23
.

Let
F
be an object of
D^{b}(X)
such that

F
has a rank
1
cohomological obstruction map (Def.
6.11
).
Assume that the line
\ell(F)
in

\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
is spanned by a class of the form
0\alpha+\lambda+s\beta
, where
\lambda
is a class in
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
.
Then
ch_{i}(F)=0
, for
i<n
, and
ch_{n}(F)\cup\sigma=0
.

Proof.

Let
\bar{\sigma}\in H^{2}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
be the class conjugate to that of
\sigma
and let
m_{\bar{\sigma}}
be the image of
\bar{\sigma}
in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
via (
6.9
).
The element
m_{\bar{\sigma}}
has weight
(0,2)
and thus it maps the direct summand
\tilde{H}_{\rm fine}^{p,q}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
to

\tilde{H}_{\rm fine}^{p,q+2}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
. It follows that
m_{\bar{\sigma}}
annihilates
\tilde{H}_{\rm fine}^{p,q}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
if
(p,q)
is
(1,1)
or
(2,2)
.
Consequently,
m_{\bar{\sigma}}

annihilates
\ell(F)
, as it is contained in
\tilde{H}_{\rm fine}^{1,1}(X,{\mathbb{C}})\oplus\tilde{H}_{\rm fine}^{2,2}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
.
The subalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{v(F)}
annihilating
v(F)
is equal to
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\ell(F)}

(use the proof of Theorem
6.13
part (
1
)
replacing the use of Lemma
6.10
by the assumption that

F
has a rank
1
cohomological obstruction map).
Hence,
m_{\bar{\sigma}}
belongs to
{\mathfrak{g}}_{v(F)}
.

Wedge product with
\sigma^{k}
induces an isomorphism
\Omega_{X}^{n-k}\rightarrow\Omega_{X}^{n+k}
, for
0\leq k\leq n
, and thus an isomorphism
H^{n-k,q}(X)\cong H^{n+k,q}(X)
. It follows that
cup product with the class of
\sigma
induces an injective homomorphism from
H^{p,p}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
into
H^{p+2,p}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
, for
p<n
. Thus, cup product with
\bar{\sigma}

induces an injective homomorphism from
H^{p,p}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
into
H^{p,p+2}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
, for
p<n
. We conclude that the direct summand
v_{p}(F)
of
v(F)
in
H^{p,p}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
vanishes, for
p<n
, as the operator
m_{\bar{\sigma}}
acts on
H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
via cup product with
\bar{\sigma}
. The equality
v(F)=ch(F)\sqrt{td_{X}}
implies that the first non zero graded summand of
ch(F)
and
v(F)
are equal. Thus,
ch_{p}(F)=0
, for
p<n
, and

ch_{n}(F)=v_{n}(F)
. Thus
ch_{n}(F)\cup\sigma
vanishes, being the complex conjugate of
v_{n}(F)\cup\bar{\sigma}
which vanishes.
∎

Theorem 6.24
.

Let
\iota:Z\hookrightarrow X
be an embedding of an irreducible variety
Z
of dimension
<2n
and let
F
be a torsion free sheaf over
Z
. Assume that
v(\iota_{*}F)
deforms in co-dimension
1
and the projection
\hat{v}(\iota_{*}F)
does not vanish. Then
\iota(Z)
is either a lagrangian subvariety or a point.

Proof.

Let
p
be the complex co-dimension of
\iota(Z)
in
X
.
Let
[Z]\in H^{p,p}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
be the class Poincaré dual to
\iota(Z)
.
Then
ch_{i}(\iota_{*}F)=0
, for
i<p
, and
v_{p}(\iota_{*}F))=ch_{p}(\iota_{*}F)={\rm rank}(F)[Z]
. The inequality
p\geq n
and the vanishing of
ch_{n}(\iota_{*}F)\cup\sigma
follow, by
Lemma
6.23
.

Let
r\alpha+\lambda+s\beta
be a non-zero element of
\ell(\iota_{*}F)
. Then
r=0
, since
\iota(Z)
is a proper subvariety of
X
. If
\lambda=0
, then
v(F)
is annihilated by
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\beta}
. In particular,
v(F)
is annihilated by

L_{\omega}
, for every Kähler class
\omega
on
X
. Hence,
v_{p}(\iota_{*}F)={\rm rank}(F)[Z]

is annihilated by

L_{\omega}
, for every Kähler class
\omega
on
X
.
Hence,
\iota^{*}\omega=0
, for every Kähler class
\omega
on
X
, and so
Z
is a point.

Assume that
\lambda\neq 0
. Let
\omega
be a Kähler class on
X
such that
(\lambda,\omega)\neq 0
.
Then
L_{\omega}^{n}((\lambda+s\beta)^{n})=n!(\omega,\lambda)^{n}\beta^{n}
does not vanish.
Now
L_{\omega}
belongs to
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
,
\Psi
is
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
-equivariant, and

\Psi(\hat{v}(\iota_{*}F))
spans
\ell(\iota_{*}F)^{n}
.
Hence,
L_{\omega}^{n}
does not annihilate
\hat{v}(\iota_{*}F)
and thus neither
v(\iota_{*}F)
.
Now
L_{\omega}^{n}
annihilates
the graded summand
v_{i}(\iota_{*}F)
in
H^{2i}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
, for
i>n
. Thus
L_{\omega}^{n}(v_{n}(\iota_{*}F))\neq 0
.
We conclude that
Z
is
n
-dimensional.
The subvariety
Z
is lagrangian by the vanishing of

ch_{n}(\iota_{*}F)\cup\sigma
(see
[
Le
, Prop. 5]
).
∎

Let
\iota:Z\rightarrow X
be an embedding of a compact Kähler manifold as a holomorphic lagrangian submanifold.
Assume that the obstruction map (
3.1
) from
HT^{2}(X)
to
{\rm Ext}^{2}(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z},\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})
has rank
1
. Then the homomorphism
H^{1}(X,TX)\rightarrow H^{1}(Z,N_{Z/X})
,
induced by the sheaf homomorphism
TX\rightarrow\iota_{*}N_{Z/X}
, has rank
1
. Conjugating via the isomorphisms

TX\cong\Omega^{1}_{X}
and
N_{Z/X}\cong\Omega^{1}_{Z}
, induced by the symplectic structure, we get that

H^{1}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})\rightarrow H^{1}(Z,\Omega^{1}_{Z})
has rank
1
.
The restriction homomorphism
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{C}})\rightarrow H^{2}(Z,{\mathbb{C}})
factors through the latter, since
Z
is lagrangian, and hence
has rank
1
.
Let
L\in{\rm Pic}(X)
be a line bundle, such that the sublattice
c_{1}(L)^{\perp}
orthogonal to
c_{1}(L)
with respect to the BBF-pairing is equal to the kernel of the restriction homomorphism
\iota^{*}:H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})\rightarrow H^{2}(Z,{\mathbb{Z}})
.
Assume that there exists
a class
\tau\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
, such that

\iota^{*}(\tau)=c_{1}(\omega_{Z}).
This is the case if
h^{1,1}(Z)=1
, as in
Example
3.8
, if
Z
is a complex torus, or if
Z
is the zero subscheme of a global section of a rank
n
vector bundle on
X
.

Lemma 6.25
.

\ell(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})
is spanned by
{\displaystyle c_{1}(L)-\frac{(\tau,c_{1}(L))}{2}\beta}
.

Proof.

Let
\gamma:=r\alpha+\lambda+s\beta
be a non-zero element of
\ell(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})
.
The restriction homomorphism
\iota^{*}:H^{2}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})\rightarrow H^{2}(Z,{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})
vanishes, so
\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}
deforms along gerby deformations of
X
. Hence,

H^{2}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
is contained in
\Sigma(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})
. The Duflo automorphism of
HT^{*}(X)
restricts to
H^{2}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
as the identity, and so
H^{2}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
is contained in
\tilde{\Sigma}(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})
.
Thus

\mu(H^{2}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X}))
is orthogonal to
\ell(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})
, by Theorem
6.13
.
Now,
\mu(H^{2}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X}))={\mathbb{C}}\beta
(see Equation (
6.11
)).
Hence,
\beta
is orthogonal to
\gamma
and thus the coefficient
r
of
\alpha
is
0
.

The sheaf
\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}
deforms with
X
in a direction
\xi\in H^{1}(TX)
, if and only if the line bundle
L
does, by

[
Vo1
]
. Hence,
\mu(\tilde{\Sigma}(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})\cap H^{1}(TX))=c_{1}(L)^{\perp}\cap H^{1,1}(X)
and

\lambda
is a non-zero scalar multiple of
c_{1}(L)
. We may and thus do choose
\lambda
to be
c_{1}(L)
, so that
\gamma=c_{1}(L)+s\beta
.

We have
\ell((\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})^{\vee})=\tilde{D}(\gamma)=-c_{1}(L)+s\beta
, by Lemma
6.15
.
Local duality yields
(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})^{\vee}\cong\iota_{*}(\omega_{Z})[-n]
, so that
v((\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})^{\vee})=(-1)^{n}\exp(\tau)\cdot v(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})
. Hence,

\ell((\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})^{\vee})=-\exp(e_{\tau})(\ell(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}))
, which is spanned by

-\exp(e_{\tau})(\gamma)=-c_{1}(L)-[s+(\tau,c_{1}(L))]\beta
. Comparing coefficients of
\beta
we get

s=-s-(\tau,c_{1}(L))
, so
s=-\frac{(\tau,c_{1}(L))}{2}
.
∎

Remark 6.26
.

Let
S
be a
K3
surface and let
\iota:C\rightarrow S
be an embedding of a smooth rational curve
C
.
Set
X:=S^{[n]}
,
n\geq 2
. We get the embedding

\iota^{[n]}:C^{(n)}\rightarrow X
of the
n
-th symmetric product of
C
, hence a lagrangian
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
in
X
.
Example
3.12
exhibits an auto-equivalence
\Phi:D^{b}(S)\rightarrow D^{b}(S)
which maps
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
to
\iota_{*}\omega_{C}
. We get the auto-equivalence
\Phi^{[n]}:D^{b}(X)\rightarrow D^{b}(X)
which maps
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
to the object in
D^{b}(X)
corresponding to the
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
-equivariant sheaf

\boxtimes_{i=1}^{n}\iota_{*}\omega_{C}
over
S^{n}
via the BKR-correspondence.
The latter object in
D^{b}(X)
is isomorphic to
\iota^{[n]}_{*}\omega_{C^{(n)}}
. Hence, the isometry
\phi^{[n]}
of

\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
associated to
\Phi^{[n]}
maps
\ell({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
to
\ell(\iota^{[n]}_{*}\omega_{C^{(n)}})
.
The latter is spanned by a class
\gamma
of the form
0\alpha+\lambda+s\beta
, where
s\in{\mathbb{Q}}
and
\lambda
is a primitive class in
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
, such that
\lambda^{\perp}
is the kernel of the restriction homomorphism
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})\rightarrow H^{2}(C^{(n)},{\mathbb{Z}})
. The class
\lambda
has BBF self-intersection
-2(n+3)

[
Ba
, Theorem 3]
.
This agrees with the fact that
\ell({\mathcal{O}}_{X})

is spanned by the class
2\alpha+\frac{n+3}{2}\beta
of self-intersection
-2(n+3)
(see Lemma
6.18
).

7.
Lagrangian surfaces in IHSMs of
K3^{[2]}
-type

In Section
7.1

we use the determination of
\ell(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})
in Lemma
6.25

to calculate the Chern character of
\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}
for the embedding

\iota:Z\rightarrow X
of a smooth lagrangian surface
Z
in
X
of
K3^{[2]}
-type (Lemma
7.1
).
In Section
7.2

we show that
\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}
has a rank
1
cohomological obstruction map if
Z
is a
smooth lagrangian surface in
X
of
K3^{[2]}
-type with a rank
1
restriction homomorphism
H^{2}(X)\rightarrow H^{2}(Z)
with an image containing
c_{1}(\omega_{Z})
(Lemma
7.3
).
Integral constraints on the topological invariants of such surfaces are presented in Section
7.3
(Lemma
7.6
).
In Section
7.4
we explain why examples of such smooth lagrangian subvarieties with a non-vanishing first Betti number may yield new examples of holomorphic symplectic varieties.

7.1.
The Chern character of
{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}
for a maximally deformable lagrangian surface
Z

Keep the notation and assumptions of Lemma
6.25
.
Set
\lambda:=c_{1}(L)
and assume that
(\lambda,\lambda)\neq 0
and
c_{1}(\omega_{Z})=\iota^{*}\tau
,
for some
\tau\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
. Then

c_{1}(\omega_{Z})=t\iota^{*}\lambda,

for some
14
14

14

In Section
7.3
we will assume that
\lambda
is a primitive class and derive arithmetic constraints on
\chi(Z)
and
(\lambda,\lambda)
. This is the reason we introduce the variable
t
, rather than allow
\lambda
to be a rational class.

t\in{\mathbb{Q}}
, since
\lambda
does not belong to
\lambda^{\perp}
. Set

(7.1)

c:=\frac{5}{4|(\lambda,\lambda)|}\sqrt{\frac{\chi(Z)}{3}},

where
\chi(Z)
is the topological Euler characteristic of
Z
. The following Lemma expresses
ch(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})

in terms of
\chi(Z)
and
\lambda
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Assume, furthermore, that
X
is of
K3^{[2]}
-type.
Then
\chi(Z)>0
and the following equalities hold.

(7.2)

\displaystyle t^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4\sqrt{3\chi(Z)}}{5|(\lambda,\lambda)|}-\frac{6}{5(\lambda,\lambda)}.

(7.3)

\displaystyle\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{Z})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c\left(\frac{(\lambda,\lambda)}{10}+\frac{t^{2}(\lambda,\lambda)^{2}}{4}\right).

(7.4)

\displaystyle ch_{2}(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[Z]=c\left(\lambda^{2}-\frac{(\lambda,\lambda)}{30}c_{2}(X)\right).

(7.5)

\displaystyle ch_{3}(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{ct}{3}\lambda^{3}.

(7.6)

\displaystyle ch_{4}(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c\left(\frac{t^{2}(\lambda,\lambda)^{2}}{4}-\frac{(\lambda,\lambda)}{10}\right)[pt].

Proof.

Lemma
6.25

yields the equality

(7.7)

\Psi(ch(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})\sqrt{td_{X}})=a\Pi\left(\lambda^{2}-t(\lambda,\lambda)\lambda\beta+\frac{t^{2}(\lambda,\lambda)^{2}}{4}\beta^{2}\right),

for some
a\in{\mathbb{Q}}
, where
\Pi
is the projection from
{\rm Sym}^{2}(\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}))
to
\ker(\Delta)
.
We get

\displaystyle\Psi\left([Z]+ch_{3}(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})+ch_{4}(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})+\frac{1}{24}[Z]c_{2}(X)\right)\ \ \ =

\displaystyle a\left(\lambda^{2}-(\lambda,\lambda)\tilde{q}-t(\lambda,\lambda)\lambda\beta+\frac{t^{2}(\lambda,\lambda)^{2}}{4}\beta^{2}\right)

Comparing the weight
(2,2)
summands we get Equation (
7.4
), with
a
instead of
c
, using also Example
5.2
, Equation (
6.23
), and the equality
\Psi(\lambda^{2})=\lambda^{2}+(\lambda,\lambda)\alpha\beta
. We have

\chi(Z)=\int_{X}[Z]^{2}=a^{2}\int_{X}\left(\lambda^{4}-\frac{(\lambda,\lambda)}{15}c_{2}(X)\lambda^{2}+\frac{(\lambda,\lambda)^{2}}{900}c_{2}(X)^{2}\right)=a^{2}\frac{48}{25}(\lambda,\lambda)^{2}.

Hence,
\chi(Z)>0
and
a=c
.
Equation (
7.5
) follows from the equality
\Psi(\lambda^{3})=3(\lambda,\lambda)\lambda\beta
, by comparing the weight
(3,3)
summands. Equation (
7.6
)
follows by equating the weight
(4,4)
summands using Equation
(
7.4
). Equation (
7.3
) follows from the equality

\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{Z})=\chi(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})=\int_{X}ch(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})td_{X}=\int_{X}\left(ch_{4}(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})+\frac{1}{12}[Z]c_{2}(X)\right)

and (
7.6
).

It remains to prove Equation (
7.2
).
We have the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow TZ\rightarrow\iota^{*}TX\rightarrow N_{Z/X}\rightarrow 0.

The isomorphism
TZ\cong N^{*}_{Z/X}
yields

1+\iota^{*}c_{2}(TX)=1-c_{1}(TZ)^{2}+2c_{2}(TZ).

Hence,
c_{2}(TZ)=\frac{1}{2}\left[\iota^{*}c_{2}(TX)+c_{1}(\omega_{Z})^{2}\right].

The equality
\chi(Z)=\int_{Z}c_{2}(TZ)
yields

\int_{Z}c_{1}(\omega_{Z})^{2}=2\chi(Z)-\int_{X}[Z]c_{2}(TX).

Hirzebruch-Riemann-Roch over
Z
yields the first equality below.

\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{Z})=\frac{1}{12}\int_{Z}\left(c_{1}(\omega_{Z})^{2}+c_{2}(TZ)\right)=\frac{\chi(Z)}{4}-\frac{1}{12}\int_{X}[Z]c_{2}(TX).

Equation (
7.4
) and the definition of
c
yield

(7.8)

\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{Z})=\frac{\chi(Z)}{4}-\frac{c(\lambda,\lambda)}{5}=\frac{1}{4}\left(\chi(Z)-\frac{(\lambda,\lambda)}{|(\lambda,\lambda)|}\sqrt{\frac{\chi(Z)}{3}}\right).

Comparing the right hand sides of the above equation with that of Equation (
7.3
) and solving for
t^{2}
we get Equation (
7.2
).
∎

Remark 7.2
.

Note that Equation (
7.2
) and the definition of
c
yield

(7.9)

t^{2}=\frac{48}{25}c-\frac{6}{5(\lambda,\lambda)}.

The latter, combined with
Equation (
7.3
), yields

(7.10)

\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{Z})=\frac{1}{4}\left(\chi(Z)-\frac{(\lambda,\lambda)}{|(\lambda,\lambda)|}\sqrt{\frac{\chi(Z)}{3}}\right).

The above and the definition of
\chi(Z)
yield the two linear equations in three variables.

\displaystyle 2\pm\sqrt{\frac{\chi(Z)}{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h^{1,1}(Z)-2h^{2,0}(Z),

\displaystyle\chi(Z)-2

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h^{1,1}(Z)+2h^{2,0}(Z)-4h^{1,0}(Z),

where the sign in the first equation is that of
(\lambda,\lambda)
.
If
(\lambda,\lambda)>0
, then

(7.11)

\displaystyle h^{2,0}(Z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\left[\chi(Z)-\sqrt{\frac{\chi(Z)}{3}}+4h^{1,0}(Z)-4\right],

\displaystyle h^{1,1}(Z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[\chi(Z)+\sqrt{\frac{\chi(Z)}{3}}+4h^{1,0}(Z)\right].

Equation (
7.10
) yields
\chi(Z)=3\left[h^{1,1}(Z)-2-2h^{2,0}(Z)\right]^{2}
(the Euler characteristic is
3
times the signature minus
2
squared), or equivalently,

h^{1,0}(Z)=\frac{1}{4}\left(2+2h^{2,0}(Z)+h^{1,1}(Z)-3\left[h^{1,1}(Z)-2-2h^{2,0}(Z)\right]^{2}\right).

If
h^{1,1}(Z)=1
, then
h^{1,0}(Z)=-\frac{1}{2}(5h^{2,0}(Z)+6h^{2,0}(Z)^{2})
, so
h^{1,0}(Z)=h^{2,0}(Z)=0
.
If, for example,
h^{2,0}(Z)=0
, then the pair
(h^{1,0}(Z),h^{1,1}(Z))
is either
(0,1)
, or
(1,2)
. The former is realized by
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
, while the latter implies
\chi(Z)=0
which is impossible if
(\lambda,\lambda)\neq 0
, by Lemma
7.1
. If
h^{2,0}=1
, then the pair
(h^{1,0}(Z),h^{1,1}(Z))
is either

(1,3)
, or
(2,4)
. The latter is realized by a lagrangian abelian surface, while the former can not occur if
(\lambda,\lambda)>0
,
by Equation (
7.11
). More generally, the last displayed equality implies that
h^{1,1}(Z)
and
2+2h^{2,0}(Z)
are “close”, in the sense that if
2+2h^{2,0}(Z)=ah^{1,1}(Z)
, then

h^{1,1}(Z)\leq\frac{a+1}{3(a-1)^{2}}
, since
h^{1,0}(Z)\geq 0
, so
a
is close to
1
for large values of
h^{1,1}(Z)
.

7.2.
Lagrangian surfaces
Z
with
{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}
having a rank
1
cohomological obstruction map

Lemma 7.3
.

Assume that
X
is of
K3^{[2]}
-type and that
\iota^{*}:H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})\rightarrow H^{2}(Z,{\mathbb{Z}})
has rank
1
,
Let
L
be the line bundle over
X
such that
\ker(\iota^{*})=c_{1}(L)^{\perp}
. Assume that
(c_{1}(L),c_{1}(L))\neq 0

and that
c_{1}(\omega_{Z})=t\iota^{*}c_{1}(L)
, for some
t\in{\mathbb{Q}}
.
Then
{\mathfrak{g}}_{v(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})}={\mathfrak{g}}_{\ell}
, where
\ell
is spanned by

c_{1}(L)-t\frac{(c_{1}(L),c_{1}(L))}{2}\beta
.

Proof.

Set
\lambda:=c_{1}(L)
.
It suffices to verify Equation (
7.7
) with
a=c
.
Equation (
7.7
) would follow once we verify Equations
(
7.4
), (
7.5
), and (
7.6
).
The class
[Z]\in H^{4}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
is invariant under the stabilizer of
\lambda
in the monodromy group of
X
.
Hence,
[Z]=a\lambda^{2}+bc_{2}(TX)
, for some
a,b\in{\mathbb{Q}}
. Let
\sigma
be a non-zero element of
H^{0}(X,\Omega^{2}_{X})
. The pull back
\iota^{*}\sigma
vanishes.
Hence, the cohomology class
[Z]\cup\sigma
vanishes, since
\int_{X}[Z]\cup\sigma\cup(\bullet)=\int_{Z}\iota^{*}(\sigma)\cup(\bullet)
.
We drop the symbol
\cup
below.
We have

0=\int_{X}[Z]\sigma\bar{\sigma}=\int_{X}[a\lambda^{2}+bc_{2}(TX)]\sigma\bar{\sigma}=a[2(\lambda,\sigma)(\lambda,\bar{\sigma})+(\lambda,\lambda)(\sigma,\bar{\sigma})]+30b(\sigma,\bar{\sigma})

and
(\lambda,\sigma)=0
.
Hence,
b=-a(\lambda,\lambda)/30
and Equation (
7.4
) holds with
c
replaced by
a
. The equality
a=c
is proven using the equality
\chi(Z)=\int_{X}[Z]^{2}
as in the proof of
Lemma
7.1
.

Equation (
7.6
) is equivalent to Equation (
7.3
), by Hirzebruch-Riemann-Roch (and the fact that Equation (
7.4
) is verified and the vanishing of the direct summand of
td_{X}
in
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
). Now Equation (
7.3
) follows from Equation (
7.4
) and the fact that
Z
is lagrangian, see the derivation of Equation (
7.8
) which exhibits
\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{Z})
as a function of only
\chi(Z)
and the sign of
(\lambda,\lambda)
.

It remains to prove Equation (
7.5
). The class
ch_{3}(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})

must be equal to
a\lambda^{3}
, for some
a\in{\mathbb{Q}}
, as it is invariant under the stabilizer of
\lambda
in the monodromy group of
X
. We need to prove the equality
a=-ct/3
. We calculate
\chi(\iota^{*}(L^{\otimes nt}))
in two ways.
Using Grothendieck-Riemann-Roch on
X
we get the polynomial in
n

\chi(\iota^{*}(L^{\otimes nt}))=\int_{X}\left(1+nt\lambda+\dots+\frac{n^{4}t^{4}}{4!}\lambda^{4}\right)ch(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})td_{X}.

The coefficient of
n^{1}
is
3at(\lambda,\lambda)^{2}
.
On the other hand, Hirzebruch-Riemann-Roch on
Z
yields the second equality below:

\chi(\iota^{*}(L^{\otimes nt}))=\chi(\omega_{Z}^{\otimes n})=\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{Z})+\frac{n(n-1)}{2}\int_{Z}c_{1}(\omega_{Z})^{2}.

The coefficient of
n^{1}
is

-\frac{1}{2}\int_{Z}c_{1}(\omega_{Z})^{2}=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{X}c(\lambda^{2}-\frac{(\lambda,\lambda)}{30}c_{2}(TX))t^{2}\lambda^{2}=-ct^{2}(\lambda,\lambda)^{2}.

Comparing the two coefficients of
n^{1}
we get the equality
a=-ct/3
.
∎

Example 7.4
.

If we let
Z={\mathbb{P}}^{2}
in Lemma
7.1
, then
(\lambda,\lambda)=-10
, the divisibility (Definition
3.14
) of
\lambda
is
2
,
c=\frac{1}{8}
, and
t=\pm\frac{3}{5}
. If
Z
is the Fano variety of lines on a hyperplane section of a cubic
4
-fold, as in Example
3.11
(
2
), then
(\lambda,\lambda)=6
, the divisibility of
\lambda
is
2
,
c=\frac{5}{8}
, and
t=1
.
If
Z
is the fixed locus of an anti-symplectic involution, as in Example
3.11
, then
(\lambda,\lambda)=2
, the divisibility of
\lambda
is
1
,
c=5
(since
\chi(Z)=192
), and
t=3
, by
[
F
, Prop. 1.10]
.

7.3.
Integral constraints for the existence of smooth lagrangian surfaces

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
X
be of
K3^{[2]}
-type and let
\lambda
be a primitive class in
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
.

(1)

If
{\rm div}(\lambda)=1
, then
\frac{1}{\gcd(5,(\lambda,\lambda))}\left(5\lambda^{2}-\frac{(\lambda,\lambda)}{6}c_{2}(TX)\right)

is a primitive class in
H^{4}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
.

(2)

If
{\rm div}(\lambda)=2
, then
\frac{1}{\gcd\left(40,5+\frac{(\lambda,\lambda)}{2}\right)}\left(5\lambda^{2}-\frac{(\lambda,\lambda)}{6}c_{2}(TX)\right)

is a primitive class in
H^{4}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
.

The proof of Lemma
7.5
uses the integral generators for the cohomology ring
H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
found in
[
Ma0
]
. The proof is omitted.

Smooth lagrangian surfaces satisfying the hypotheses of Lemma
7.1

must satisfy the following arithmetic constraints. Let
c
be the rational number given in Equation (
7.1
).

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
\iota:Z\rightarrow X
be an embedding of a smooth projective lagrangian surface in
X
of
K3^{[2]}
-type. Assume that
the kernel of the restriction homomorphism
\iota^{*}:H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})\rightarrow H^{2}(Z,{\mathbb{Z}})
is equal to
c_{1}(L)^{\perp}
for a line bundle
L
with primitive
\lambda:=c_{1}(L)
, such that
(\lambda,\lambda)>0
.
Assume furthermore that
c_{1}(\omega_{Z})=t\iota^{*}\tau
, for some class
\tau\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
and
t\in{\mathbb{Q}}
. Then

(1)

5
does not divide
(\lambda,\lambda)
.

(2)

If
3
divides
x:=\frac{(\lambda,\lambda)}{2}
,
then

{\rm div}(\lambda)=2
,
x\equiv 3
modulo
8
,
8c/5
is an integer, and both

3x
and
16cx-5
are perfect squares.

(3)

If

3
does not divide

x:=\frac{(\lambda,\lambda)}{2}
, then
x
and
3(16cx-5)
are perfect squares.
If
{\rm div}(\lambda)=1
, then
c/5
is an integer. If
{\rm div}(\lambda)=2
, then
\gcd\left(8,5+\frac{(\lambda,\lambda)}{2}\right)\frac{c}{5}
is an integer.

Proof.

Assume first that
\gcd(5,(\lambda,\lambda))=1
.
If
{\rm div}(\lambda)=1
, set
n:=0
. If
{\rm div}(\lambda)=2
, let
n
the integer, such that
2^{n}:=\gcd\left(8,5+\frac{(\lambda,\lambda)}{2}\right)
.
Then
[Z]=\frac{k}{2^{n}}\left(5\lambda^{2}-\frac{(\lambda,\lambda)}{6}c_{2}(TX)\right)
, for an integer
k=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccl}c/5&\mbox{if}&{\rm div}(\lambda)=1,\\
2^{n}c/5&\mbox{if}&{\rm div}(\lambda)=2,\end{array}\right.

by Lemmas
7.1
and
7.5
.
Hence,

\frac{48k}{5\cdot 2^{n}}-\frac{6}{5(\lambda,\lambda)}\ \ =\ \ \frac{6(2^{3-n}k(\lambda,\lambda)-1)}{5(\lambda,\lambda)}

must be the square of the rational number
t
, by Equation (
7.9
).
Equivalently,

15\frac{(\lambda,\lambda)}{2}(2^{3-n}k(\lambda,\lambda)-1)

is a perfect square.

Set
x:=\frac{(\lambda,\lambda)}{2}
.
Then
15x(2^{4-n}kx-1)
is a perfect square.
Let
p
be a prime integer. If
p
divides
x
, then

p
does not divide
(2^{4-n}kx-1)
.
Thus, either both
3x
and
5(2^{4-n}kx-1)
are perfect squares, or both

x
and
15(2^{4-n}kx-1)
are perfect squares. Part (
3
) follow from the second case.
The first case implies that
n=3
, since otherwise
5(2^{4-n}kx-1)\equiv-1
modulo
4
and it can not be a square.
Hence, if
3
divides
x
, then

{\rm div}(\lambda)=2
,
x=(\lambda,\lambda)/2\equiv 3
modulo
8
,
k=\frac{8c}{5}
is an integer, and both
3x
and
5(2kx-1)
are perfect squares. Part (
2
) follows.

Assume next that
5
divides
(\lambda,\lambda)
.
Then the primitive lagrangian class is

\frac{1}{2^{n}}\left(\lambda^{2}-\frac{(\lambda,\lambda)}{30}c_{2}(TX)\right)
, where
n=0
, if

{\rm div}(\lambda)=1
, or

2^{n}=\gcd\left(8,\frac{(\lambda,\lambda)}{10}+1\right)
, if

{\rm div}(\lambda)=2
, by Lemma

7.5
.
Thus,
[Z]=\frac{k}{2^{n}}\left(\lambda^{2}-\frac{(\lambda,\lambda)}{30}c_{2}(TX)\right)
, for the integer
k=2^{n}c
.
Set
x:=\frac{(\lambda,\lambda)}{10}
. Then

\sqrt{\frac{48k}{25\cdot 2^{n}}-\frac{6}{5(\lambda,\lambda)}}=\frac{1}{5}\sqrt{\frac{3(2^{4-n}kx-1)}{x}}

should be a rational number. Hence,
3x(2^{4-n}kx-1)
is a perfect square.
If a prime
p
divides
x
, then it does not divide
(2^{4-n}kx-1)
.
Hence, either both
x
and
3(2^{4-n}kx-1)
are perfect squares,
or both
3x
and
2^{4-n}kx-1
are perfect squares.
If both
x
and
3(2^{4-n}kx-1)
are perfect squares, then
n\geq 2
, since
-3
is not a square modulo
8
. Hence
{\rm div}(\lambda)=2
. It not possible for
both
3x
and
2^{4-n}kx-1
to be perfect squares, since in that case

3
divides
x
and so
2^{4-n}kx-1\equiv-1
modulo
3
, which is not a square.
We conclude that
x
is a perfect square. Now
10x\equiv 2x
modulo
8
and
10x=(\lambda,\lambda)\equiv 6
modulo
8
, since
{\rm div}(\lambda)=2
. Hence
x\equiv 3
or
-1
modulo
8
. The latter contradicts the fact that
x
is a perfect square. Thus
5
does not divide
(\lambda,\lambda)
and
Part (
1
) follows.
∎

Remark 7.7
.

Following are two cases satisfying the arithmetic constraints above.
The case
(\lambda,\lambda)=8
,
{\rm div}(\lambda)
either
1
or
2
,
c=620
(so that
\chi(Z)=3\cdot 2^{14}\cdot 31^{2}
), and
t=69/2
.
The case
(\lambda,\lambda)=54
,
{\rm div}(\lambda)=2
,
c=245/8
(so
\chi(Z)=3^{7}7^{4}
), and
t=23/3
.
A statement similar to Lemma
7.6
can be formulated for
(\lambda,\lambda)<0
and proven essentially with the same argument.

Remark 7.8
.

Singular lagrangian ruled surfaces, which deform with a polarized holomorphic symplectic manifold
(X,L)
of
K3^{[2]}
deformation type, are more common and their existence is proven
15
15

15

The existence of a uniruled divisor in
X
in the linear system of
L
is proven in
[
CMP
]
and a choice of a curve in the base of the ruling determines a lagrangian surface.

in
[
CMP
]
for all but finitely many deformation types of the polarization
L
.

7.4.
Lagrangian subvarieties with a positive LLV line may lead to new examples of IHSMs

Though rather constrained, additional examples
of lagrangian surfaces satisfying the assumptions of Lemma
7.1

would be potentially interesting.
Smooth lagrangian subvarieties
Z
of projective irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifolds

X
satisfying the conditions

(1)

H^{1,0}(Z)
does not vanish,

(2)

the restriction homomorphism
\rho:H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})\rightarrow H^{2}(Z,{\mathbb{Z}})
has rank
1
,

(3)

the lattice orthogonal to
\ker(\rho)
is spanned by an ample class
\lambda
,

are interesting from the point of view of the classification of holomorphic symplectic varieties for the following reason. The ampleness of
\lambda
implies that
(\lambda,\lambda)>0

and so
\lambda^{\perp}
is a non-degenerate sublattice of co-rank
1
in
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
.
The pair
(X,Z)
deforms over an open subset of the period domain
\Omega_{\lambda^{\perp}}
of weight
2
Hodge structures associated to the lattice
\lambda^{\perp}

[
Vo1
]
. The class
\lambda
remains ample over an open dense subset in moduli.
Note that
\dim(\Omega_{\lambda^{\perp}})=h^{1,1}(X)-1
. Let
M
be the connected component of the moduli space of
\lambda
-stable sheaves on
X
containing

\iota_{*}L
, where
\iota:Z\rightarrow X
is the inclusion and
L
is a line bundle on
Z
with

c_{1}(L)\in{\rm span}_{\mathbb{Q}}\{\iota^{*}\lambda,c_{1}(\omega_{Z})\}
. Let
B
be the irreducible component of the
Hilbert scheme of
X
containing
Z
.
Then
Z
is a smooth point of
B
and
\dim(B)=h^{1,0}(Z)
, since the normal bundle of
Z
in
X
is isomorphic to

T^{*}Z

[
Vo1
]
.
A Zariski open subset
M^{0}
of
M
parametrizes pairs
(Z^{\prime},\iota_{*}L^{\prime})
, where
Z^{\prime}
is a smooth lagrangian subvariety of
X
and

L^{\prime}
is a line bundle over
Z^{\prime}
.
M^{0}
is holomorphic symplectic and the support morphism
\pi:M^{0}\rightarrow B
is projective and a lagrangian fibration, by

[
DM
, Theorem 8.1]
. Being quasi-projective,
M^{0}
admits an ample divisor class, which restricts non-trivially to the projective fibers of
\pi
, as well as the pull back of an ample class from
B
.
In particular, the rank of the Neron-Severi group of any compactification
\bar{M}
of
M^{0}

is at least
2
.

Assume that
M^{0}
admits a compactification
\bar{M}
, which is an irreducible symplectic variety with at worst
{\mathbb{Q}}
-factorial terminal singularities.
Then the moduli space of complex deformations of
\bar{M}
admits a surjective and generically injective period map onto the period domain
\Omega_{\Lambda}
of weight
2
pure Hodge structures on the lattice
\Lambda:=H^{2}(\bar{M},{\mathbb{Z}})
, by
[
BL
]
. In particular, the moduli space of
\bar{M}
has dimension
h^{1,1}(\bar{M})
and
the locus
{\mathcal{L}}
in moduli of
\bar{M}
, arising from varying
(X,Z)
via the above construction, has co-dimension
\geq 2
,
since such symplectic varieties with Picard rank
r
vary in co-dimension
r
in moduli. Hence, the dimension of
{\mathcal{L}}
is at most

h^{1,1}(\bar{M})-2.

The locus
{\mathcal{L}}
is expected
to have the same dimension
h^{1,1}(X)-1
as
\Omega_{\lambda^{\perp}}
, which would imply the inequality
h^{1,1}(\bar{M})\geq h^{1,1}(X)+1
. Only one deformation type of irreducible symplectic varieties with second Betti number
\geq 24
is known, namely O’Grady’s desingularization of a
10
dimensional moduli space of sheaves on a
K3
surface

[
O3
,
Ra
]
.
The latter is related to the above strategy in the papers
[
LSV
,
Vo2
]
.

8.
Effective LLV lines

Let
X
be a projective irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifold.
We say that a line
\ell
in
\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
is
effective
, if
\ell=\ell(F)
for an object
F
in
D^{b}(X)
,
such that

F
has a rank
1
cohomological obstruction map
(Definition
6.11
).
We say that a line
\ell
in
\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
is
potentially effective
,
if there exists a projective irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifold
Y
and a derived parallel transport operator

\tilde{\phi}:\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow\tilde{H}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})
, such that
\tilde{\phi}(\ell)
is effective.
Clearly, if
\ell
is potentially effective, then so is every line in the orbit of
\ell
under the derived monodromy group. Theorem
3.4
makes the following plausible.

Conjecture 8.1
.

A line
\ell
in
\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
is effective, if it is potentially effective and is contained in
\tilde{H}^{0,0}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
.

Assume that
X
is of
K3^{[2]}
-type.
Choose a class
\delta\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
of divisibility
2
satisfying
(\delta,\delta)=-2
.
Set
B_{\lambda}:=\exp(e_{\lambda})
, for
\lambda\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
.
Set

(8.1)

\Lambda_{X}:=B_{-\delta/2}({\mathbb{Z}}\alpha+H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})+{\mathbb{Z}}\beta)\subset\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}).

The subgroup
\Lambda_{X}
of
\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
is independent of the choice of
\delta
. We refer to
\Lambda_{X}
as the
integral LLV lattice
, since it is invariant under the derived monodromy group of
X
, by
[
Ta
, Theorem 9.8]
.

Remark 8.2
.

Assume that
X
is of
K3^{[n]}
-type.
Choose a primitive class
\delta\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
of divisibility
2n-2
satisfying
(\delta,\delta)=2-2n
.
Define
\Lambda_{X}
as in Equation (
8.1
).

(1)

Let us show that the lattice
\Lambda_{X}
depends only on the

{\rm Mon}(X)
-orbit of
\delta
. Notice that
B_{-\delta/2}(H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})+{\mathbb{Z}}\beta)=H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})+{\mathbb{Z}}\beta
and

B_{-\delta/2}(\alpha)=\alpha-\delta/2+\frac{1-n}{4}\beta
. Given
g\in O(H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}}))
we get that

B_{-g(\delta)/2}(\alpha)-B_{-\delta/2}(\alpha)=(\delta-g(\delta))/2
. It suffices to show that the latter difference belongs to
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
.
Indeed, the discriminant group
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})^{*}/H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
is cyclic generated by
\delta/(2n-2)
and the coset
\delta/2+H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
is
invariant under
{\rm Mon}(X)
, as the monodromy action on
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
factors through a (surjective) homomorphism onto
the subgroup of

O^{+}(H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}}))
of elements acting on the discriminant group by
\pm 1
, by
[
Ma2
, Theorem 1.2 and Lemma 4.2]
.

(2)

The lattice
\Lambda_{X}
is invariant under
{\rm Mon}(X)
, by part (
1
).
Indeed, we have the equalities
g\circ B_{-\delta/2}=(g\circ B_{-\delta/2}\circ g^{-1})\circ g=B_{-g(\delta)/2}\circ g
, which takes
{\mathbb{Z}}\alpha\oplus H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}\beta
to
\Lambda_{X}

if
g\in{\rm Mon}(X)
.
When
X=S^{[n]}
, for some
K3
-surface
S
, then

\Lambda_{X}
is
DMon(S)
-invariant, by Theorem
12.2
, as well as with respect to
the auto-equivalence
\Phi_{\chi}^{[n]}
which is the
BKR
-conjugate of tensorization with the sign character of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
(Lemma

12.1
). Hence,
\Lambda_{X}
is invariant by the subgroup of
DMon(S^{[n]})
generated by these three types of elements.

Assume that
X
is of
K3^{[2]}
-type.
A rank
1
sublattice of
\Lambda_{X}
determines a unique orbit of primitive elements in

\Lambda_{X}
for the action of the
derived monodromy group
DMon(X)
.

Question 8.3
.

Which primitive elements of
\Lambda_{X}
span potentially effective lines in
\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
?

As is well known, the positive cone
{\mathcal{C}}^{+}:=\{x\in\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{R}})\ :\ (x,x)>0\}
is homotopic to the
3
-sphere (see for example
[
Ma3
, Sec. 4]
).

DMon(X)
contains the index
2
subgroup
O^{+}(\Lambda_{X})
of
O(\Lambda_{X})
acting trivially on
H^{3}({\mathcal{C}}^{+},{\mathbb{Z}})
, by
[
Ta
, Theorem 9.8]
.
It follows that the self intersection
(x,x)
and the divisibility of a class
x\in\Lambda_{X}
determine the
DMon(X)
-orbit of
x
,
by
[
GHS
, Cor. 3.7]
.

We have seen that the primitive isotropic element
\beta
of divisibility
1
is effective, by Example
6.16
.
The element
0\alpha+\lambda+0\beta
is potentially effective, for every primitive isotropic class
\lambda\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
, by Example
3.11
(
1
).
The latter two examples represent the same
DMon(X)
-orbit, since every primitive isotropic class in
\Lambda_{X}
has divisibility
1
(see for Example
[
Ma5
, Lemma 2.5]
).
The primitive element

2\alpha+\frac{5}{2}\beta
of self intersection
-10
and divisibility
2
is effective, by Example
6.17
of the trivial line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
.
The class
\lambda+3\beta
is potentially effective, for
\lambda\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
primitive of divisibility
2
with
(\lambda,\lambda)=-10
, by Example
7.4
of a lagrangian
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
.
The latter two examples represent the same
DMon(X)
-orbit.
The class
\lambda-3\beta
, for
\lambda\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
primitive of divisibility
2
with
(\lambda,\lambda)=6
is potentially effective,
by Example
7.4
of the lagrangian Fano variety of lines on a cubic threefold and Lemma
7.3
. So is
the class
\lambda-3\beta
, for
\lambda\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
primitive of divisibility
1
with
(\lambda,\lambda)=2
,
by Example
7.4
of the lagrangian fixed locus of an anti-symplectic involution and Lemma
7.3
. Reference
[
CMP
]
may lead to more effective classes associated to singular lagrangian surfaces (see Remark
7.8
).

9.
The Chern character of a very modular vector bundle isomorphic to
\Phi({\mathcal{O}}_{K3^{[2]}})

We calculate the Chern character of a very modular vector bundle obtained from the structure sheaf of
X
of
K3^{[2]}
-type via
equivalences of derived categories and deformations. The higher Chern classes of
F
are determined by its rank and first Chern class and the values of the latter two are constrained.

Let
X
be of
K3^{[2]}
-type.
Let
\Lambda_{X}
be its integral LLV lattice and let
B_{-\delta/2}
be the isometry of
\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
both given in (
8.1
).
We have

(9.1)

\displaystyle B_{-\delta/2}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\delta-\frac{1}{4}\beta,

\displaystyle B_{-\delta/2}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda-\frac{(\lambda,\delta)}{2}\beta,\ \ \ \forall\lambda\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Q}}),\ \ \ \mbox{and}

\displaystyle B_{-\delta/2}(\beta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta.

Note the equality

(9.2)

B_{-\delta/2}(H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})+{\mathbb{Z}}\beta)=H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})+{\mathbb{Z}}\beta.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
F
be a vector bundle over
X
which is obtained via a sequence of equivalences of derived categories and deformations from the structure sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}
, where
Y
is of
K3^{[2]}
-type as well.
Then
{\rm rank}(F)=r_{0}^{2}
, for some integer
r_{0}
. Set
h:=c_{1}(F)
. Then
h=\frac{r_{0}}{\gcd(r_{0},2)}\eta
for an integral class
\eta\in H^{1,1}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
. The class
\eta
satisfies

(9.3)

\displaystyle r_{0}\mid(5+2(\eta,\eta))

if

\displaystyle r_{0}\ \mbox{is}\ \mbox{odd},

(9.4)

\displaystyle r_{0}\mid\left(5+\frac{(\eta,\eta)}{2}\right)

if

\displaystyle r_{0}\ \mbox{is}\ \mbox{even}.

Furthermore,
\Psi(v(F))=\frac{1}{8}(\gamma^{2}+10\tilde{q})
, where

(9.5)

\gamma=2r_{0}\alpha+2h/r_{0}+\left(\frac{5r_{0}^{2}+2(h,h)}{2r_{0}^{3}}\right)\beta

is a primitive class of divisibility
2
in the lattice
\Lambda_{X}

satisfying
(\gamma,\gamma)=-10
. Consequently,

\displaystyle ch_{2}(F)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{h^{2}}{2r_{0}^{2}}+\frac{1-r_{0}^{2}}{24}c_{2}(X),

\displaystyle ch_{3}(F)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{h^{3}}{6r_{0}^{4}}+\frac{(1-r_{0}^{2})}{24r_{0}^{2}}hc_{2}(X),

\displaystyle ch_{4}(F)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4(h,h)^{2}+20r_{0}^{2}(1-r_{0}^{2})(h,h)+25r_{0}^{4}-46r_{0}^{6}+21r_{0}^{8}}{32r_{0}^{6}}[pt],

(9.6)

\displaystyle\chi(F)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4(h,h)^{2}+20(h,h)r_{0}^{2}(r_{0}^{2}+1)+25r_{0}^{4}(r_{0}^{4}+1)+46r_{0}^{6}}{32r_{0}^{6}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8r_{0}^{6}}\left((h,h)+\frac{5r_{0}^{2}(r_{0}^{2}+1)}{2}-r_{0}^{3}\right)\left((h,h)+\frac{5r_{0}^{2}(r_{0}^{2}+1)}{2}+r_{0}^{3}\right).

Proof.

Let
\Lambda_{Y}
be the integral LLV lattice of
Y
, given in (
8.1
). The line
\ell({\mathcal{O}}_{Y})
is spanned by the class
4\alpha+5\beta
, by Example
6.17
.
We have
(4\alpha+5\beta)=B_{-\delta/2}(4\alpha+2\delta+4\beta)
and

2\alpha+\delta+2\beta
is a primitive class in
{\mathbb{Z}}\alpha+H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})+{\mathbb{Z}}\beta
of divisibility
2
.
Then
\gamma_{0}:=2\alpha+\frac{5}{2}\beta
is a primitive class of divisibility
2
in
\Lambda_{Y}

satisfying
(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{0})=-10
.
Let
\widetilde{H}(\phi):\Lambda_{Y}\rightarrow\Lambda_{X}
be the
integral isometry associated to a
derived parallel transport operator
\phi:H^{*}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
, which is induced by
a sequence of equivalences of derived categories and deformations used to obtain
F
from
{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}

(see Theorem
5.3
).
Set
\gamma:=(\widetilde{H}(\phi))(\gamma_{0})
. Then
\gamma
is a primitive class of divisibility
2
in
\Lambda_{X}

satisfying
(\gamma,\gamma)=-10
, since
\phi
is an isometry.
Note that

H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
is contained in
\Lambda_{X}

and the component in
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
of a class in
\Lambda_{X}
belongs to
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})+{\mathbb{Z}}\left(\frac{\delta}{2}\right)
,
by Equations (
9.1
) and (
9.2
).
Write
\gamma=r\alpha+\lambda+s\beta
, with
r,s\in{\mathbb{Q}}
and
\lambda\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})+{\mathbb{Z}}\left(\frac{\delta}{2}\right)
.
Then
r=-(\gamma,\beta)
is an even integer by the divisibility of
\gamma
.
Set
r_{0}:=r/2
.
Furthermore,
s
belongs to
\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{Z}}
, since

(\gamma,B_{-\delta/2}(\alpha))=(\gamma,\alpha-\delta/2-\beta/4)=-s+\frac{r_{0}}{2}-(\lambda,\delta/2)
is an even integer and

(\lambda,\delta/2)
belongs to
\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{Z}}
.
We have
v(F)=\phi(v({\mathcal{O}}_{Y}))
and the equality

(9.7)

\epsilon(\widetilde{H}(\phi))\Psi(v(F))=\frac{1}{8}(\gamma^{2}+10\tilde{q})=\frac{1}{8}\left(r^{2}\alpha^{2}+\lambda^{2}+s^{2}\beta^{2}+2r\lambda\alpha+2rs\alpha\beta+2s\lambda\beta+10\tilde{q}\right),

by Equation (
6.24
) and Theorem
5.3
.
The coefficient of
\alpha^{2}
in
\Psi(v(F))
is
{\rm rank}(F)/2
and the coefficient of
\alpha^{2}
in the right hand side is

r^{2}/8
, so
\epsilon(\widetilde{H}(\phi))=1
(by Remark
5.4
) and
{\rm rank}(F)=r_{0}^{2}
.

Let
h=c_{1}(F)
. Note that
\Psi(h)=\Psi(e_{h}(1))=e_{h}(\alpha^{2}/2)=h\alpha
.
The coefficient in
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
of
\alpha
in the left hand side of (
9.7
) is
h
and the coefficient on the right hand side is
(r_{0}/2)\lambda
. Hence,
h=(r_{0}/2)\lambda
.
We have

-10=(\gamma,\gamma)=-4r_{0}s+\frac{4(h,h)}{r_{0}^{2}}.

So
s=\frac{5}{2r_{0}}+\frac{(h,h)}{r_{0}^{3}}=\frac{5r_{0}^{2}+2(h,h)}{2r_{0}^{3}},
and

\gamma=2r_{0}\alpha+\frac{2}{r_{0}}h+\frac{5r_{0}^{2}+2(h,h)}{2r_{0}^{3}}\beta,

verifying Equation (
9.5
).
The above displayed formula yields

\gamma=B_{-\delta/2}\left(2r_{0}\alpha+\frac{2}{r_{0}}h+r_{0}\delta+\left[\frac{5r_{0}^{2}+2(h,h)-2r_{0}^{4}}{2r_{0}^{3}}+\frac{(h,\delta)}{r_{0}}\right]\beta\right).

We see that
\frac{2}{r_{0}}h
is an integral class. Hence,
\frac{(h,\delta)}{r_{0}}
is an integer, since
\delta
has divisibility
2
, and
so
2r_{0}^{3}
divides
5r_{0}^{2}+2(h,h)-2r_{0}^{4}
.

If
r_{0}
is odd, then
h=r_{0}\eta
, for
\eta\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
, and
r_{0}^{3}
divides
r_{0}^{2}(5+2(\eta,\eta))
, so
Equation (
9.3
) holds. If

r_{0}
is even,
then
h=(r_{0}/2)\eta,
for
\eta\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
, and
r_{0}^{3}
divides
r_{0}^{2}(5+(\eta,\eta)/2)
, so Equation (
9.4
) holds.

Let us first calculate
\kappa(F)=\exp(-h/r_{0}^{2})(ch(F))
. Set
\kappa(\gamma):=\exp(-h/r_{0}^{2})(\gamma)=2r_{0}\alpha+\frac{5}{2r_{0}}\beta
. Equation (
9.7
) yields the first equality below

8\Psi(\kappa(F)\sqrt{td_{X}})=\kappa(\gamma)^{2}+10\tilde{q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq-tilde-q-in-terms-of-tilde-b-X})}}{{=}}4r_{0}^{2}\alpha^{2}+\frac{25}{4r_{0}^{2}}\beta^{2}+\frac{46}{5}(\tilde{b}_{X}+\alpha\beta).

Hence,
8\kappa(F)\sqrt{td_{X}}=8r_{0}^{2}+\frac{46}{5}b_{X}+\frac{25}{4r_{0}^{2}}[pt]
, by Equation (
6.22
), and using
\frac{46}{5}b_{X}=\frac{1}{3}c_{2}(X)
, established in Equation (
6.21
), we get

\kappa(F)=(\sqrt{td_{X}})^{-1}\left(r_{0}^{2}+\frac{1}{24}c_{2}(X)+\frac{25}{32r_{0}^{2}}[pt]\right).

Now,
(\sqrt{td_{X}})^{-1}=1-\frac{1}{24}c_{2}(X)+\frac{21}{32}[pt]
and we get

(9.8)

\kappa(F)=r_{0}^{2}+\frac{1-r_{0}^{2}}{24}c_{2}(X)+\frac{1}{32}\left(21r_{0}^{2}+\frac{25}{r_{0}^{2}}-46\right)[pt].

Multiplying both sides by
1+\frac{h}{r_{0}^{2}}+\frac{h^{2}}{2r_{0}^{4}}+\frac{h^{3}}{6r_{0}^{6}}+\frac{h^{4}}{24r_{0}^{8}}
and using
h^{4}=3(h,h)^{2}
and
\int_{X}c_{2}(X)h^{2}=30(h,h)
we get

\displaystyle ch(F)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r_{0}^{2}+h+\left[\frac{h^{2}}{2r_{0}^{2}}+\frac{1-r_{0}^{2}}{24}c_{2}(X)\right]+\left[\frac{h^{3}}{6r_{0}^{4}}+\frac{(1-r_{0}^{2})}{24r_{0}^{2}}hc_{2}(X)\right]+

\displaystyle\frac{4(h,h)^{2}+20r_{0}^{2}(1-r_{0}^{2})(h,h)+25r_{0}^{4}-46r_{0}^{6}+21r_{0}^{8}}{32r_{0}^{6}}[pt].

Formula (
9.6
) now follows, by Grothendieck-Riemann-Roch.
∎

Remark 9.2
.

The determination of the Chern character of
F
in
Lemma
9.1

in terms of
{\rm rank}(F)
and
c_{1}(F)
agrees with the computation of
ch_{2}(F)
in O’Grady’s
[
O2
, Prop. 5.8]
.
The formula (
9.6
) for
\chi(F)
agrees with
[
Hu4
, Example 7]
, when
r_{0}=1
.
When
r_{0}=2
and
(h,h)=6
it yields
\chi(F)=6
, as in the case of the Fano variety of lines on a cubic fourfold
[
O2
, Sec. 2.1]
.
When
r_{0}=2
and
(h,h)=22
it yields
\chi(F)=10
, as in the case of Debarre-Voisin varieties
[
O2
, Remark 8.2]
.
More generally, whenever
F
is generated by global sections and has vanishing higher cohomology, we get a morphism
\varphi_{F}:X\rightarrow Gr(r_{0}^{2},\chi(F))
. When
(X,F)=(S^{[2]},G^{[2]})
, as in Theorem
1.4
, and the vector bundle
G
on the
K3
surface
S
has vanishing higher cohomology, then
the vector bundle
G^{[2]}
has vanishing higher cohomology, by the fact that
BKR
is an equivalence and Equation (
4.6
), and formula (
9.6
) agrees with the fact that

\chi(G^{[2]})=\dim H^{0}(S^{[2]},G^{[2]})=\dim{\rm Sym}^{2}(H^{0}(S,G)).

Formula (
9.6
) and the integrality of
\chi(F)
imply that

(h,h)+\frac{5r_{0}^{2}(r_{0}^{2}+1)}{2}
is an odd multiple of
r_{0}^{3}
. In particular,

(h,h)\equiv r_{0}^{3}-\frac{5r_{0}^{2}(r_{0}^{2}+1)}{2}\ (\mbox{mod}\ 2r_{0}^{3}).

The latter congruence relation and the equality
h=\frac{r_{0}}{\gcd(r_{0},2)}\eta
of Lemma
9.1
imply the congruence relations involving
r_{0}
and
(\eta,\eta)
, which are conjectured in
[
O2
, Remark 1.6]
.

Remark 9.3
.

Let us relate Lemma
9.1

to the lagrangian surfaces in Example
3.11
.
The total Segre class of an object in
D^{b}(X)
is the inverse of its total Chern class.
The second Segre class of the vector bundle
F
is Lemma
9.1
is

s_{2}(F)=\frac{r_{0}^{2}+1}{2r_{0}^{2}}h^{2}-\frac{r_{0}^{2}-1}{24}c_{2}(X)

and it is proportional to the lagrangian class associated in Lemma
7.5
to the class
\eta=\frac{\gcd(2,r_{0})}{r_{0}}h
, if and only if

(h,h)=\frac{5r_{0}^{2}(r_{0}^{2}-1)}{2(r_{0}^{2}+1)}
. The only integral solutions of the latter with positive
r_{0}
are

(r_{0},(\eta,\eta))\in\{(1,0),(2,6),(3,2)\},
and
\chi(F)=3,6,10
, respectively, by (
9.6
).
In all three cases
s_{2}(F)
is the primitive lagrangian class in Lemma
7.5
.
In the first two cases the generic such
F
is generated by global sections, and so
s_{2}(F)=c_{2}(E)
, where
E
is the kernel of the evaluation map
ev
in the short exact sequence

(9.9)

0\rightarrow E\rightarrow H^{0}(X,F)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ev}}{{\rightarrow}}F\rightarrow 0,

E
has rank
\chi(F)-r_{0}^{2}=2
, and the zero loci of regular sections of
E^{*}
are the lagrangian surfaces in Example
3.11
(
1
),(
2
). In the third case of

(r_{0},(\eta,\eta))=(3,2)

the lagrangian class associated to
\eta
in Lemma
7.5
corresponds to the surface
Z
of fixed points of the anti-symplectic involution in Example
3.11
(
3
), but in this case
the kernel of
ev
has rank
1
,
and so
F
is not generated by global sections, since
s_{2}(F)
does not vanish.
Finally, let
L
be the line bundle with
c_{1}(L)=\eta
of BBF degree
2
, and let
E_{L}
be the kernel of the evaluation homomorphism

H^{0}(X,L)\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\rightarrow L
. The fixed locus
Z
of the anti-symplectic involution is the set theoretic degeneracy locus
of the differential
d\varphi_{L}:TX\rightarrow{\mathcal{H}}om(E_{L},L)
of the morphism from
X
to
{\mathbb{P}}H^{0}(X,L)^{*}\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
exhibiting
X
as a double cover of an EPW sextic. The class of the scheme theoretic degeneracy locus is

c_{2}(TX\rightarrow{\mathcal{H}}om(E_{L},L))
, by the Thom-Porteous formula, and is equal to
3[Z]
.

Example 9.4
.

Let
S
be a
K3
surface and

X=S^{[n]}
. Let
F
be the vector bundle
corresponding via the BKR equivalence to

G\boxtimes G\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes G\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\chi^{i}
, for a rigid slope-stable vector bundle
G
on
S
.
If
n=2
, Lemma
9.1
applies to the vector bundle
F
,
by Remark
1.5
. We use Lemma
9.1
to show that
\ell(F)
is spanned by

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccl}r_{0}^{2}\alpha+\left(r_{0}c_{1}(G)-\frac{r_{0}(r_{0}-1)}{2}\delta\right)+\left[\frac{(c_{1}(G),c_{1}(G))+2}{2}+\frac{r_{0}^{2}}{4}-\frac{r_{0}(r_{0}-1)}{2}\right]\beta&\mbox{if}&i=0,\\
r_{0}^{2}\alpha+\left(r_{0}c_{1}(G)-\frac{r_{0}(r_{0}+1)}{2}\delta\right)+\left[\frac{(c_{1}(G),c_{1}(G))+2}{2}+\frac{r_{0}^{2}}{4}-\frac{r_{0}(r_{0}+1)}{2}\right]\beta&\mbox{if}&i=1.\end{array}\right.

We start with comments for all
n
, which will be used in the following Remark
9.5
to calculate
\ell(F)
for all
n
.
The line
\ell(F)
is invariant under the action of the stabilizer of
\ell(G)
in the isometry group of the sublattice

\tilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Q}})
of
\tilde{H}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}})
, where the isometry group of the former is embedded as a subgroup of the isometry group of the latter via the homomorphism
h
making the diagram below commutative.

\textstyle{DMon(S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{DMon(S^{[n]})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{O(\tilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Q}}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{O(\tilde{H}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}})),}

The top horizontal arrow above is
{}^{[n]}:{\rm Hom}_{K3^{[1]}}(S,S)\rightarrow{\rm Hom}_{K3^{[n]}}(S^{[n]},S^{[n]})

induced by the functor (
5.3
).
The homomorphism
h
is given by

h(g)=\det(g)\cdot(B_{-\delta/2}\circ\iota(g)\circ B_{\delta/2})
, where
\iota(g)
is the extension acting trivially on
{\rm span}\{\delta\}
(see Example
2.1
), and
B_{\lambda}
is
\exp(e_{\lambda})
, by

[
Ta
, Theorem 9.4]
when
n=2
and by Theorem
1.9
for general
n
.
It follows that
B_{\delta/2}(\ell(F))
is a line in the plane spanned by
\ell(G)
and
\delta
.

(9.10)

B_{\delta/2}(\ell(F))\subset\ell(G)+{\mathbb{Q}}\delta.

Now, if the rank of
G
is
r_{0}
, then the rank
r
of
F
is
r_{0}^{n}
and
c_{1}(F)=r_{0}^{n-1}c_{1}(G)+t\delta
, under the inclusion
H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Z}})\subset H^{2}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Z}})
given in (
3.12
), where
t
is an integer
depending on the exponent
i\in\{0,1\}
of
\chi
and
\delta
is half the class of the divisor of non-reduced subschemes.
The line
\ell(F)
is spanned by a vector of the form

r_{0}^{n}\alpha+c_{1}(F)+s(F)\beta
, for some
s(F)\in{\mathbb{Q}}
,
by Theorem
6.13
(
3
).

Assume
n=2
. The coefficient
s(F)-t-\frac{r_{0}^{2}}{4}
of
\beta
in
B_{\delta/2}(r_{0}^{2}\alpha+c_{1}(F)+s(F)\beta)
must be
equal
r_{0}\cdot s(G)
, by (
9.10
), yielding
s(F)=r_{0}\cdot s(G)+\frac{r_{0}^{2}}{4}+t
. Set
\lambda:=c_{1}(G)
. Then
s(G)=\frac{(\lambda,\lambda)+2}{2r_{0}}
and

s(F)=\frac{(\lambda,\lambda)+2}{2}+\frac{r_{0}^{2}}{4}+t.

On the other hand, multiplying the coefficient of
\beta
in (
9.5
) by
r_{0}/2
we get that

s(F)=\frac{5r_{0}^{2}+2(c_{1}(F),c_{1}(F))}{4r_{0}^{2}}=\frac{5r_{0}^{2}+2r_{0}^{2}(\lambda,\lambda)-4t^{2}}{4r_{0}^{2}}.

Comparing the above two expressions for
s(F)
we get that
t^{2}+r_{0}^{2}t+\frac{r_{0}^{4}-r_{0}^{2}}{4}=0,

so that

t\in\left\{-\frac{r_{0}(r_{0}-1)}{2},\ -\frac{r_{0}(r_{0}+1)}{2}\right\}.

O’Grady calculated in
[
O2
, Prop. 5.8]
that
t=-r_{0}(r_{0}-1)/2
if
i=0
and
t=-r_{0}(r_{0}+1)/2
if
i=1
as follows.
Using the notation of (
4.5
) we have the short exact sequence over
\Gamma

0\rightarrow q^{*}F\rightarrow b^{*}(G\boxtimes G)\rightarrow\left[(G\boxtimes G)_{\mid_{\hat{D}}}\right]^{\chi^{i+1}}\rightarrow 0,

where
\hat{D}\subset\Gamma
is the exceptional divisor of the blow-up
b:\Gamma\rightarrow S\times S
centered along the diagonal.
Now
q^{*}(\delta)=[E]
,
c_{1}(b^{*}(G\boxtimes G))=r_{0}q^{*}(\lambda)
, and so

(9.11)

-t={\rm rank}\left[(G\boxtimes G)_{\mid_{\hat{D}}}\right]^{\chi^{i+1}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccl}r_{0}(r_{0}-1)/2&\mbox{if}&i=0,\\
r_{0}(r_{0}+1)/2&\mbox{if}&i=1.\end{array}\right.

O’Grady denotes
F
by
G[2]^{+}
, if
i=0
, and by
G[2]^{-}
, if
i=1
.
Note that
G[2]^{-}
is isomorphic to
(G^{*}[2]^{+})^{*}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(-\delta)
,
by Lemma
13.4
.
Indeed, the above description of
\ell(G[2]^{-})
agrees with that of
\ell((G^{*}[2]^{+})^{*}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(-\delta))

via Lemma
6.15
.

Remark 9.5
.

The argument of Example
9.4
generalizes to calculate
\ell(F)
for
the vector bundle
F

corresponding via the BKR equivalence to

G\boxtimes G\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes G\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\chi^{i}
, for a rigid slope-stable vector bundle
G
on a
K3
surface
S
, for all
n
. Again
c_{1}(F)=r_{0}^{n-1}c_{1}(G)+t\delta
and the coefficient

\frac{1-n}{4}r_{0}^{n}+(1-n)t+s(F)

of
\beta
in the image

B_{\delta/2}(r_{0}^{n}\alpha+c_{1}(F)+s(F)\beta)
must be equal to
r_{0}^{n-1}s(G)=r_{0}^{n-2}\left(\frac{(c_{1}(G),c_{1}(G))+2}{2}\right)
, by (
9.10
).
We get that

s(F)=r_{0}^{n-2}\left(\frac{(c_{1}(G),c_{1}(G))+2}{2}\right)+r_{0}^{n}\left(\frac{n-1}{4}\right)+(n-1)t.

It remains to calculate
t
.
The quotient
\Gamma/{\mathfrak{A}}_{n}
of
\Gamma
by the alternating group is a double cover
of
S^{[n]}
branched over the divisor of non-reduced subschemes

\Gamma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Gamma/{\mathfrak{A}}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}S^{[n]}.

We have the short exact sequence over
\Gamma/{\mathfrak{A}}_{n}

0\rightarrow q_{2}^{*}F\rightarrow\left(q_{1,*}(b^{*}(G\boxtimes\ \cdots\ \boxtimes G))\right)^{{\mathfrak{A}}_{n}}\rightarrow Q\rightarrow 0.

The quotient sheaf
Q
is again supported over the inverse image of the divisor of non-reduced subschemes in
S^{[n]}
.
Away from the locus of co-dimension
2
, where more then two points come together,
Q
is locally free of rank

r_{0}^{n-2}
times the right hand side of (
9.11
). We conclude that

-t=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccl}r_{0}^{n-1}(r_{0}-1)/2&\mbox{if}&i=0,\\
r_{0}^{n-1}(r_{0}+1)/2&\mbox{if}&i=1.\end{array}\right.

10.
Very modular vector bundles isomorphic to
\Phi({\mathcal{O}}_{K3^{[n]}})

We prove Theorem
1.4
in this section.

Proposition 10.1
.

Let
X_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, be compact complex manifolds with trivial Picard groups and let
V_{i}
be a
\theta_{i}
-twisted vector bundle over
X_{i}
or rank
r_{i}
. Assume that
V_{i}
does not have any non-zero subsheaf of rank less than
r_{i}
.
Then the vector bundle
V_{1}\boxtimes V_{2}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes V_{n}
does not have any non-zero subsheaf of rank less than
\prod_{i=1}^{n}r_{i}
.

The proof of the proposition will use the following lemma.

Lemma 10.2
.

Let
X
be a connected compact Kähler manifold with a trivial Picard group. Let
V
be a possibly twisted rank
r
vector bundle over
X
. Assume that
V
does not have any non-zero subsheaf of rank less than
r
. Let
U
be a vector space and
E\subset U\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V
a saturated non-zero subsheaf. Then there exists a subspace
W\subset U
, such that
E=W\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V
.

Proof.

The vector bundle
V
is slope-stable with respect to every Kähler class on
X
.
The determinant of
{\rm Hom}(E,U\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V)
is trivial, since
{\rm Pic}(X)
is trivial. The statement is thus a special case of the
the more general fact that saturated subsheaves of polystable sheaves of the same slope are direct summands.
∎

Proof.

(of Proposition
10.1
). The proof is by induction on
n
. The case
n=1
is clear. Assume that

n>1
and the statement holds for
n-1
. Let
E
be a non-zero saturated subsheaf of
