V:=V_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes V_{n}
.
Denote the rank of
E
by
e
.
There exists an open subset
U
of
X_{n}
, such that the restriction of
E
to
(\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}X_{i})\times\{p\}
maps to a non-zero subsheaf
E_{p}
of rank
e
of the restriction
V_{p}
of
V
, for all
p\in U
. Then there exists a subspace
W_{p}
of the fiber
V_{n,p}
of
V_{n}
at
p
, such that the saturation of
E_{p}
in
V_{p}
is

V_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes V_{n-1}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}W_{p}
and
e=\dim(W_{p})\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}r_{i}
, by the induction hypothesis and Lemma
10.2
. We conclude that there is a
rank
e/\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}r_{i}
subsheaf
W
of
V_{p}
, such that

E
is a subsheaf of
V_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes V_{n-1}\boxtimes W
. But the saturation of
W
must be equal to
V_{n}
, as
V_{n}
does not have non-zero saturated subsheaves. Hence,
E
must be equal to
V
.
∎

Proof.

(of Theorem
1.4
).
Being
H
-slope-stable,
G
is projectively
H
-hyperholomorphic
[
Ve5
]
(see also
[
HSc
]
). Hence,
the triple
(S,H,G)
determines a twistor family
\pi:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and a possibly twisted vector bundle
{\mathcal{G}}
over
{\mathcal{S}}
, such that each fiber
S_{t}
of
\pi
over
t\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
is endowed with a Kähler class
\omega_{t}
and the restriction
G_{t}
of
{\mathcal{G}}
to
S_{t}
is

\omega_{t}
-slope-stable. We may assume, possibly after replacing
H
by another ample class with respect to which
G
is slope-stable, that
{\rm Pic}(S_{t})
is trivial, for a generic
t\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. Then
G_{t}
does not have any non-zero saturated proper subsheaves. Thus, the vector bundle
G_{t}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes G_{t}
over
(S_{t})^{n}
does not have any non-zero saturated proper subsheaves, by Proposition
10.1
.

The isospectral Hilbert scheme
\Gamma\subset S^{n}\times S^{[n]}
is the reduced fiber product of
S^{n}
and
S^{[n]}
over the symmetric product
S^{(n)}
, by
[
Hai
, Def. 3.2.4]
. Hence, we have a relative isospectral subscheme over the base of the twistor deformation with fiber
\Gamma_{t}\subset S_{t}^{n}\times S_{t}^{[n]}
.
Let
b_{t}:\Gamma_{t}\rightarrow S^{n}
and
q_{t}:\Gamma_{t}\rightarrow S_{t}^{[n]}
be the restriction of the two projections.
The Brauer class
\alpha_{t}\in H^{2}_{an}(S_{t},{\mathcal{O}}_{S})
of
G_{t}
is the image of a class
\tilde{\alpha}_{t}\in H^{2}(S_{t},\mu_{r})
,
where
r
is the rank of
G_{t}
, as pointed out in Equation (
3.5
).
The Brauer class
\alpha_{t}^{\boxtimes n}
of
G_{t}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes G_{t}
is thus the image of an
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
-invariant class in

H^{2}(S_{t}^{n},\mu_{r})
, which is the pullback of a class in
H^{2}(S_{t}^{(n)},\mu_{r})
.
Hence, the Brauer class
b_{t}^{*}(\alpha_{t}^{\boxtimes n})
of
b_{t}^{*}(G_{t}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes G_{t})

is the pullback via
q_{t}
of a class in
H^{2}_{an}(S_{t}^{[n]},{\mathcal{O}}_{S_{t}^{[n]}})
. The morphism
q_{t}
is flat, by the proof of

[
Hai
, Prop. 3.7.4 ]
.
The pushforward
q_{t,*}b_{t}^{*}(G_{t}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes G_{t})
is thus a well defined locally free twisted sheaf.
Set

G_{t}^{[n]}:=(q_{t,*}b_{t}^{*}(G_{t}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes G_{t}))^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}.

A non-zero proper saturated subsheaf
E
of
G_{t}^{[n]}
over the Douady space
S_{t}^{[n]}
determines an
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
-invariant non-zero subsheaf
\tilde{E}
of
G_{t}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes G_{t}

of the same rank as
E
, since
q_{t}
restricts to a Galois covering over the complement of the divisor in
S_{t}^{[n]}
of non-reduced subschemes. We have seen that such a subsheaf
\tilde{E}
does not exist. Hence, the vector bundle
G_{t}^{[n]}
does not have any non-zero saturated proper subsheaves. Thus,
G_{t}^{[n]}
is slope-stable with respect to every Kähler class on
S_{t}^{[n]}
.

The paragraph before the statement of Theorem
1.4
establishes that
{\rm obs}_{G^{[n]}}
has rank
1
. Hence, the class
\kappa(G^{[n]})
remains of Hodge type under every Kähler deformation of
S^{[n]}
, by Proposition
3.3
(applied with
\Phi
the identity endofunctor). The same thus holds for the class
\kappa(G_{t}^{[n]})
.
We conclude that
G_{t}^{[n]}
deforms with
S_{t}^{[n]}
to a pair
(X,F)
of a vector bundle
F
over every Kähler deformation
X
of
S_{t}^{[n]}
, by
[
Ma4
, Prop. 6.17]

(applied via an argument identical to the one used in the proof of Theorem

3.4
).

The vector space
{\rm Ext}^{j}(G_{t},G_{t})
is one dimensional, for
j=0
and
j=2
, and it vanishes otherwise. Hence,
the vector space
{\rm Ext}^{j}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}((G_{t}^{[n]},\rho_{\boxtimes}),(G_{t}^{[n]},\rho_{\boxtimes}))
is one dimensional, for
j=2i
,
0\leq i\leq n
, and it vanishes otherwise. We conclude that
{\rm Ext}^{j}(G_{t}^{[n]},G_{t}^{[n]})
is one dimensional, for
j=2i
,
0\leq i\leq n
, and it vanishes otherwise, since
BKR
is an equivalence of categories. Now

{\rm Ext}^{j}(G_{t}^{[n]},G_{t}^{[n]})
is isomorphic to
H^{j}(S_{t}^{[n]},{\mathcal{E}}nd(G_{t}^{[n]}))
and
{\mathcal{E}}nd(G_{t}^{[n]})

is a poly-stable hyperholomorphic vector bundle.
The dimensions of sheaf cohomologies of poly-stable hyperholomorphic vector bundles are constant along twistor deformations, by

[
Ve4
, Cor. 8.1]
. Hence, the deformations of
G_{t}^{[n]}
remain
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
-objects in the sense that
{\rm Ext}^{i}(F,F)\cong H^{i}({\mathbb{P}}^{n},{\mathbb{C}})
. In particular,
{\rm Ext}^{1}(F,F)
vanishes and
F
is infinitesimally rigid.
∎

11.
Very modular vector bundles with isotropic Mukai vector over Hilbert schemes

In Section
11.1
we compute the Chern character of a vector bundle on an irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifold of
K3^{[n]}
-type, which is the Fourier-Mukai image of a sky-scraper sheaf.
The Chern character is determined by the rank and first Chern class of
F
.
Let
S
be a
K3
surface.
In Section
11.2
we prove Theorem
1.6
about the modularity of the BKR images on
S^{[n]}
of equivariant vector bundles on
S^{n}
constructed from
n
vector bundles
\{G_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
on
S
, where all
G_{i}
belong to the same two dimensional component of the moduli space of stable sheaves on
S
.

11.1.
The Chern character of the Fourier-Mukai image of a sky-scraper sheaf

11.1.1.
An example of a Fourier-Mukai image of a sky-scraper sheaf

Let
\Phi:D^{b}(M)\rightarrow D^{b}(S)
be an equivalence of derived categories of
K3
surfaces. Assume that for every point
p\in M
, the image
\Phi({\mathcal{O}}_{p})
of the sky-scraper sheaf of
p
is isomorphic to a
vector bundle of Mukai vector
v

on
S
. Let
\{p_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
be a set of
n
distinct points on
M

and denote by
G_{i}
the vector bundle isomorphic to
\Phi({\mathcal{O}}_{p_{i}})
.
Let
Z\subset M
be the length
n
zero dimensional subscheme of
M
with support
\{p_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
.
Denote by
z
the corresponding point of
M^{[n]}
.
Note that the object
{\mathcal{O}}_{p_{\sigma(1)}}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes{\mathcal{O}}_{p_{\sigma(n)}}
is isomorphic in
D^{b}(M^{n})
to
the sky scraper sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{(p_{\sigma(1)},\dots,p_{\sigma(n)})}
.
The “usual” BKR equivalence is given by the functor

(11.1)

bkr:=\Phi_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma_{M}},\rho}:D^{b}(M^{[n]})\rightarrow D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}(M^{n})

using the same object as in (
4.4
) as Fourier-Mukai kernel, but going in the reverse direction.
We see that the sky-scraper sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{z}
corresponds via
bkr

to the
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
-equivariant object

\oplus_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}\left[{\mathcal{O}}_{p_{\sigma(1)}}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes{\mathcal{O}}_{p_{\sigma(n)}}\right]

over
M^{n}
. The equivalence
\Phi^{\boxtimes n}:D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}(M^{n})\rightarrow D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}(S^{n})

takes the above displayed object to

(11.2)

G_{z}:=\oplus_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}\left[G_{\sigma(1)}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes G_{\sigma(n)}\right]

with its natural
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
-linearization
\rho
.
Let
\tilde{\Phi}^{[n]}:D^{b}(M^{[n]})\rightarrow D^{b}(S^{[n]})
be the composition

\tilde{\Phi}^{[n]}:=BKR\circ\Phi^{\boxtimes n}\circ bkr,

where
BKR
is given in (
4.4
).
Let
F_{z}
be the sheaf over
S^{[n]}
isomorphic to
BKR(G_{z},\rho)
. Then

F_{z}
is isomorphic to
\tilde{\Phi}^{[n]}({\mathcal{O}}_{z})
and hence

{\rm obs}_{F_{z}}
has rank
1
.
Write
v=(r_{0},\lambda,(\lambda,\lambda)/2r_{0})
, so that
r_{0}:={\rm rank}(G_{i})
and
\lambda:=c_{1}(G_{i})
, for
1\leq i\leq n
. Let
S^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\leftarrow}}\Gamma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q}}{{\rightarrow}}S^{[n]}
be the morphisms from the isospectral Hilbert scheme given in (
4.5
). Let
\theta:H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Z}})\rightarrow H^{2}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Z}})
be the homomorphism given in (
3.12
) and let
\delta
be half the class of the divisor of non-reduced subschemes.

Lemma 11.1
.

F_{z}
is a locally free sheaf of rank
n!r_{0}^{n}
, which is isomorphic to
q_{*}b^{*}(G_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes G_{n}),

and
c_{1}(F_{z})=n!r_{0}^{n-1}\left(\theta(\lambda)-r_{0}\delta/2\right).

Proof.

Set
G_{\sigma}:=G_{\sigma(1)}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes G_{\sigma(n)}
. Denote the automorphisms of
S^{n}
and of
\Gamma
associated to a permutation
\sigma
by
\sigma
as well.
The equality
q=q\circ\sigma
and the isomorphism
\rho_{\sigma^{-1}}:G_{\sigma}\rightarrow\sigma_{*}(G_{id})
induced by the linearization give rise to the natural isomorphism

q_{*}b^{*}(\rho_{\sigma^{-1}}^{-1}):q_{*}b^{*}G_{id}\rightarrow q_{*}b^{*}G_{\sigma}.

The invariant subsheaf
F_{z}
of
\oplus_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}q_{*}b^{*}G_{\sigma}
is hence the diagonal embedding of
q_{*}b^{*}G_{id}

into the direct sum.

F_{z}
is locally free, by the flatness of the morphism
q
(see the proof of
[
Hai
, Prop. 3.7.4]
).
The computation of
c_{1}(F_{z})
is carried out in Corollary
12.3
.
∎

11.1.2.
A relative version of the construction

Assume next that
M
is a two dimensional projective moduli space of sheaves with a primitive Mukai vector
v
which are

H
-stable with respect to a
v
-generic polarization
H
on
S
. Assume that a universal sheaf
{\mathcal{G}}
exists over
M\times S
.
Then
{\mathcal{G}}
is the Fourier-Mukai kernel of an equivalence
\Phi_{\mathcal{G}}:D^{b}(M)\rightarrow D^{b}(S)
.
Let
M^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\leftarrow}}\Gamma_{M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q}}{{\rightarrow}}M^{[n]}
be the projections from the isospectral Hilbert scheme of the
K3
surface
M
. The exterior product
{\mathcal{G}}^{\boxtimes n}
over
M^{n}\times S^{n}
admits a natural linearization
\rho_{\boxtimes}

with respect to the diagonal action of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
.
A relative version of the above construction produces the object

\tilde{{\mathcal{F}}}:=({\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma_{M}},\rho)\circ({\mathcal{G}}^{\boxtimes n},\rho_{\boxtimes})\circ({\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma},\rho)

over
M^{[n]}\times S^{[n]}
, which is the Fourier-Mukai kernel of the composition

\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathcal{G}}^{[n]}:=BKR\circ\Phi^{\boxtimes n}_{\mathcal{G}}\circ bkr
. Using
[
P
, Lemma 5(3)]
we get the isomorphism

(11.3)

\tilde{{\mathcal{F}}}\cong R(q\times q)_{*}^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n,\Delta}}\left((b\times b)^{*}({\mathcal{G}}^{\boxtimes n},\rho_{\boxtimes}\right),

which is locally free by the flatness of
q\times q
.

The isospectral Hilbert scheme
\Gamma
is Gorenstein, by
[
Hai
, Theorem 3.1]
, and its dualizing sheaf
\omega_{\Gamma}
is the line bundle
q^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[n]}}(\delta)
, by

[
Hai
, Theorem 3.1, Prop. 3.4.3, comment after Lemma 3.4.2]
.
Hence, the right adjoint
q^{!}
of
Rq_{*}
is
\omega_{\Gamma}\otimes Lq^{*}
and so

BKR^{-1}(\bullet)\cong(Rq_{*}^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}\circ L_{b}^{*})^{-1}(\bullet)\cong(Rb_{*}\circ q^{!})(\bullet)\cong Rb_{*}((\omega_{\Gamma},\rho_{\omega})\otimes Lq^{*})(\bullet),

where
\rho_{\omega}
is the natural
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
-linearization of
\omega_{\Gamma}
. We have

(11.4)

(\omega_{\Gamma},\rho_{\omega})\cong\chi\otimes Lq^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[n]}}(\delta),

since
Lq^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[n]}}(\delta)
, with its induced linearization, does not have an invariant global section, while
(\omega_{\Gamma},\rho_{\omega})
does.
Hence,

(11.5)

BKR^{-1}(\bullet)\cong\chi\otimes(Rb_{*}\otimes Lq^{*})({\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[n]}}(\delta)\otimes(\bullet))=\chi\otimes bkr({\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[n]}}(\delta)\otimes(\bullet)).

We conclude that the kernel of
\Phi^{[n]}:=BKR\circ\Phi^{\boxtimes n}_{\mathcal{G}}\circ BKR^{-1}:D^{b}(M^{[n]})\rightarrow D^{b}(S^{[n]})
is the locally free

(11.6)

{\mathcal{F}}\cong\pi_{M^{[n]}}^{*}\left({\mathcal{O}}_{M^{[n]}}(\delta)\right)\otimes R(q\times q)_{*}^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n,\Delta}}\left((b\times b)^{*}({\mathcal{G}}^{\boxtimes n},\chi\otimes\rho_{\boxtimes})\right).

Equation (
11.5
) and Remark
4.1
yield the isomorphism

bkr({\mathcal{O}}_{z})\cong BKR^{-1}({\mathcal{O}}_{z})
,
for sky-scraper sheaves of points
z\in M^{[n]}\setminus D_{M^{[n]}}
, where
D_{M^{[n]}}
is the divisor of non-reduced subschemes. Consequently,

(11.7)

F_{z}\cong\tilde{\Phi}^{[n]}({\mathcal{O}}_{z})\cong\Phi^{[n]}({\mathcal{O}}_{z}),

for
z\in M^{[n]}\setminus D_{M^{[n]}}
.

Remark 11.2
.

Let
X
and
Y
be two projective irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifolds.
Is every rational Hodge isometry
f:H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow H^{2}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})

induced by an algebraic correspondence? Shafarevich asked this question in case
X
and
Y
are
K3
surfaces. Buskin, building on results of Mukai, proved that every rational Hodge isometry is algebraic
[
Mu2
,
Bu
]
(see
[
Hu4
]
for another proof). Buskin’s approach uses the fact the the universal bundle
{\mathcal{G}}
as above is hyperholomorphic and it deforms over
the graph of the automorphism of the period domain of Kähler
K3
-surfaces induced by an isometry
g:H^{2}(M,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Q}})
associated to
{\mathcal{G}}
. It is natural to expect that both the vector bundle
\tilde{{\mathcal{F}}}
given in (
11.3
) and

{\mathcal{F}}
given in (
11.6
)
have an analogous property and that the techniques of
[
Bu
]
would establish the algebraicity of many rational Hodge isometries between the second cohomologies of irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifolds of
K3^{[n]}
-type. We plan to return to this question in the future.

11.1.3.
The Chern character in terms of the rank and first Chern class

Lemma 11.3
.

Let
X
be of
K3^{[n]}
-type. Let
F
be a vector bundle over
X
which is obtained via a sequence of equivalences of derived categories and deformations from a sky scraper sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{z}
,
z\in Y
, where
Y
is of
K3^{[n]}
-type as well.
Then
v(F)
belongs to
SH^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
. Set
h:=c_{1}(F)
. The rank of
F
is equal
n!r_{0}^{n}
, for some rational number
r_{0}
, and

\Psi(v(F))=\gamma^{n},

where

(11.8)

\gamma=r_{0}\alpha+\frac{h}{n!r_{0}^{n-1}}+\frac{(h,h)}{2(n!)^{2}r_{0}^{2n-1}}\beta

is an isotropic class. In particular,
\ell(F)={\rm span}_{\mathbb{Q}}\{\gamma\}.

Proof.

Let
\phi:H^{*}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow H^{*}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
be the derived parallel transport operator associated
to the sequence of derived equivalences and deformations used to obtain
F
from the sky scraper sheaf.
Let
\widetilde{H}(\phi):\tilde{H}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow\tilde{H}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
be the
image of
\phi
via the functor (
5.1
).
Then
v(F)=\phi(v({\mathcal{O}}_{z}))=\phi([pt])
, the class
[pt]
belongs to
SH^{*}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})
, and

\phi
restricts to an isomorphism from
SH^{*}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})
onto
SH^{*}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})
, by
[
Ta
, Theorem B]
. Hence,
v(F)

belongs to
SH^{*}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})
.

Set
\gamma:=(\widetilde{H}(\phi))(\beta)
. Then
\Psi(v(F))=\epsilon(\widetilde{H}(\phi))\gamma^{n}
, by the equality
\Psi(v({\mathcal{O}}_{z}))=\beta^{n}
of Example
6.16
and the equivariance of
\Psi
with respect to derived parallel transport operators (Theorem
5.3
).
When
n
is odd
\epsilon(\widetilde{H}(\phi))=1
, by definition, and
the fact that the rank of
F
is positive implies that
\epsilon(\widetilde{H}(\phi))=1
also when
n
is even, by Remark
5.4
.
Write
\gamma=r_{0}\alpha+\lambda+s\beta
. Then

\Psi(v(F))=\gamma^{n}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\left(\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}n\\
k\end{array}\!\!\right)r_{0}^{n-k}\lambda^{k}\alpha^{n-k}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}n\\
k\end{array}\!\!\right)s^{k}(r_{0}\alpha+\lambda)^{n-k}\beta^{k}.

The coefficient of
\alpha^{n}
on the left hand side is
{\rm rank}(F)/n!
and on the right hand side it is
r_{0}^{n}
, so

{\rm rank}(F)=n!r_{0}^{n}
.
The coefficient in
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})
of
\alpha^{n-1}
on the left hand side is
h/(n-1)!
, since
\Psi(h)=e_{h}(\alpha^{n}/n!)=h\alpha^{n-1}/(n-1)!
, and on the right hand side it is
nr_{0}^{n-1}\lambda
, so
\lambda=h/(n!r_{0}^{n-1})
.
We have

0=(\gamma,\gamma)=\lambda^{2}-2r_{0}s=\frac{(h,h)}{(n!)^{2}r_{0}^{2n-2}}-2r_{0}s.

We get that
s=\frac{(h,h)}{2(n!)^{2}r_{0}^{2n-1}}
and Equation (
11.8
) is verified.
∎

Lemma 11.4
.

Keep the notation and hypotheses of Lemma
11.3
and assume that
n=2
. Then

\gamma=r_{0}\alpha+\frac{h}{2r_{0}}+\frac{(h,h)}{8r_{0}^{3}}\beta
is a primitive isotropic class of divisibility
1
in the LLV lattice
\Lambda_{X}
given in (
8.1
). In particular,
r_{0}
is an integer,

h=r_{0}\gcd(2,r_{0})\psi
, for a class
\psi\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
such that
2
divides
{\rm div}(\psi)
if
r_{0}
is odd,
\frac{2r_{0}}{\gcd(2,r_{0})^{2}}
divides
(\psi,\psi)
, and

\frac{(\psi,\psi)\gcd(2,r_{0})^{2}}{2r_{0}}\equiv r_{0}
(mod
4
).
Furthermore,

\displaystyle ch_{2}(F)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4r_{0}^{2}}h^{2}-\frac{r_{0}^{2}}{12}c_{2}(X)

\displaystyle ch_{3}(F)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{24r_{0}^{4}}h^{3}-\frac{1}{24}c_{2}(X)h

\displaystyle ch_{4}(F)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\frac{(h,h)^{2}}{2^{6}r_{0}^{6}}-\frac{5(h,h)}{16r_{0}^{2}}+\frac{21r_{0}^{2}}{16}\right][pt]

\displaystyle\chi(F)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{(h,h)+10r_{0}^{4}}{8r_{0}^{3}}\right)^{2}.

Proof.

We have
\Psi(v(F))=\gamma^{n},
where

\gamma=(\widetilde{H}(\phi))(\beta)=r_{0}\alpha+\frac{h}{2r_{0}}+\frac{(h,h)}{8r_{0}^{3}}\beta
, by
Lemma
11.3
. We get that

\gamma=B_{-\delta/2}\left(r_{0}\alpha+\frac{1}{2r_{0}}h+\frac{r_{0}}{2}\delta+\left[\frac{(h,h)}{8r_{0}^{3}}+\frac{(h,\delta)}{4r_{0}}-\frac{r_{0}}{4}\right]\beta\right).

Hence,
r_{0}
is an integer,
\eta=\frac{1}{2r_{0}}h+\frac{r_{0}}{2}\delta
is a class in
H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
, and

h=r_{0}(2\eta-r_{0}\delta)
. Set
\psi:=[2\eta-r_{0}\delta]/\gcd(2,r_{0})
. Then
2
divides
{\rm div}(\psi)
, if
r_{0}
is odd.
Furthermore, the coefficient
of
\beta
in the above displayed formula is an integer equal to

\left[\frac{(h,h)}{8r_{0}^{3}}+\frac{(h,\delta)}{4r_{0}}-\frac{r_{0}}{4}\right]=\frac{r_{0}^{2}[4(\eta,\eta)-4r_{0}(\eta,\delta)-2r_{0}^{2}]}{8r_{0}^{3}}+\frac{2r_{0}[(\eta,\delta)+r_{0}]}{4r_{0}}-\frac{r_{0}}{4}=\frac{(\eta,\eta)}{2r_{0}}.

Hence,

2r_{0}
divides
(\eta,\eta)
. Now,
(\psi,\psi)=\frac{4(\eta,\eta)-2r_{0}^{2}}{\gcd(2,r_{0})^{2}}
and so
2r_{0}/\gcd(2,r_{0})
divides
(\psi,\psi)
. We have

\frac{\gcd(2,r_{0})^{2}(\psi,\psi)}{2r_{0}}=4\frac{(\eta,\eta)}{2r_{0}}-r_{0},

hence,
\frac{(\psi,\psi)}{2r_{0}}\equiv r_{0}
(mod
4
).

We have
\Psi(h)=h\alpha
,
\Psi(h^{2})=h^{2}+(h,h)\alpha\beta
, and
\Psi(h^{3})=3(h,h)h\beta
. Hence,

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r_{0}^{2}\alpha^{2}+h\alpha+\frac{1}{4r_{0}^{2}}h^{2}+\frac{(h,h)}{4r_{0}^{2}}\alpha\beta+\frac{(h,h)}{8r_{0}^{4}}h\beta+\left(\frac{(h,h)}{8r_{0}^{3}}\right)^{2}\beta^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Psi\left(2r_{0}^{2}+h+\frac{1}{4r_{0}^{2}}h^{2}+\frac{1}{24r_{0}^{4}}h^{3}+\frac{(h,h)^{2}}{2^{6}r_{0}^{6}}[pt]\right)=\Psi(v(F)).

Using
c_{2}(X)h^{2}=30(h,h)[pt]
we get

\displaystyle ch(F)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v(F)(\sqrt{td_{X}})^{-1}=\left(2r_{0}^{2}+h+\frac{1}{4r_{0}^{2}}h^{2}+\frac{1}{24r_{0}^{4}}h^{3}+\frac{(h,h)^{2}}{2^{6}r_{0}^{6}}[pt]\right)(1-\frac{1}{24}c_{2}(X)+\frac{21}{32}[pt])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2r_{0}^{2}+h+\left[\frac{1}{4r_{0}^{2}}h^{2}-\frac{r_{0}^{2}}{12}c_{2}(X)\right]+\left[\frac{1}{24r_{0}^{4}}h^{3}-\frac{1}{24}c_{2}(X)h\right]+\left[\frac{(h,h)^{2}}{2^{6}r_{0}^{6}}-\frac{5(h,h)}{16r_{0}^{2}}+\frac{21r_{0}^{2}}{16}\right][pt]

The formula for
\chi(F)
follows, by Grothendieck-Riemann-Roch.
∎

11.2.
Proof of Theorem
1.6

The following will be needed for the proof of Theorem
1.6
.

Lemma 11.5
.

Let
X
be a connected compact Kähler manifold with a trivial Picard group.
Let
V_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, be vector bundles, possibly twisted, but if so then with respect to the same Brauer class.
Assume that
V_{i}
does not have any non-zero saturated proper subsheaf,
1\leq i\leq n
.
Assume that
V_{i}
and
V_{j}
are not isomorphic, if
i\neq j
.
Let
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
act on
X^{n}
by permuting the factors. Choose the linearization
\rho
of

W:=\oplus_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}V_{\sigma(1)}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes V_{\sigma(n)}
permuting the factors of each direct summand.
Then
W
does not have any
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
-invariant non-zero saturated proper subsheaf.

Proof.

The direct summands of
W
are stable, by Proposition
10.1
, are pairwise non-isomorphic, and
W
is polystable with respect to every Kähler class on
X^{n}
.
Let
E
be a saturated subsheaf of
W
. Then
\det({\mathcal{H}}om(E,W))
is the trivial line bundle, since
{\rm Pic}(X^{n})
is trivial.
Hence,
E
is a direct sum of a subset of the direct summands of
W
. If
E
is
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
-invariant, then this subset must be the whole of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
and so
E=W
.
∎

Proof.

(of Theorem
1.6
).
Being
H
-slope-stable, each
G_{i}
is projectively
H
-hyperholomorphic
[
Ve5
]
(see also
[
HSc
]
).
The proof is the same as that of Theorem
1.4
, except that one replaces the use of
Proposition
10.1
by that of Lemma
11.5

and the reference to the paragraph before Theorem
1.4
by that before Theorem
1.6
.
∎

12.
The action of the derived monodromy group of
S
on the LLV lattice of
S^{[n]}

We prove in this section Theorem
1.9

describing the composite homomorphism

(\widetilde{H}\circ^{[n]}):DMon(S)\rightarrow O(\widetilde{H}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}}))
from the derived monodromy group of a
K3
surface to the isometry group of the rational LLV lattice of its
n
-th Hilbert scheme.

Let
\Phi^{[n]}_{\chi}:D^{b}(S^{[n]})\rightarrow D^{b}(S^{[n]})
be the auto-equivalence (
4.8
), which is
BKR-conjugate to tensorization by the sign character
\chi
.
Let

\phi^{[n]}_{\chi}:H^{*}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow H^{*}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}})
be the derived monodromy operator corresponding to
\Phi^{[n]}_{\chi}
.
Let
\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi}):\widetilde{H}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow\widetilde{H}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}})
be the induced isometry via
(
5.2
). Clearly,
(\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi}))^{2}=id
.
Let
\delta\in H^{1,1}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Z}})
be half the class of the divisor of non-reduced subschemes.
Denote by
(r,\lambda,s)
the element
r\alpha+\lambda+s\beta
of
\widetilde{H}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}})

(12.1)

(r,\lambda,s):=r\alpha+\lambda+s\beta.

Lemma 12.1
.

The isometry
\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi})
is
(-1)^{n+1}
times the reflection with respect to the hyperplane orthogonal to
u_{0}:=(0,\delta,n-1)=B_{-\delta/2}(0,\delta,0)
,

\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi})(x)=(-1)^{n+1}\left[x+\frac{(x,u_{0})}{n-1}u_{0}\right].

Proof.

Let
e:\Gamma\rightarrow S^{n}\times S^{[n]}
be the inclusion.
The kernel of the functor
BKR^{-1}
is isomorphic to
(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma})^{\vee}[2n]
, the derived dual
(\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma})^{\vee}
is
\iota_{*}\omega_{\Gamma}[-2n]
, and
\omega_{\Gamma}
is a line bundle, since
\Gamma
is a normal Cohen-Macaulay and Gorenstein variety, by
[
Hai
, Theorem 3.1]
.
So
BKR^{-1}
has Fourier-Mukai kernel
\iota_{*}\omega_{\Gamma}
, while the functor
bkr
given in (
11.1
),
has kernel
\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma}
, each with its natural linearization.
Let
p\in S^{[n]}
correspond to a reduced subscheme supported on the subset
\{x_{1},\ \dots,\ x_{n}\}
of
S
,
x_{i}\neq x_{j}
for
i\neq j
. The restriction of
\omega_{\Gamma}
to the
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
-orbit of
(x_{1},\ \dots,\ x_{n})
is trivial.
Hence,
BKR^{-1}({\mathbb{C}}_{p})\cong bkr({\mathbb{C}}_{p})\cong\left(\oplus_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}{\mathbb{C}}_{(x_{\sigma(1)},\ \dots,\ x_{\sigma(n)})},\rho\right)
, for a linearization
\rho
, which is isomorphic to

\left({\mathbb{C}}_{(x_{\sigma(1)},\ \dots,\ x_{\sigma(2)})},\chi\otimes\rho\right)
by Remark
4.1
, so
\Phi^{[n]}_{\chi}({\mathbb{C}}_{p})\cong{\mathbb{C}}_{p}
.
Hence,
\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi})
maps the line
\ell({\mathbb{C}}_{p})={\rm span}_{\mathbb{Q}}\{(0,0,1)\}
to itself,
{\rm Sym}^{n}(\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi}))
maps

\Psi(v({\mathbb{C}}_{p}))
to itself, and
\phi^{[n]}_{\chi}(v({\mathbb{C}}_{p}))=v({\mathbb{C}}_{p})
. If
n
is even, it follows that
\det(\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi}))=1
, by the equality

{\rm Sym}^{n}(\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi}))\circ\Psi=\Psi\circ[\det(\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi}))\phi^{[n]}_{\chi}]
in Theorem
5.3
.
If
n
is odd, it follows that
\ell({\mathbb{C}}_{p})
is an eigenline of
\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi})
with eigenvalue
1
, as the eigenvalue is
\pm 1
as well as an
n
-th root of unity
by the equality
{\rm Sym}^{n}(\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi}))\circ\Psi=\Psi\circ\phi^{[n]}_{\chi}

in Theorem
5.3
.

Note that the action of
\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi})
is independent of the complex structure of the
K3
surface
S
.
Furthermore,
\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi})
commutes with
\widetilde{H}(g^{[n]}):\widetilde{H}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow\widetilde{H}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}})
, for every
g
in
DMon(S)
, by Equation (
4.9
).
Let
V
be the subspace of
\widetilde{H}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}})
spanned by the
DMon(S)
orbit of the line

\ell({\mathbb{C}}_{p})={\rm span}_{\mathbb{Q}}\{(0,0,1)\}
. The subspace

V
is
\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi})
-invariant and
\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi})
acts on
V
as the identity, if
n
is odd, and
V
is contained in either the
1
or
-1
eigenspace of
\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi})
, if
n
is even.

Assume that
S
admits an elliptic fibration

\pi:S\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and let
E_{1}
, …,
E_{n}
be
n
distinct smooth fibers. The abelian variety

A:=E_{1}\times\cdots\times E_{n}
embeds as a lagrangian subvariety of
S^{[n]}
. Let
\iota:A\rightarrow S^{[n]}
be the embedding.
Set
\tilde{A}:=\cup_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}E_{\sigma(1)}\times\cdots\times E_{\sigma(n)}
.
We get the equivariant embedding
\tilde{\iota}:\tilde{A}\rightarrow\Gamma
into the universal subscheme
\Gamma\subset S^{[n]}\times S^{n}
.
Let
b:\Gamma\rightarrow S^{n}
and
q:\Gamma\rightarrow S^{[n]}
be the restriction to
\Gamma
of the two projections.
Then
\tilde{A}
is contained in the open subset where
b
is an isomorphism and
q
is étale.
Given a line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}
on
S^{[n]}
, then

Lq^{*}(\iota_{*}\iota^{*}{\mathcal{L}})\cong\tilde{\iota}_{*}\tilde{\iota}^{*}(Lq^{*}{\mathcal{L}})
in
D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}(\Gamma)
. Denote the linearization of
Lq^{*}{\mathcal{L}}
by
\lambda
. Then
\tilde{\iota}_{*}(\tilde{\iota}^{*}Lq^{*}{\mathcal{L}},\tilde{\iota}^{*}\lambda)
is isomorphic to
\tilde{\iota}_{*}(\tilde{\iota}^{*}Lq^{*}{\mathcal{L}},\chi\otimes\tilde{\iota}^{*}\lambda)
, by Remark
4.1
, as the irreducible components
E_{\sigma(1)}\times\cdots\times E_{\sigma(n)}
are disjoint for different permutations
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
. Furthermore,

Rb_{*}^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n,\Delta}}\tilde{\iota}_{*}(\tilde{\iota}^{*}Lq^{*}{\mathcal{L}},\chi\otimes\tilde{\iota}^{*}\lambda)
is isomorphic to

\chi\otimes Rb_{*}^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n,\Delta}}\tilde{\iota}_{*}(\tilde{\iota}^{*}Lq^{*}{\mathcal{L}},\tilde{\iota}^{*}\lambda)
, since
b
is an equivariant
morphism with respect to the isomorphism
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
.
It follows that

BKR^{-1}(\iota_{*}\iota^{*}{\mathcal{L}})\cong\chi\otimes BKR^{-1}(\iota_{*}\iota^{*}{\mathcal{L}})
. Thus,
\Phi^{[n]}_{\chi}(\iota_{*}\iota^{*}{\mathcal{L}})\cong\iota_{*}\iota^{*}{\mathcal{L}}
.
Now,
\ell({\mathcal{O}}_{A})={\rm span}\{(0,f,0)\}
, where
f
is the class of a fiber of
\pi
in
H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Z}})
considered as the subspace of
H^{2}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Z}})
orthogonal to
\delta
, by Lemma
6.25
. Thus,

\ell(\iota_{*}\iota^{*}{\mathcal{L}})=\exp(e_{c_{1}({\mathcal{L}})})(0,f,0)=(0,f,(f,c_{1}({\mathcal{L}})))
.
We see that
\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi})
leaves invariant the lines
{\rm span}\{(0,f,s)\}
, whenever
f
is an isotropic primitive class in
H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Z}})
and there is a complex structure on
S
such that
f
is the class of a fiber of an elliptic fibration and there exists a line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}
on
S
, such that
s=(f,c_{1}({\mathcal{L}}))
.
We conclude that
\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi})
acts by multiplication by
1
or
-1

on
W:=\{(0,0,1),(0,\delta,0)\}^{\perp}
.

Assume that the elliptic fibration
\pi
has a section and all its fibers are integral. Then
S
is isomorphic to the moduli space
M_{H}(0,f,0)

of
H
-stable sheaves
F
of rank
0
,
c_{1}(F)=f
, and
\chi(F)=0
, for a suitable choice of a polarization
H
on
S

[
Br
]
. Furthermore, there exists over
M_{H}(0,f,0)\times S
a universal sheaf
{\mathcal{P}}
and

\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}:D^{b}(M_{H}(0,f,0))\rightarrow D^{b}(S)
is an equivalence
[
Br
]
.
Choose the length
n
subscheme
p
of
S
to consists of the
n
distinct points
p_{i}
of intersection of the section with the fibers
E_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
. The Fourier Mukai transform

\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}:D^{b}(S)\cong D^{b}(M_{H}(0,f,0))\rightarrow D^{b}(S)

satisfies
\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}^{[n]}({\mathbb{C}}_{p_{i}})\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{i}}
. Hence,

\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}^{[n]}({\mathbb{C}}_{p})\cong\iota_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
. We conclude that
W
is contained in
V
.

Note that
V
contains the LLV line of the vector bundle (
11.2
). The latter line is not contained in
W
, by Lemma
11.4
. Hence, the co-dimension of
V
in
\tilde{H}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}})
is at most
1
.
Recall that
\ell({\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[n]}})={\rm span}\{(4,0,n+3)\}
, by Lemma
6.18
.
Equation (
4.10
) implies that
\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi})
interchanges the two lines

\ell({\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[n]}})
and
\ell({\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[n]}}(-\delta))={\rm span}\{(4,-4\delta,7-3n)\}
.
Hence,
V
is a co-dimension
1
subspace of
\tilde{H}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}})
. Furthermore,
\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi})
is neither
id
, nor
-id
.
Hence,
\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi})
is either the reflection
R_{V}
in
V
or
-R_{V}
.
The reflection
R_{V}
must map
(4,0,n+3)
to
\pm(4,-4\delta,7-3n)
, as the two have the same self-intersection.
Furthermore,
(4,0,n+3)+R_{V}((4,0,n+3))
belongs to
V
.
If
R_{V}((4,0,n+3))=-(4,-4\delta,7-3n)
, then
(4,0,n+3)+R_{V}((4,0,n+3))=4(0,\delta,n-1)
and so
V=(0,0,1)^{\perp}
.
This contradicts the fact that
V
contains LLV lines of vector bundles of positive rank as in Lemma
11.4
.
Hence,
R_{V}((4,0,n+3))=(4,-4\delta,7-3n)
, and
V=(0,\delta,n-1)^{\perp}
, since

(0,\delta,n-1)=\frac{1}{4}[(4,0,n+3)-R_{V}((4,0,n+3))]
.
When
n
is even we observed above that
\det(\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi}))=1
and so
\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi})=-R_{V}
. When
n
is odd
we observed above that
\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi})
maps
(0,0,1)
to itself, and so
\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi})=R_{V}
.
∎

Let
v=(1,0,1-n)
be the Mukai vector of the ideal sheaf of a length
n
subscheme of a
K3
surface
S
.
Then the co-rank
1
sublattice
v^{\perp}
of the Mukai lattice
\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Z}})
contains
H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Z}})
.
Let
\theta:H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Z}})\rightarrow H^{2}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Z}})
be the homomorphism (
3.12
).
Denote by

\theta:H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow H^{2}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}})
the induced homomorphism as well.
Let
\tilde{\theta}:\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow\widetilde{H}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}})
be the extension mapping
\alpha
to
\alpha
and

\beta
to
\beta
.
Let
\eta:O(\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Q}}))\rightarrow O(\widetilde{H}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}}))
be the homomorphism extending an isometry by acting
as the identity on the orthogonal complement
{\rm span}_{\mathbb{Q}}\{(0,\delta,0)\}
of the image of
\tilde{\theta}
. Explicitly,

\eta_{g}(\tilde{\theta}(x))=\tilde{\theta}(g(x))
, and
\eta_{g}(0,\delta,0)=(0,\delta,0)
, for all
g\in O(\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Q}}))
.

The following was proven for
n=2
in
[
Ta
, Theorem 9.4]
.
Given an element
\phi
of
DMon(S)
denote by
\phi^{[n]}
its image in
DMon(S^{[n]})
via the functor
(
5.3
). Denote the image of
\phi^{[n]}
in
O(\tilde{H}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}}))
via (
5.1
) by
\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]})
. Define
\widetilde{H}(\phi)\in O(\tilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Q}}))
similarly. Set
\eta_{\phi}:=\eta_{\widetilde{H}(\phi)}
, for
\phi\in DMon(S)
.

Theorem 12.2
.

\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]})=\det(\widetilde{H}(\phi))^{n+1}\left(B_{-\delta/2}\circ\eta_{\phi}\circ B_{\delta/2}\right).

Proof.

Recall that
DMon(S)=O^{+}(\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Z}}))
, by
[
Ta
, Prop. 9.2]
.
Let us first verify the theorem for all
\phi\in O^{+}(\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Q}}))
satisfying
\phi(\alpha)=\alpha
and
\phi(\beta)=\beta
.
Note that in this case
B_{-\delta/2}\circ\eta_{\phi}\circ B_{\delta/2}=\eta_{\phi}
.
Such
\phi
belongs to the monodromy group
O^{+}(H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Z}}))
of
S
. Then the image
\phi^{[n]}
of
\phi
in the monodromy group of
S^{[n]}
acts on
H^{2}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Z}})
via
\phi^{[n]}(\theta(x))=\theta(\phi(x))
and
\phi^{[n]}(\delta)=\delta
. This determines the action
SH(\phi^{[n]})

of
\phi^{[n]}
on the subring
SH(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}}))
generated by
H^{2}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}})
.
The action of
\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]})

on the image of
\Psi
in
{\rm Sym}^{n}(\widetilde{H}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}}))
is determined as follows.
If
n
is odd, then
\Psi
is equivariant, by Theorem
5.3
.
If
n
is even, then

\Psi\circ\det(\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}))SH(\phi^{[n]})={\rm Sym}^{n}(\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}))\circ\Psi,

by Theorem
5.3
.
Now
\Psi
is monodromy equivariant with respect to the action of

\eta_{\phi}
on
\widetilde{H}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}})
.
If
n
is odd, the theorem follows for
\phi
.
If
n
is even we get that
\det(\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}))=1
and so

\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}\eta_{\phi}&\mbox{if}&\det(\widetilde{H}(\phi))=1,\\
-\eta_{\phi}&\mbox{if}&\det(\widetilde{H}(\phi))=-1.\end{array}\right.

It remains to verify the theorem for the index
2
subgroup
SO^{+}(\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Z}}))
of
DMon(S)
.
The statement of the theorem for this subroup is equivalent to the statement that the composition

B_{-\frac{\delta}{2}}\circ\tilde{\theta}:\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow\widetilde{H}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}})

is
SO^{+}(\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Z}}))
-equivariant, since
SO^{+}(\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Z}}))
does not have any non-trivial characters, and so must act trivially on the one-dimensional orthogonal complement
{\rm span}\{u_{0}\}
,
u_{0}:=(0,\delta,n-1)=B_{-\frac{\delta}{2}}(0,\delta,0)
, of its image.
Note first the equality

(12.2)

(B_{\lambda})^{[n]}=B_{\theta(\lambda)},

for all
\lambda\in H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Q}})
.
It suffices to prove (
12.2
) for integral
\lambda
, and by varying the complex structure, we may assume that
\lambda
is of type
(1,1)
.
Given a line bundle
L
on
S
, the equivariant line bundle

(L\boxtimes\ \cdots\ \boxtimes L,\rho_{\boxtimes})
over
S^{n}
is a pullback of a line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}
on the symmetric product

S^{(n)}
. We have the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\Gamma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{S^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{q}}
\textstyle{S^{[n]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{b}}
\textstyle{S^{(n)}.}

Hence,
BKR((L\boxtimes\ \cdots\ \boxtimes L,\rho_{\boxtimes}))\cong Rq_{*}^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}(Lb^{*}L\bar{q}^{*}{\mathcal{L}})\cong Rq_{*}^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}(Lq^{*}L\bar{b}^{*}{\mathcal{L}})\cong\bar{b}^{*}{\mathcal{L}}
.
We get that

\displaystyle BKR((F\boxtimes\ \cdots\ \boxtimes F,\lambda)\otimes(L\boxtimes\ \cdots\ \boxtimes L,\rho_{\boxtimes}))\cong Rq_{*}^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}(Lb^{*}(F\boxtimes\ \cdots\ \boxtimes F,\lambda)\otimes Lq^{*}L\bar{b}^{*}{\mathcal{L}})\cong

\displaystyle Rq_{*}^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}(Lb^{*}(F\boxtimes\ \cdots\ \boxtimes F,\lambda))\otimes L\bar{b}^{*}{\mathcal{L}}\cong BKR(F\boxtimes\ \cdots\ \boxtimes F,\lambda)\otimes BKR((L\boxtimes\ \cdots\ \boxtimes L,\rho_{\boxtimes})),

where the second isomorphism is via the projection formula.
The equality (
12.2
) thus follows for
\lambda=c_{1}(L)
from the well known equality

\theta(c_{1}(L))=c_{1}(\bar{b}^{*}{\mathcal{L}})
.

Passing to Lie algebras we get the homomorphism

(12.3)

{}^{[n]}:{\mathfrak{s}o}(\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Q}}))\rightarrow{\mathfrak{s}o}(\widetilde{H}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}}))

and the
equality
e_{\lambda}^{[n]}=e_{\theta(\lambda)}
.
As
B_{\lambda}^{[n]}
commutes with
\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi})
, by Equation (
4.9
),
so does
e_{\lambda}^{[n]}
and hence so does
e_{\theta(\lambda)}
. This agrees with the fact that the eigenspace
V:=(0.\delta,n-1)^{\perp}
of
\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]}_{\chi})

is
e_{\theta(\lambda)}
-invariant, for all
\lambda\in H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Q}})
.

Every non-trivial
24
-dimensional representation of
{\mathfrak{s}o}(\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Q}}))
is isomorphic to

\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Q}})
. Hence there exists an isomorphism

\gamma:\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow V

of
{\mathfrak{s}o}(\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Q}}))
-modules, which must also be an isomorphism of
SO^{+}(\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Q}}))
-representations.
The element
\beta
of
\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Q}})
spans the common kernel of

e_{\lambda}
,
\lambda\in H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Q}})
. Hence,
the element
\gamma(\beta)
must belong to the common kernel
{\rm span}\{\beta\}

of
e_{\theta(\lambda)}
,
\lambda\in H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Q}})
. We may normalize
\gamma
to satisfy
\gamma(\beta)=\beta
.

Let
\tilde{\gamma}:\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Q}})
be the composition

\tilde{\gamma}=\tilde{\theta}^{-1}\circ B_{\delta/2}\circ\gamma
, where
\tilde{\theta}^{-1}:(0,\delta,0)^{\perp}\rightarrow\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Q}})
is the left inverse of
\tilde{\theta}
. Then
\tilde{\gamma}
is
SO^{+}(H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Z}}))
-equivariant (the two actions, via
[n]
and via
\eta
coincide for this subgroup, as demonstrated in the first paragraph of the proof).
Indeed,
both
\tilde{\theta}^{-1}
and
B_{\delta/2}
are
SO^{+}(H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Z}}))
-equivariant and
\gamma
is
SO^{+}(H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Z}}))
-equivariant as it is
SO^{+}(\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Q}}))
-equivariant by assumption.
Hence,
\tilde{\gamma}
must map
H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Q}})
to itself and act on it by
\pm 1
.
Being an isometry,
\tilde{\gamma}
maps the orthogonal complement

U:={\rm span}\{\alpha,\beta\}
of
H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Q}})
to itself.
Furthermore,

\tilde{\gamma}(\beta)=\beta
, and
\tilde{\gamma}
commutes with
e_{\lambda}
, for all
\lambda\in H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Q}})
.
Thus
\tilde{\gamma}(\alpha)=\alpha
, since
\alpha
is the unique isotropic class in
U
which pairs to
-1
with
\beta
.
Finally,
\tilde{\gamma}(\lambda)=\tilde{\gamma}(e_{\lambda}(\alpha))=e_{\lambda}(\tilde{\gamma}(\alpha))=e_{\lambda}(\alpha)=\lambda
. Hence
\tilde{\gamma}
is the identity.
Hence
B_{-\frac{\delta}{2}}\circ\tilde{\theta}
is
SO^{+}(\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Q}}))
-equivariant.
The theorem is thus verified also for
the subgroup
SO^{+}(\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Z}}))
of
DMon(S)
.
∎

Corollary 12.3
.

The vector bundle
F_{z}
in Lemma
11.1
satisfies

c_{1}(F_{z})=n!r_{0}^{n-1}(\theta(\lambda)-r_{0}\delta/2)
.

Proof.

Note that
n!r_{0}^{n-1}(\theta(\lambda)-r_{0}\delta/2)
is
n!r_{0}^{n-1}
times the direct summand of
B_{-\frac{\delta}{2}}(\tilde{\theta}(v))
in
H^{2}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}})
, where
v=(r_{0},\lambda,(\lambda,\lambda)/2)

is the Mukai vector of
G_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
.
Theorem
6.13
(
3
) reduces the proof of the Corollary to that of the equality

\ell(F_{z})={\rm span}\{B_{-\frac{\delta}{2}}(\tilde{\theta}(v))\}
.
Let
\phi
be the element of
DMon(S)
corresponding to the equivalence
\Phi
.
Denote by
\beta_{1}=(0,0,1)\in\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Q}})
the Mukai vector of a point. Then
v=\widetilde{H}(\phi)(\beta_{1})
,
since
G_{i}
is isomorphic to
\Phi({\mathcal{O}}_{p_{i}})
.

The equality
\ell(F_{z})=\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]})({\rm span}\{\beta\})
follows from
Equation (
11.7
).
Theorem
12.2
implies that
the embedding
B_{-\frac{\delta}{2}}\circ\tilde{\theta}:\widetilde{H}(S,{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow\widetilde{H}(S^{[n]},{\mathbb{Q}})
is equivariant up to sign with respect to (it spans a character of)
DMon(S):={\rm Aut}_{K3^{[1]}}(S)
acting via the functor
\widetilde{H}
on the domain and via the functor
\widetilde{H}\circ^{[n]}
on the co-domain. This equivariance explains the third equality below.

\displaystyle\ell(F_{z})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}(\phi^{[n]})(
span{
β
})=

~

H

(
ϕ
^[n])(span{B_-

δ

2

∘

~

θ

(
β
_1)})=
span{B_-

δ

2

(

~

θ

(
ϕ
(
β
_1))}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm span}\{B_{-\frac{\delta}{2}}(\tilde{\theta}(v)\}.

∎

13.
A reflexive torsion free Fourier-Mukai image of a lagrangian line bundle with a positive LLV line

Let
S
be a
K3
surface of degree
6
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{4}
. The Fano variety of lines on a smooth cubic
3
-folds
C
containing
S

embeds as a smooth Lagrangian surface
Z
in the Hilbert scheme
S^{[2]}
.
In Section
13.1
we state
the main result of this section that the images
E_{k}
of the natural line bundles
{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}(k)
on
Z
via a particular Fourier-Mukai auto-equivalence
\Phi^{[2]}_{{\mathcal{P}},\chi}

of
D^{b}(S^{[2]})
are reflexive torsion free sheaves, for
k
sufficiently large, and we compute the LLV line of
E_{k}

(Proposition
13.2
).
The auto-equivalence
\Phi^{[2]}_{{\mathcal{P}},\chi}
is the BKR-conjugate of the auto-equivalence of
D^{b}(S\times S)
induced by tensoring with the sign character
\chi
of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}
the cartesian-square of the spherical twist
\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}
of
D^{b}(S)
with respect to
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
.
In Section
13.2

we describe in more detail the equivalences
BKR:D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}(S\times S)\rightarrow D^{b}(S^{[2]})
and

\Phi_{{\mathcal{P}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{P}}}:D^{b}(S\times S)\rightarrow D^{b}(S\times S)
.
In section
13.3
we provide a local coordinate description of
strict transforms of certain diagonals in the cartesian square
\widehat{S\times S}\times\widehat{S\times S}

of the blow-up
\widehat{S\times S}
of the diagonal in
S\times S
.
In Section
13.4
we provide an explicit complex over
S^{[2]}\times S^{[2]}
representing the Fourier-Mukai kernel of
\Phi^{[2]}_{{\mathcal{P}},\chi}
(Lemma
13.6
).
In Section
13.5
we provide a general criterion
for the Fourier-Mukai image via
\Phi^{[2]}_{{\mathcal{P}},\chi}
of a line-bundle over a lagrangian surface in
S^{[2]}
to be a reflexive torsion free sheaf (Lemma
13.8
).
We then verify that the hypotheses of this criterion are satisfied for sufficiently large powers
{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}(k)
of the natural ample line bundle over the Fano variety of lines
Z
over a cubic
3
-fold. In Section
13.6

we complete the proof of Proposition
13.2
.

13.1.
Fourier-Mukai images of line bundles on the Fano variety of lines on a cubic 3-fold

Let
S
be a
K3
surface. Let
\Delta:S\rightarrow S\times S
the diagonal embedding, and let
\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}:D^{b}(S)\rightarrow D^{b}(S)
be the auto-equivalence with Fourier-Mukai kernel the complex
{\mathcal{P}}
given by

{\mathcal{O}}_{S\times S}\rightarrow\Delta_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
supported in degrees
0
and
1
.
{\mathcal{P}}
is quasi-isomorphic to the ideal sheaf of the diagonal. We will denote
\Delta_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
by
{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta_{S}}
and use a similar abuse of notation for subschemes of
S^{4}
and
S^{[2]}\times S^{[2]}
.

\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}
is the spherical twist with respect to the spherical object
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
. We get the
auto-equivalence
\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}\boxtimes\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}:D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}(S\times S)\rightarrow D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}(S\times S)
of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}
-equivariant
categories.
Denote by
\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}^{[2]}:D^{b}(S^{[2]})\rightarrow D^{b}(S^{[2]})
its conjugate (
4.7
) via the BKR equivalence.
Let
\Phi_{\chi}
be the auto-equivalence of
D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}(S\times S)
corresponding to tensorization with the sign character
\chi
of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}
and let
\Phi^{[2]}_{\chi}:D^{b}(S^{[2]})\rightarrow D^{b}(S^{[2]})
be its conjugate (
4.8
) via the BKR equivalence.
Set

\Phi^{[2]}_{{\mathcal{P}},\chi}:=\Phi^{[2]}_{\chi}\circ\Phi^{[2]}_{\mathcal{P}}.

Let
Q
be a quadric in
{\mathbb{P}}^{4}
and let
C
be a cubic in
{\mathbb{P}}^{4}
satisfying the following.

Assumption 13.1
.

(1)

Q
and
C
are smooth and their intersection
S:=Q\cap C
is smooth.

(2)

S
does not contain any line.

(3)

C
does not have any Eckhardt points, i.e., through every point of
C
pass only finitely many lines on
C
.

Note that
S
is a
K3
surface.
The Fano variety
F(C)
of lines on
C
is a smooth surface, by
[
CG
]
.
Let

(13.1)

g:F(C)\rightarrow S^{[2]}

be the morphism sending a line
\ell
on
C
to the length
2
subscheme
\ell\cap Q
of
S
.
Let
{\mathcal{O}}_{F(C)}(1)
be the pull back of the line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}_{Gr(2,5)}(1)
via the inclusion
F(C)\subset Gr(2,5)
. Set
h:=c_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{S}(1))
and let
\tilde{h}:=\theta(h)\in H^{2}(S^{[2]},{\mathbb{Z}})
be its image via (
3.12
).
Set
Z:=g(F(C))
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}(1):=g_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{F(C)}(1)
.
Keep the notation for elements of the rational LLV lattice as triples introduced in Equation (
12.1
).
Following is the main result of this section.

Proposition 13.2
.

The image
g(F(C))
is a smooth lagrangian surface in
S^{[2]}
and

g_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{F(C)}
has a rank
1
cohomological obstruction map
(Definition
6.11
(
2
)). For all integers
k
sufficiently large the object
\Phi_{{\mathcal{P}},\chi}^{[2]}(g_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{F(C)}(k))
is isomorphic to a reflexive torsion free sheaf
E_{k}
over
S^{[2]}
of rank
45k^{2}
and
c_{1}(E_{k})=-15k\tilde{h}-\frac{45k(k+1)}{2}\delta
. The
sheaf
E_{k}
has a rank
1
cohomological obstruction map
and
\ell(E_{k})
is spanned by
\left(45k^{2},-15k\tilde{h}-\frac{45k(k-1)}{2}\delta,-\frac{45k(k-2)}{4}\right)
. The sheaf
E_{k}
fits in a short exact sequence

(13.2)

0\rightarrow E_{k}\rightarrow\left[H^{0}(Z,{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}(k+1))\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}\right]\oplus\left[H^{0}(Z,{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}(k))\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(-\delta)\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a_{k}}}{{\rightarrow}}F_{k}\rightarrow 0,

where
F_{k}
is a torsion free sheaf of rank
12
and
c_{1}(F_{k})=15k\tilde{h}-6\delta
. The restriction of
a_{k}
to the first direct summand is surjective onto
F_{k}
, while the image of its restriction to the second summand is isomorphic to
F_{k-1}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(-\delta)
.

The proposition is proved at the end of this section. Note that
E_{k}
is the image of
g_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{F(C)}
via an auto-equivalence, hence
\dim{\rm Ext}^{i}(E_{k},E_{k})=\dim{\rm Ext}^{i}(g_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{F(C)},g_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{F(C)})=\dim H^{i}(F(C),{\mathbb{C}})
, where the latter equality follows from
[
Ml
, Theorem 0.1.3]
.
In particular,

E_{k}
is simple, i.e.,
{\rm Hom}(E_{k},E_{k})\cong{\mathbb{C}}
,
\dim{\rm Ext}^{1}(E_{k},E_{k})=10
, and
\dim{\rm Ext}^{2}(E_{k},E_{k})=45
, by Example

3.11
(
2
).
We do not know
16
16

16

We check in Remark
13.13
that
the intersections of
E_{k}
with each of the two direct summands in (
13.2
) do not slope-destabilize
E_{k}
for polarizations in an open subcone of the ample cone.

if
E_{k}
is slope-stable for line bundles in some open subcone of the ample cone. If it is, then Theorem
3.4
would apply and the Azumaya algebra
{\mathcal{E}}nd(E_{k})
would deform with
S^{[2]}
to every irreducible holomorphic symplectic manifold of
K3^{[2]}
-type.

Remark 13.3
.

Assumption
13.1
does not rule out the case where
S
contains a smooth conic and hence admits an elliptic fibration onto the pencil of hyperplane sections containing the conic. In this case
S^{[2]}
admits a lagrangian fibration the generic fiber of which is the product of two elliptic curves. Techniques for proving stability of vector bundles over IHSMs admitting lagrangian fibrations were developed in
[
O2
, Prop. 3.6]
.

13.2.
Explicit description of the isospectral Hilbert scheme for
n=2

Let
S
be a
K3
surface. Let
b:\widehat{S\times S}\rightarrow S\times S
be the blow-up of the diagonal, and
q:\widehat{S\times S}\rightarrow S^{[2]}
the quotient map by the natural
{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}
-action.
The BKR equivalence, given in (
4.4
),

BKR:D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}(S\times S)\rightarrow D^{b}(S^{[2]})

is the composition
Rq_{*}^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}\circ Lb^{*}
,
where

Rq_{*}^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}:D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}(\widehat{S\times S})\rightarrow D^{b}(S^{[2]})
is the equivariant pushforward associated to the homomorphism
{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}\rightarrow\{1\}
, and

Lb^{*}:D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}(S\times S)\rightarrow D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}(\widehat{S\times S})
is the equivariant pullback.
The functor
Rq_{*}^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}
takes an
{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}
-equivariant object
F
to the
{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}
-invariant direct summand
(Rq_{*}(F))^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}
of the object

Rq_{*}(F).

The morphism
(b,q):\widehat{S\times S}\rightarrow(S\times S)\times S^{[2]}
embeds
\widehat{S\times S}
isomorphically onto
the isospectral Hilbert scheme
\Gamma
.
The sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma}
admits a natural
{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}\times 1
linearization
\rho
and
({\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma},\rho)

is the Fourier-Mukai kernel of the functor
BKR
given in (
4.4
).
Its inverse has Fourier-Mukai kernel
({\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma}^{\vee}[2],\rho^{\vee})\cong(\omega_{\Gamma},\rho_{\omega})
in
D^{b}_{1\times{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}(S^{[2]}\times(S\times S))
, where
\rho_{\omega}
is the natural linearization (see
[
Hu3
, Cor. 3.40 and Prop. 5.9]
).
The functor

\Phi_{(\omega_{\Gamma},\rho_{\omega})}:D^{b}(S^{[2]})\rightarrow D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}(S\times S)
is the composition of tensorization by
{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(\delta)
, followed by

R^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}b_{*}\circ\chi\otimes Lq^{*}
, where
\chi
is the non-trivial character of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}
, by Equation (
11.4
).
Indeed,

(13.3)

\displaystyle BKR({\mathcal{O}}_{S\times S},1)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}},\ \mbox{and}

\displaystyle BKR({\mathcal{O}}_{S\times S},\chi)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(-\delta),

as observed already in (
4.6
),
and so

BKR^{-1}({\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}})\cong({\mathcal{O}}_{S\times S},1)
and

BKR^{-1}({\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(-\delta))\cong({\mathcal{O}}_{S\times S},\chi)
, which agrees with the above description of
BKR^{-1}
.

The following lemma describes the involutive auto-equivalence
\Phi^{[2]}_{\chi}:D^{b}(S^{[2]})\rightarrow D^{b}(S^{[2]})
, given in (
4.8
), which is the BKR conjugate of tensorization with the sign character
\chi
.

Lemma 13.4
.

The following isomorphisms holds for every object
E
in
D^{b}(S^{[2]})
and every object
(F,\rho)
in
D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}(S\times S)
.

\displaystyle\Phi^{[2]}_{\chi}(E):=BKR(BKR^{-1}(E)\otimes\chi)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle(BKR\left[\left(BKR^{-1}(E))^{\vee}\right]\right)^{\vee}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(-\delta),

\displaystyle BKR(F,\rho\otimes\chi)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle(BKR(F^{\vee},\rho^{\vee}))^{\vee}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(-\delta).

Proof.

The existence of the second isomorphism is equivalent to that of the first, by setting
E=BKR(F,\rho)
. We prove the second.

\displaystyle[BKR(F^{\vee},\rho^{\vee})]^{\vee}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\left[Rq_{*}^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}(Lb^{*}(F^{\vee},\rho^{\vee}))\right]^{\vee}\cong Rq_{*}^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}((\omega_{\widehat{S\times S}},\rho_{\omega})\otimes Lb^{*}(F,\rho))

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle Rq_{*}^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}\left[\chi\otimes Lq^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(\delta)\otimes Lb^{*}(F,\rho)\right]\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(\delta)\otimes Rq_{*}^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}(Lb^{*}(F,\rho\otimes\chi))

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(\delta)\otimes BKR(F,\rho\otimes\chi),

where the first isomorphism is a definition, the second is by equivariant Grothendieck-Verdier duality, the third by the isomorphism
(\omega_{\widehat{S\times S}},\rho_{\omega}\otimes\chi)\cong Lq^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(\delta)
, the fourth by the projection formula,
and the fifth by definition of
BKR
.
∎

Denote by
{\mathfrak{S}}_{2,\Delta}
the diagonal subgroup of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}\times{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}
.
The non-unit of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{2,\Delta}
acts by
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})\mapsto(x_{2},x_{1},x_{4},x_{3}).

We get the
{\mathfrak{S}}_{2,\Delta}
-equivariant object

{\mathcal{P}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{P}}:=L\pi_{13}^{*}{\mathcal{P}}\otimes L\pi_{24}^{*}{\mathcal{P}}

over
(S\times S)\times(S\times S)
, endowed with the linearization
\rho_{\boxtimes}
acting by permuting the factors. The object
{\mathcal{P}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{P}}
is represented by the complex

(13.4)

{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{4}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\rightarrow}}{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta_{13}}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta_{24}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta}}{{\rightarrow}}{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta_{13}\cap\Delta_{24}},

where
\Delta_{ij}\subset S^{4}
is the subvariety of quadruples
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
satisfying
x_{i}=x_{j}
,

\beta
is the difference of the
natural homomorphisms from each direct summand, and
\alpha
is the direct sum of the two natural homomorphisms.
Note that
\ker(\beta)
is
{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta_{13}\cup\Delta_{24}}
and so the above complex is quasi-isomorphic to the ideal sheaf
{\mathcal{I}}_{\Delta_{13}\cup\Delta_{24}}
.
The integral transform
\Phi_{({\mathcal{P}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{P}},\rho_{\boxtimes})}:D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}(S\times S)\rightarrow D^{b}_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}(S\times S)
is an auto-equivalence, which is conjugated via the BKR equivalence to an auto-equivalence

(13.5)

\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}^{[2]}:=\Phi_{{({\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma},\rho)}}\circ\Phi_{({\mathcal{P}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{P}},\rho_{\boxtimes})}\circ\Phi_{(\omega_{\Gamma},\rho_{\omega})}:D^{b}(S^{[2]})\rightarrow D^{b}(S^{[2]}),

by
[
P
, Lemma 5(3)]
.
Composing
\Phi_{({\mathcal{P}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{P}},\rho_{\boxtimes})}
with the auto-equivalence
\Phi_{\chi}
of tensoring with the sign character
\chi
of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}
we get the auto-equivalence
\Phi_{({\mathcal{P}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{P}},\chi\otimes\rho_{\boxtimes})}
and we denote its BKR-conjugate by

\Phi_{{\mathcal{P}},\chi}^{[2]}:D^{b}(S^{[2]})\rightarrow D^{b}(S^{[2]})
.
The projections
\pi_{i}:(S\times S)\times(S\times S)\rightarrow(S\times S)
,
i=1,2
, are equivariant with respect to the natural isomorphism
{\mathfrak{S}}_{2,\Delta}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}
, and we get
\Phi_{\chi}\circ\Phi_{({\mathcal{P}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{P}},\rho_{\boxtimes})}=\Phi_{({\mathcal{P}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{P}},\rho_{\boxtimes})}\circ\Phi_{\chi}
.
We have

\Phi_{{\mathcal{P}},\chi}^{[2]}=\Phi_{\chi}^{[2]}\circ\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}^{[2]}=\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}^{[2]}\circ\Phi_{\chi}^{[2]},

by Equation (
4.9
).

Lemma 13.5
.

\Phi^{[2]}_{{\mathcal{P}}.\chi}({\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}})\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(-\delta)[-4]
and

\Phi^{[2]}_{{\mathcal{P}}.\chi}({\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(-\delta))\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}[-4].

Proof.

The isomorphism
\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{O}}_{S})\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{S}[-2]
explains the third isomorphism below.

\displaystyle\Phi^{[2]}_{{\mathcal{P}}.\chi}({\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle BKR\left(\chi\otimes\Phi_{({\mathcal{P}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{P}},\rho_{\boxtimes})}(BKR^{-1}({\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}))\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq-BKR-of-structure-sheaf})}}{{\cong}}

\displaystyle BKR\left(\chi\otimes\Phi_{({\mathcal{P}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{P}},\rho_{\boxtimes})}({\mathcal{O}}_{S\times S},1)\right)\cong BKR\left(\chi\otimes({\mathcal{O}}_{S\times S}[-4],1)\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{Lemma \ref{lemma-BKR-conjugate-of-tensorization-by-sign-character}}}}{{\cong}}

\displaystyle\left(BKR({\mathcal{O}}_{S\times S}[4],1)\right)^{\vee}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(-\delta)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq-BKR-of-structure-sheaf})}}{{\cong}}{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(-\delta)[-4].

The proof of the second isomorphism is similar.

\displaystyle\Phi^{[2]}_{{\mathcal{P}}.\chi}({\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(-\delta))

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle BKR\left(\chi\otimes\Phi_{({\mathcal{P}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{P}},\rho_{\boxtimes})}(BKR^{-1}({\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(-\delta)))\right)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle BKR\left(\chi\otimes\Phi_{({\mathcal{P}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{P}},\rho_{\boxtimes})}({\mathcal{O}}_{S\times S},\chi))\right)\cong BKR(({\mathcal{O}}_{S\times S},1)[-4])\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}[-4].

∎

13.3.
A local coordinate description of the strict transforms of diagonals

We will need a local coordinates description of
\widehat{S\times S}\times\widehat{S\times S}
and its exceptional divisors.
We replace
S
by
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
and let
(x_{i},y_{i})
be coordinates on the
i
-th factor of
S
. Let
(s:t)
be homogeneous coordinates on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
.
The blow-up of
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
at the origin is the hypersurface
V(xt-ys)
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. Let
z=s/t
denote the affine coordinate on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\setminus\{(1:0)\}
. Away from

{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\times\{(1:0)\}

the blow-up
has coordinates
y,z
and
x=yz
. The exceptional divisor
\hat{D}
is
V(y)
in these coordinates.
Consider the map

\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{C}}^{2}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{C}}^{2},

\displaystyle((x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2}))

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle((x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{1}-x_{2},y_{1}-y_{2})).

The blow-up of the left copy of
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
along the diagonal corresponds
to the blow-up along
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\times\{0\}
via the above change of coordinates.
Hence,
\widehat{S\times S}
embeds as the subvariety

V((x_{1}-x_{2})t-(y_{1}-y_{2})s)

of
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and has coordinates
x_{1},y_{1},y_{2},z
, and
x_{1}-x_{2}=(y_{1}-y_{2})z
, away from the divisor

{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\times\{(1:0)\}
.
The exceptional divisor is
V(y_{1}-y_{2})
in these coordinates.
Over
\widehat{S\times S}\times\widehat{S\times S}
we get the coordinates

(x_{1},y_{1},y_{2},z_{1};x_{3},y_{3},y_{4},z_{2})

satisfying
x_{1}-x_{2}=(y_{1}-y_{2})z_{1}
and
x_{3}-x_{4}=(y_{3}-y_{4})z_{2}
.
The strict transform
\hat{\Delta}_{13}
of
\Delta_{13}
in
\widehat{S\times S}\times\widehat{S\times S}
corresponds to
V(x_{1}-x_{3},y_{1}-y_{3})
.
The strict transform
\hat{\Delta}_{24}
of
\Delta_{24}
corresponds to
V(y_{2}-y_{4},x_{2}-x_{4})
, where

x_{2}=x_{1}-z_{1}(y_{1}-y_{2})
and
x_{4}=x_{3}-z_{2}(y_{3}-y_{4})
.
The intersection
\hat{\Delta}_{13}\cap\hat{\Delta}_{24}
is given by

\displaystyle\hat{\Delta}_{13}\cap\hat{\Delta}_{24}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle V(x_{1}-x_{3},y_{1}-y_{3},y_{2}-y_{4},(y_{1}-y_{2})(z_{1}-z_{2})),\mbox{where}

\displaystyle\hat{D}\times_{S}\hat{D}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle V(x_{1}-x_{3},y_{1}-y_{3},y_{2}-y_{4},y_{1}-y_{2})

\displaystyle\Delta_{\widehat{S\times S}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle V(x_{1}-x_{3},y_{1}-y_{3},y_{2}-y_{4},z_{1}-z_{2}).

Note that
x_{1}-x_{3},y_{1}-y_{3},y_{2}-y_{4},(y_{1}-y_{2})(z_{1}-z_{2})
is a regular sequence, and so
\hat{\Delta}_{13}\cap\hat{\Delta}_{24}

is a complete intersection. Consider the sections
s_{1}:=(x_{1}-x_{3},y_{1}-y_{3})
and
s_{2}:=(y_{2}-y_{4},x_{2}-x_{4})
of

{\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{S\times S}\times\widehat{S\times S}}^{\oplus 2}
. The Koszul complex associated to
s_{1}

is a locally free resolution of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\hat{\Delta}_{13}}
and the one associated to
s_{2}
is a locally free resolution of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\hat{\Delta}_{24}}
. The tensor product of these two Koszul complexes is the
Koszul complex associated to the section
(s_{1},s_{2})
of

{\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{S\times S}\times\widehat{S\times S}}^{\oplus 4}
, which is exact, as the section
(s_{1},s_{2})
is regular.
Hence, the torsion sheaves
{\mathcal{T}}or_{i}({\mathcal{O}}_{\hat{\Delta}_{13}},{\mathcal{O}}_{\hat{\Delta}_{24}})
vanish, for
i>0
.

13.4.
The kernel of the BKR-conjugate of the square of a spherical twist by
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}

Let
I\subset S^{[2]}\times S^{[2]}
be the incidence variety consisting of pairs of ideals of non-disjoint subschemes.
Let
\nu:\tilde{I}\rightarrow I
be its normalization.
Let
\hat{\Delta}_{ij}
be the strict transform of
\Delta_{ij}
in
\widehat{S\times S}\times\widehat{S\times S}
.
Let
\hat{\delta}:\widehat{S\times S}\rightarrow\widehat{S\times S}\times\widehat{S\times S}
be the diagonal embedding
and denote its image by
\Delta_{\widehat{S\times S}}
.
Let
\hat{D}\subset\widehat{S\times S}
be the exceptional divisor of
b
.
Then
\hat{\Delta}_{13}\cap\hat{\Delta}_{24}=\Delta_{\widehat{S\times S}}\cup\hat{D}\times_{S}\hat{D}
and each of

\hat{\Delta}_{13}
and
\hat{\Delta}_{24}
is naturally isomorphic to
\tilde{I}
. The diagonal involution interchanges
\hat{\Delta}_{13}
and
\hat{\Delta}_{24}
leaving
\hat{D}\times_{S}\hat{D}
pointwise invariant. Thus

\hat{D}\times_{S}\hat{D}
embeds naturally in
\tilde{I}
and we denote its image by
\hat{D}\times_{S}\hat{D}
as well.
Denote by
{\mathcal{I}}_{\tilde{I},\hat{D}\times_{S}\hat{D}}
the ideal sheaf of
\hat{D}\times_{S}\hat{D}
in
\tilde{I}
.
The homomorphism
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tilde{I}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta_{\widehat{S\times S}}}

maps a local function vanishing to order
1
along
\hat{D}\times_{S}\hat{D}
to an anti-invariant function with respect to the diagonal involution.
We have the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow{\mathcal{I}}_{I,D\times_{S}D\cup\Delta_{S^{[2]}}}\rightarrow\nu_{*}{\mathcal{I}}_{\tilde{I},\hat{D}\times_{S}\hat{D}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta_{S^{[2]}}}\otimes\pi_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(-\delta)\rightarrow 0.

Lemma 13.6
.

(1)

The auto-equivalence

\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}^{[2]}:D^{b}(S^{[2]})\rightarrow D^{b}(S^{[2]})
is the Fourier-Mukai transformation with kernel

{\mathcal{P}}^{[2]}:=\pi_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(\delta)\otimes\left[\left\{\pi_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(-\delta)\oplus\pi_{2}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(-\delta)\right\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}\nu_{*}{\mathcal{I}}_{\tilde{I},\hat{D}\times_{S}\hat{D}}\right],

where the homomorphism
a
is surjective and is induced from the restriction homomorphism

{\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{S\times S}\times\widehat{S\times S}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\hat{\Delta}_{13}\cup\hat{\Delta}_{24}}
via push-forward via
q\times q
and taking the
{\mathfrak{S}}_{2,\Delta}
-anti-invariant subsheaves.

(2)

The auto-equivalence

\Phi_{{\mathcal{P}},\chi}^{[2]}:D^{b}(S^{[2]})\rightarrow D^{b}(S^{[2]})
is the Fourier-Mukai transformation with kernel

{\mathcal{P}}^{[2]}_{\chi}:=\pi_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(\delta)\otimes\left[\left\{{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}\times S^{[2]}}\oplus\left[\pi_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(-\delta)\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(-\delta)\right]\right\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a_{\chi}}}{{\rightarrow}}{\mathcal{O}}_{I}\right],

where the homomorphism
a_{\chi}
is surjective and is induced from the restriction homomorphism

{\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{S\times S}\times\widehat{S\times S}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\hat{\Delta}_{13}\cup\hat{\Delta}_{24}}
via push-forward via
q\times q
and taking the
{\mathfrak{S}}_{2,\Delta}
-invariant subsheaves.

Proof.

(
1
).
Step 1:

The kernel of
\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}^{[2]}
, as defined in (
13.5
) using
[
P
, Lemma 5(3)]
, is
isomorphic to

\pi_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(\delta)\otimes R(q\times q)_{*}^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2,\Delta}}\left(L(b\times b)^{*}({\mathcal{P}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{P}})\right),

by the argument below using the following diagram.

\textstyle{\widehat{S\times S}\times\widehat{S\times S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q\times q}
\scriptstyle{(q,b)\times(b,q)}
\scriptstyle{b\times b}
\textstyle{S^{[2]}\times S^{[2]}}
\textstyle{[S^{[2]}\times(S\times S)]\times[(S\times S)\times S^{[2]}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{14}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{23}}
\textstyle{(S\times S)\times(S\times S).}

Above
\pi_{ij}
is the projection onto the product of the
i
-th and
j
-th factors, where each
(S\times S)
is regarded as a single factor of the middle bottom variety in the diagram.
Let
\hat{\pi}_{i}
be the projection from
\widehat{S\times S}\times\widehat{S\times S}
to the
i
-th factor,
i=1,2
.
The object
L\pi_{34}^{*}R(b,q)_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{S\times S}}
is isomorphic to the structure sheaf of

[(S\times S)\times S^{[2]}]\times\Gamma
. Similarly,
L\pi_{12}^{*}R(q,b)_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{S\times S}}

is isomorphic to the structure sheaf of

\Gamma\times[S^{[2]}\times(S\times S)]
. The transversality of these two subschemes yields the isomorphism

13.5.
Reflexivity of the Fourier-Mukai image of
{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}(k)

Let

be a line bundle over

.

Lemma 13.8
.

Assume that the following three conditions hold.

(1)

The projection
\pi_{2}
restricts to the intersection
(Z\times S^{[2]})\cap I
as a finite and surjective morphism onto
S^{[2]}

(13.15)

\pi_{2}:(Z\times S^{[2]})\cap I\rightarrow S^{[2]}.

(2)

H^{i}(Z,e^{*}{\mathcal{L}})=0
and
H^{i}(Z,e^{*}({\mathcal{L}}(\delta)))=0
, for
i>0
.

(3)

Let
d
be the maximal length of fibers of the finite morphism (
13.15
).
The homomorphism
H^{0}(Z,e^{*}({\mathcal{L}}(\delta)))\rightarrow H^{0}(A,{\mathcal{L}}(\delta)_{\mid_{A}})
is surjective, for every zero-dimensional subscheme
A
of
Z
of length
\leq d
.

Then
\Phi^{[2]}_{{\mathcal{P}},\chi}(e_{*}e^{*}{\mathcal{L}})
is represented by a torsion free reflexive sheaf.

Proof.

Note that

\Phi^{[2]}_{{\mathcal{P}},\chi}(e_{*}e^{*}{\mathcal{L}})

is represented by the complex

(13.16)

H^{0}(Z,e^{*}({\mathcal{L}}(\delta)))\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}\oplus H^{0}(Z,e^{*}{\mathcal{L}})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}}(-\delta)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a_{\chi}}}{{\rightarrow}}\pi_{2,*}\left(\pi_{1}^{*}({\mathcal{L}}(\delta))\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{(Z\times S^{[2]})\cap I}\right),

by Lemma
13.6
and
assumptions (
1
) and (
2
). Furthermore, the homomorphism
a_{\chi}
is surjective, by assumption (
3
), and so
\Phi^{[2]}_{{\mathcal{P}},\chi}(e_{*}e^{*}{\mathcal{L}})
is represented by the sheaf
\ker(a_{\chi})
. Each of the irreducible components of
(Z\times S^{[2]})\cap I
is
4
-dimensional, since it has dimension
\geq 4
and is finite over
S^{[2]}
. Hence, the sheaf
\pi_{2,*}\left(\pi_{1}^{*}({\mathcal{L}}(\delta))\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{(Z\times S^{[2]})\cap I}\right)
is torsion free and so
\ker(a_{\chi})
is reflexive, being a saturated subsheaf of a locally free sheaf.
∎

Remark 13.9
.

(1)

The sheaf
\ker(a_{\chi})
is locally free away from a closed subscheme of
S^{[2]}
of co-dimension

\geq 3
as it is reflexive. We claim that this closed subscheme of singularities of
\ker(a_{\chi})
is contained in
Z
. It suffices to show that the morphism
(
13.15
) is flat over
S^{[2]}\setminus Z
, since then the sheaf

F:=\pi_{2,*}\left(\pi_{1}^{*}({\mathcal{L}}(\delta))\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{(Z\times S^{[2]})\cap I}\right)
is locally free over
S^{[2]}\setminus Z
and the kernel of a surjective homomorphism of locally free sheaves is locally free as well.
The length of the fiber of (
13.15
) over
y\in S^{[2]}
is equal to
the length of the intersection subscheme
(Z\times S^{[2]})\cap I\cap(S^{[2]}\times\{y\})
of
S^{[2]}\times S^{[2]}
, which is independent of
y
as long as it is of the expected dimension
0
and
the object
{\mathcal{O}}_{Z\times S^{[2]}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L}}{{\otimes}}{\mathcal{O}}_{I}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L}}{{\otimes}}{\mathcal{O}}_{S^{[2]}\times\{y\}}
is represented by the tensor product sheaf, i.e., all the higher torsion sheaves vanish.
This is the case if the intersection
(Z\times S^{[2]})\cap I\cap(S^{[2]}\times\{y\})
is contained in the non-singular locus of
I
.
Indeed, in that case each of
Z\times S^{[2]}
,
I
, and
S^{[2]}\times\{y\}
is non singular at every point
p
of their common intersection and
their local equations at
p
yield a regular sequence for the subscheme
(Z\times S^{[2]})\cap I\cap(S^{[2]}\times\{y\})
locally around
p
. The Koszul complex associated to that regular sequence is exact on the one hand, and is isomorphic to the tensor product of the three Koszul complexes associated to the local equations of each of the three subvarieties. Hence, the higher torsion sheaves vanish, and the intersection multiplicity at each point
p
is equal to the length of the intersection subscheme at
p
.
We have seen in Step 3 of the proof of Lemma
13.6
that the singular locus of
I
is the diagonal
\Delta_{S^{[2]}}
. Hence,
if
y
does not belong to
Z
the fiber over
y
is contained in the non-singular locus of
I
.
The morphism (
13.15
) is thus flat over
S^{[2]}\setminus Z
, by
[
Har
, Theorem III.9.9]
.

(2)

The sheaf
F
is not locally free over
Z
, hence not reflexive. Indeed, if
y\in Z
and
y
is reduced, then the intersection
I\cap(S^{[2]}\times\{y\})
consists of two smooth surfaces in
S^{[2]}\times\{y\}
meeting at
(y,y)
, and so the intersection of
I\cap(S^{[2]}\times\{y\})
with

Z\times\{y\}
is a subscheme with stalk at
(y,y)
of length at least
3
(while the intersection multiplicity is
2
if
Z
is transversal at
y
to each of the two surfaces), by

[
Har
, Appendix A, Example 1.1.1]
. Nevertheless,
\ker(a_{\chi})
is locally free over the generic point of
Z
, being reflexive.

Lemma 13.10
.

The morphism
g
, given in (
13.1
), is a closed immersion.

Z:=g(F(C))
is a lagrangian surface in
S^{[2]}
and the projection
\pi_{2}
restricts to
(Z\times S^{[2]})\cap I
as a finite and surjective morphism of degree
12
onto
S^{[2]}
.

Proof.

Let
q(x_{0},\dots,x_{4})
and
c(x_{0},\dots,x_{4})
be homogeneous quadratic and cubic forms with zero divisors
Q
and
C
respectively satisfying Assumption
13.1
.
Set

d(x_{0},\dots,x_{4},x_{5}):=x_{5}q(x_{0},\dots,x_{4})+c(x_{0},\dots,x_{4})

and let
X
be the corresponding cubic fourfold in
{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
.
Then
X
has a single ordinary double point at
p:=(0\!:\!0\!:\!0\!:\!0\!:\!0\!:\!1)
.
The Fano variety
F(X)
of lines on
X
is birational to
S^{[2]}
, as observed in

[
Mu1
, Example 4.3]
.
Let
F_{0}(X)\subset F(X)
be the open subset of lines not passing through
p
.
Let
A\subset S^{[2]}
the the divisor of subschemes
y
, such that the line
\bar{\ell}_{y}
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{4}
is contained in
Q
.
Note that
A
admits a morphism of degree
3
onto the Fano variety
F(Q)
of lines on
Q
, and that the latter is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
. Note also that
A
admits a morphism

(13.17)

\phi:A\rightarrow S,

by sending a subscheme
y\in A
to the point of
\bar{\ell}_{y}\cap C
complementary to
y
. In fact,
\phi:A\rightarrow S
is a conic bundle, which is the restriction to
S
of the following conic bundle
\tilde{\phi}:\tilde{A}\rightarrow Q
. Given a point
a\in Q
, let
a^{\perp}
be the hyperplane in
{\mathbb{C}}^{5}
orthogonal to
a
with respect to
q
and denote by
q_{a}
the non-degenerate quadratic form on
a^{\perp}/a
induced by
q
.
Then the fiber of
\tilde{\phi}
over
a\in Q
is the conic in the plane
{\mathbb{P}}(a^{\perp}/a)
defined by
q_{a}
, as the latter conic parametrizes lines on
Q
through
a
.
Let
S^{[2]}_{0}
be the complement of
A
.
Let
\iota:S\rightarrow F(X)
be the embedding of
S
as the singular locus of
F(X)
, sending
q\in S
to the line
\overline{pq}
through
p
and
q
.
The following is a slightly more detailed version of
[
Mu1
, Example 4.3]
.

Claim 13.11
.

There exists a canonical morphism
f:S^{[2]}\rightarrow F(X)
, which restricts to an
isomorphism
f:S^{[2]}_{0}\rightarrow F_{0}(X)
.
Furthermore,
f
restricts to an isomorphism from
Z
onto
F(C)
, which is the inverse of
g
, and
the following diagram is commutative

\textstyle{S^{[2]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{F(X)}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{S.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}

Proof.

Let
\hat{Q}
and
\hat{C}
be the cones over
Q
and
C
given by
q
and
c
considered as forms in
x_{0}
, …,
x_{5}
. A line on
X
passes through
p
, if and only if it is contained in both
\hat{Q}
and
\hat{C}
.
Indeed, if
\ell
passes through
p
then a general point of
\ell
is of the form

(sa_{0}\!:\!sa_{1}\!:\!sa_{2}\!:\!sa_{3}\!:\!sa_{4}\!:\!t)
,
(s\!:\!t)\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
,
so that
d(sa_{0},sa_{1},sa_{2},sa_{3},sa_{4},t)=ts^{2}q(a_{0},\dots,a_{4})+s^{3}c(a_{0},\dots,a_{4})
vanishes for all
(s\!:\!t)
, so that

a:=(a_{0}\!:\!\cdots\!:\!a_{4})
is a point of
S
. Conversely, if
\ell
is contained in
\hat{Q}\cap\hat{C}
, then it must pass through
p
, since otherwise it would project to a line in
S
contrary to our assumption that
S
does not contain any lines.

Let
H
be the hyperplane
x_{5}=0
.
Given a point
y
of
S^{[2]}
, we get the line
\bar{\ell}_{y}
in
H
intersecting
S
along a subscheme containing
y
,
18
18

18

The scheme
\bar{\ell}_{y}\cap S
is equal to
y
, if and only if
y
does not belong to
A
. If
y
belongs to
A
, then
y
is a proper subscheme of
\bar{\ell}_{y}\cap S
, as the latter is equal to
\bar{\ell}_{y}\cap C
and so has length
3
.
hence we get the plane
P_{y}
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
spanned by
p
and
\bar{\ell}_{y}
.
P_{y}
is not contained in
X
, since otherwise it would be contained in
\hat{Q}\cap\hat{C}
, by the above discussion, and so project to a line in
S
.
If
y
consists of two distinct points
a
and
b
of
S
, then
P_{y}
intersects
X
along three lines, two of them are
\overline{pa}
and
\overline{pb}
, and we set
f(y)
to be the third line.
If
y
is non-reduced supported set theoretically on a point
a
of
S
,
then
P_{y}
intersects
X
along
2\overline{pa}+f(y)
for a line

in
P_{y}
.
We claim that
f(y)
passes through
p
, if and only if
y
belongs to
A
. Indeed, let

a=(a_{0}\!:\!a_{1}\!:\!a_{2}\!:\!a_{3}\!:\!a_{4}\!:\!0)
,
b=(b_{0}\!:\!b_{1}\!:\!b_{2}\!:\!b_{3}\!:\!b_{4}\!:\!0)
, and choose coordinates
(s\!:\!t\!:\!u)
for
P_{y}
via

\eta(s\!:\!t\!:\!u)=s(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4},0)+t(b_{0},b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},b_{4},0)+(0,0,0,0,0,u).

Then the cubic form
d
pulls back to

2q(a,b)ust+3s^{2}tc(a,a,b)+3st^{2}c(a,b,b)=st[2q(a,b)u+3c(a,a,b)s+3c(a,b,b)t],

where the symmetric bilinear form
q(\bullet,\bullet)
is the polarization of
q
and the symmetric trilinear form
c(\bullet,\bullet,\bullet)
is that of
c
.
The morphism
\eta
maps
the line
s=0
to
\overline{bp}
, the line
t=0
to
\overline{ap}
, and
the line cut out by
2q(a,b)u+3c(a,a,b)s+3c(a,b,b)t
to
f(y)
. Note that
q(a,b)=0
if and only if the line
\ell_{y}
is contained in
Q
. Now,
\eta(0\!:\!0\!:\!1)=p
, so
f(y)
passes through
p
if and only if
q(a,b)=0
.
A similar argument shows that
f(y)
passes through
p
if and only if
y
belongs to
A
also in the case where
y
is non-reduced. The commutativity of the diagram in the statement of the claim follows.

If a line
\ell
on
X
is contained in
\hat{Q}
, then it is contained in
\hat{C}
and so passes through
p
. Hence, if a line
\ell
on
X
does not pass through
p
, then it intersects
\hat{Q}
along a length
2
subscheme.
Thus, the projection
\bar{\ell}
of
\ell
from
p
intersects
Q
along a length
2
subscheme
\tilde{g}(\ell)\in S_{0}^{[2]}
.
We get a morphism
\tilde{g}:F_{0}(X)\rightarrow S_{0}^{[2]}
, which is clearly the inverse of the restriction of
f
to
S_{0}^{[2]}
.
We conclude that
f:S_{0}^{[2]}\rightarrow F_{0}(X)
is an isomorphism. This completes the proof of Claim
13.11
.
∎

The Fano variety of lines
F(C)
is identified with
F(X\cap H)
.
The point
p
does not belong to
X\cap H
, and so
F(X\cap H)
is contained in
F_{0}(X)
.
The subvariety
F(X\cap H)
of
F(X)
is lagrangian, by
[
Vo1
]
. Hence,
Z:=\tilde{g}(F(X\cap H))

is a lagrangian surface in
S^{[2]}
.
Through a generic point of
C
pass
6
lines lying on
C
, by
[
CG
]
, and the set of lines on
C
through every point of
C
is finite, by Assumption
13.1
.
The fiber of the restriction of
\pi_{2}
to
Z\times S^{[2]}\cap I
over a point
y\in S^{[2]}

consists of length
2
subschemes of
S
lying on a line passing through one of the two points of
y
and is hence finite. If
y
consists of two distinct points
a
,
b
through each of which pass exactly
6
lines on
C
, and such that the line
\overline{ab}
does not lie on
C
,
then
the fiber of

(13.18)

\pi_{2}:(Z\times S^{[2]})\cap I\rightarrow S^{[2]}

over

consists of precisely
12
points, as
none of the
12
lines on
C
through
a
or

lies on
Q
, since
S
does not contain a line.
The morphism (
13.18
) is projective and has finite fibers, hence is a finite morphism (see
[
Har
, Ex. III.11.2]
). The morphism (
13.18
) is clearly surjective.
This completes the proof of Lemma
13.10
.
∎

13.6.
Proof of Proposition
13.2

Let
h=c_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{S}(1))
be the class of a hyperplane section of
S
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{4}
.
Let
\tilde{h}
be the nef class of
{\rm Pic}(S^{[2]})
corresponding to
h
via (
3.12
).
Let
\delta\in{\rm Pic}(S^{[2]})
be half the class of the divisor of non-reduced subschemes.

Lemma 13.12
.

(1)

The class of the divisor
A
is
[A]=\tilde{h}-2\delta
.

(2)

The pullback via
f
of

{\mathcal{O}}_{F(X)}(1)
is
\lambda:=2\tilde{h}-3\delta
.

(3)

If
{\rm Pic}(S)={\mathbb{Z}}h
, then
the nef cone of
S^{[2]}
is
\langle\lambda,\tilde{h}\rangle.

(4)

The restrictions of
\lambda
and
\delta
to
F(C)
are equal.

Proof.

(
1
)
We may and do assume that
{\rm Pic}(S)
is cyclic generated by
h
, as the parts of the lemma not assuming it follow from this case by continuity.
The contraction
f:S^{[2]}\rightarrow F(X)
of the divisor
A
in Claim
13.11
admits the following modular interpretation. The divisor
A
consists precisely of those subschemes
y
, such that
I_{y}(1)
is not generated by global sections. Indeed,
y
belongs to
A
, if and only if
\bar{\ell}_{y}
intersects
S
in a length
3
subscheme. Every hyperplane section of
S
containing
y
contains
S\cap\bar{\ell}_{y}
, so

H^{0}(S,I_{y}(1))
generates the subsheaf
I_{\bar{\ell}_{y}\cap S}(1)
of
I_{y}(1)
.
Let
M(2,h,1)
be the moduli space of
h
-stable sheaves on
S
with Mukai vector
(2,h,1)
.
There is a natural isomophism
\eta:M(1,h,2)\rightarrow M(2,h,1)
, by

[
Ma1
, Theorem 3.21]
and the fact that
the moduli space
M(2,h,3)
is empty.
M(1,h,2)
parametrizes sheaves of the form
I_{y}(1)
, where
y
is a length
2
subscheme of
S
, and is thus naturally isomorphic to
S^{[2]}
.
The divisor
\eta(A)
is precisely the divisor of non-locally-free sheaves in

M(2,h,1)
, by construction of
\eta
. Explicitly, given

, let
ev:H^{0}(I_{y}(1))\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{O}}_{S}\rightarrow I_{y}(1)
be the evaluation homomorphism. Then
\ker(ev)
belongs to
M(2,-h,2)
, which is zero dimensional, so
\ker(ev)
is isomorphic to the unique rigid and stable vector bundle
E
with Mukai vector
(2,-h,2)
. The torsion free sheaf

\eta(I_{y}(1))
is a subsheaf of
E^{*}
and the quotient
E^{*}/\eta(I_{y}(1))
is supported on the point
\phi(y)
of
S
complementary to the subscheme
y
in
\bar{\ell}_{y}\cap S
. We get the commutative diagram

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{\mid_{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}}(E^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{S,}

where
\phi
is given in (
13.17
) and the right vertical arrow is the natural projection.
The contraction
f:S^{[2]}\rightarrow F(X)
is identified with the
Gieseker-Li morphism from
M(2,h,1)
to the normalization of the
Uhlenbeck-Yau compactification
[
Li
]
.
Set

and

.
The divisor
A
corresponds to the image of the Mukai vector
(2,h,2)

in
H^{2}(M(w),{\mathbb{Z}})
via the Mukai Hodge isometry
\theta_{w}:w^{\perp}\rightarrow H^{2}(M(w),{\mathbb{Z}})

between the latter and the sublattice
